2026 Annual Meetings Curtain Raiser: Outlook for the Global Economy and Policy Priorities

IMF(206.0K)·68:35·2026-10-07
Kristalina GeorgievaManaging Director, IMF Tharman ShanmugaratnamPresident, Republic of Singapore
TL;DRThe global economy faces a complex clash between a positive AI demand shock and negative supply shocks from high energy costs and record public debt, requiring urgent structural reforms and fiscal consolidation.
The IMF and Singapore's leadership emphasize that advanced economies must address unsustainable deficits before bond markets force painful, disorderly corrections. Divergent Global Forces: The world economy is being pulled in opposite directions by a negative supply shock in...

TL;DR

The global economy faces a complex clash between a positive AI demand shock and negative supply shocks from high energy costs and record public debt, requiring urgent structural reforms and fiscal consolidation. The IMF and Singapore's leadership emphasize that advanced economies must address unsustainable deficits before bond markets force painful, disorderly corrections.

Global Economic Context & Twin Shocks

  • Divergent Global Forces: The world economy is being pulled in opposite directions by a negative supply shock in energy and a positive demand shock from Artificial Intelligence.
  • Geopolitical & Regional Strains: Growth is severely impacted in war-torn regions (Ukraine in its 5th year, the Gulf in its 8th month) and vulnerable energy-importing countries lacking policy space.
  • Asia-Pacific Expansion: Since Thailand last hosted the IMF Annual Meetings in 1991, the Asia-Pacific region's share of global GDP has grown from 25% to 43%.

The AI Boom & Energy Pressures

  • AI Investment Wave: Global investment-to-GDP in AI will reach or exceed historical amounts for railroads, electricity grids, and telecommunication networks.
  • AI Trade & Infrastructure: AI hardware and related tech products now account for more than 1/10th of world goods trade. Net importers building infrastructure include the US, China, and India, while five of the top ten global economies are in Asia supplying advanced microprocessors, memory chips, chip-making machines, and robotics.
  • Productivity Gains: Kristalina Georgieva noted IMF research suggests AI could deliver up to half a percentage point of extra world growth annually over time (e.g., lifting global growth from 3% to 3.5%, adding an economy the size of ASEAN over a decade).
  • Persistent Energy Costs: Gulf oil flows remain around $100 a barrel, compounded by a crack spread of about $100 a barrel due to global refining shortfalls, leaving retail diesel prices at record highs and natural gas supplies impaired.
  • Sovereign Yields: Ten-year sovereign yields in the US, Germany, and Japan have reached their highest levels since 2007, 2008, and 1996, respectively, and are still climbing.

Fiscal Sustainability & Record Public Debt

  • Exploding Debt Levels: Global public debt is near post-WWII highs and is on track to soon exceed 100% of GDP, driven largely by advanced economies.
  • The R minus G Shift: Kristalina Georgieva and Tharman Shanmugaratnam highlighted that the interest-to-growth differential ($R - G$) has fundamentally changed; interest rates are no longer stuck below GDP growth rates, eliminating the "easy ride" policymakers enjoyed for 17 years.
  • Structural Tax Revenue Challenges: President Tharman noted that future growth from AI may alter the distribution of income between capital and labor. Since systemically significant economies derive most tax revenue from labor rather than capital, traditional debt-to-GDP arithmetic may fail.
  • Efficiency Savings Potential: Citing IMF fiscal department research, Tharman pointed out that if systemically important advanced economies matched the spending efficiency of the best performer in their class, they could save 30% to 40% of their budgets.

Monetary Policy, Financial Stability & Fiscal Dominance

  • Monetary Independence: Central banks must resist pressure to ease burdens for fiscal authorities ("monetary cowboys") and maintain an uncompromised focus on price stability.
  • Subtle Forms of Fiscal Dominance:
    • Limited Government Room for Maneuver: Because high debts constrain governments during crises, the burden of easing falls repeatedly on central banks, leading to lower average interest rates and systemic leverage.
    • Disorderly Market Conditions: Frequent bond market disruptions driven by leveraged players (such as hedge funds) force central banks to step in as financial stability guarantors, fostering policy complacency.
  • Bond Market Discipline: Tharman credited bond markets as a "blessing in disguise" that forces polarized electorates and leaders to face reality before a meltdown occurs.

Policy Priorities & International Cooperation

  • IMF 2026 Bangkok Meetings Focus: Ministers and central bank governors from the IMF's 191 countries will convene in Thailand to address energy, AI, and fiscal deficits.
  • Required Macro Balances: Georgieva outlined that the US must save more, China must spend more, and Europe must invest more to rebalance excessive global imbalances.
  • China's Domestic Pivot: China is pursuing an infrastructural investment push to boost domestic demand, though raising household consumption is a multi-year task involving pension and household income reforms.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Global Debt Trajectory: Kristalina Georgieva stated that global public debt is on track to "soon exceed 100% of GDP."
  • Oil Price Persistence: Kristalina Georgieva noted that Brent futures predict high oil prices will persist through 2027.
  • AI Productivity Impact: Kristalina Georgieva cited IMF research predicting that AI adoption could deliver "up to half a percentage point of extra world growth annually" over time.

Notable Quotes

"Love it, hate it, fear it, AI is here, rapidly becoming a key driver of countries' relative fortunes in the world economy." — Kristalina Georgieva
"The bond markets are literally the only discipline we have now. They're forcing the issue... not only do you not have a free lunch, but lunch gets more and more and more expensive." — Tharman Shanmugaratnam
"If we were to rely on growth to take us out of the fiscal hole, we may be deeply disappointed. Without fiscal efforts, we cannot get back to fiscal sustainability." — Kristalina Georgieva
"Through the wind and rain, we are the same boat together." — Tharman Shanmugaratnam (citing a traditional Chinese saying)
audience for it. Our two institutions share a common purpose. As Asia's leading school of public policy, the Lee Kuan Yew School is dedicated to educating and inspiring the next generation of leaders and to advancing good governance across the region and beyond. We bring rigorous evidence-based research and education to bear on the complex policy challenges of our time, and work to connect ideas with the decisions that shape people's lives. Complementing this, the East Asia Institute brings deep academic and policy-oriented expertise on the region's development and its economic relationships with the wider world. Together, we are glad to foster dialogue and offer a platform where the questions that matter most can be examined through both a regional and global lens and with rigor and openness. For the Lee Kuan Yew School, this is precisely the kind of conversation we seek to convene, one that brings perspectives from Asia into global debates on governance and public policy. And there is much to examine. We meet today at a critical juncture for the global economy. As we look ahead to the 2026 annual meetings of the IMF and the World Bank Group in Bangkok next week, the international community is navigating profound structural shifts, economic fragmentation, and a pressing need for sustainable and inclusive growth. How we respond to these challenges, as nations and as institutions, will shape our global stability and shared prosperity for generations to come. That is what makes today's curtain raiser so timely and important. It offers an early view of the outlook and priorities that will frame next week's discussions in Bangkok. We are deeply honored to welcome Dr. Georgieva to deliver this address. Dr. Georgieva has helmed the IMF since October 2019 and began her second term in October 2024. Under her leadership, the IMF has helped countries around the world navigate a series of major global shocks. Before joining the IMF, she served as chief executive of the World Bank and held senior leadership positions at the European Commission, a career that has made her a champion of cooperation across borders, of economic resilience, and of growth that works for everyone. Following her address, we will have the pleasure of hearing from President Tharman who will join the managing director in a fireside conversation. President Tharman needs little introduction. Over several decades, he has served as Deputy Prime Minister, Minister for Finance, Chairman of the Monetary Authority of Singapore, and the first Asian chair of the International Monetary and Financial Committee, placing him at the center of economic policymaking and global financial governance. Their conversation promises to be a compelling and forward-looking exchange on the forces reshaping our economy and what they mean for Asia and the wider international community. On behalf of the Lee Kuan Yew School of Public Policy and the East Asia Institute, I thank you all once again for joining us today. And now, it is my pleasure to invite Dr. Georgieva to deliver the 2026 annual meetings curtain raiser address. Managing Director, please. Thank you. Thank you very much. That was very kind of you. And a very good afternoon to everybody in the audience. Dear President Tharman, ladies and gentlemen, dear friends, it is a delight to be back in Singapore on my way to Thailand for this year's annual meetings. Singapore is a showcase of integration in regional and global value chains and of the benefits of this integration. Next week, finance ministers and central bank governors of the IMF's 191 countries will come together to assess the direction of the world economy at a time of high uncertainty and rapid change. It is vital that they do it. And Thailand is just the right host to have, another open and dynamic economy at the heart of a vibrant region. Since Thailand last hosted our annual meetings 35 years ago in 1991, the Asia-Pacific's share of global GDP has grown from 25 to 43% today. Asia and the world have steered through shock after shock with remarkable agility. But whether this fortitude will be sustained and whether the world's underwhelming growth rates this decade can be bettered, will depend on our success in navigating three major cross-currents: the rapid arrival of AI, persistently high energy prices, and record levels of public debt. This will be the central focus of our discussion in Thailand next week. Let me start from the context in which our meetings will take place. To put it simply, the global economy is being pulled in two opposite directions: a negative supply shock on the energy side and a positive demand shock from AI. The combined impact of these two forces is highly uneven across the world. We will have our World Economic Outlook; it will show next week that steady global growth has continued since the spring, but behind the averages, there are significant variations. The largest hit to growth this year are in the economies ravaged by war. A war in Ukraine in its fifth year, a war in the Gulf in its eighth month and too many other raging conflicts destroying lives and causing global economic damage. Small and more scattered hits to growth are also seen in other vulnerable countries, those reliant on energy imports and lacking sufficient policy space to cushion the shocks. But we also would see higher growth in a few places, which takes me to the role of AI. Love it, hate it, fear it, AI is here, rapidly becoming a key driver of countries' relative fortunes in the world economy. Global investment to GDP in AI will reach and likely exceed the amounts that went into building the railroads, the electricity grid, and the telecommunication network. We estimate AI hardware and related tech products now to account for more than 1/10th of world goods trade, and rising. Growth in AI-related trade reflects the investment boom in economies embedded in the value chain. Those include the US, China, and India as net importers of AI hardware building the infrastructure to be key AI providers, and then five of the seven other economies in the top 10 are in Asia, supplying everything from top-end microprocessors to memory chips, chip-making machines, and robotics. Yet, as this drives forward today's economies, it largely bypasses most others, increasing the risk of widening economic inequality across the globe. AI also adds to energy demand and this takes me to the second top of mind topic at our high, the rising cost of energy, and with it fertilizers, food, and key industrial inputs. With food security put at even greater risk by this year's Super El Niño. In the Philippines, they call it the Godzilla El Niño. This pushes inflation up and it drags growth down. So far, the energy price shock has been large but contained, thanks to energy efficiency, fuel source diversification, reserve releases, supply chain agility, and the usual demand and supply response. Energy gets more expensive, we consume less of it. Nonetheless, despite a shaky recovery, flows out of the Gulf remain around $100 a barrel, reflecting risks, high transport costs, and other factors. Add to that a crack spread of about another $100 per barrel due to a global shortfall in refining capacity, and we get retail prices of diesel and other refined products at record high. And in addition, natural gas supply from the Gulf remains severely impaired, reflecting more limited LNG transport options as long as the Strait of Hormuz remains threatened. This has uneven impacts across the world. Most at risk: Europe and Asia. And to quote the Game of Thrones, winter is coming. Price pressures may build further as countries replenish reserves and demand rises with the approach of the northern atmosphere cold season. Even if the war in the Gulf were to end soon, energy prices will likely persist high for some time. Brent futures, for example, now predict high oil prices through 2027. Higher energy prices are: what are they doing? They're pushing up inflation, they're pushing up policy rates, they're pushing up benchmark yield curves. After going up very significantly this year, look at this, US, German, and Japanese 10-year sovereign yields, they are now at their highest level since 2007, 2008 and 1996 and still climbing. This takes us to the third major factor affecting the global economy today: excessive fiscal deficits, record levels of public debt, and high debt-servicing costs. Global public debt is near its highest levels since after the Second World War and is on track to soon exceed 100% of GDP. In other words, to cover our debt, we have to put everything we have produced in one year in that pile. Who is the worst offender? Advanced economies with some of the highest gross debt loads we can see. Debt to GDP ratios are generally lower for emerging markets and lower still for low-income countries, although we need to recognize that in comparison to advanced economies, these two sets of countries also have relatively lower revenues to service their debt. For many emerging market economies, the effects of rising core yields on funding costs are still being partially offset for now by tight risk spreads. These are the results of hard work over the decades to strengthen policy frameworks and institutions, but also still easy financial conditions. For high-debt countries in the euro area, the situation is more challenging as bond spreads to German bonds, they keep rising in France and Italy, but not only, also in Portugal, in Ireland, other countries that have worked hard during and after the Eurozone crisis to strengthen their debt management and reduce the levels of debt. Now, AI enters this mix in two ways. First, rising long-term private bond issuance competes for savings. Look at this, the jump in this column. Second, some portion of the increase in core yields may reflect anticipation of faster growth in the future. Yet, expectations of higher growth solving fiscal problems can cause policy complacency. Policymakers had a relatively easy ride over the last 17 years, as for all that time, interest rates were stuck below GDP growth rates. Now is no more the case. Economies are fond of expressing this as the interest-to-growth differential. How many are economists in this room? Let me see. Okay. So, we economists will love this. It is the difference between interest rate and growth. Now, what does it mean? It means that when interest rates are way below growth, you can service your debt with more ease. Used to be the case, no more. In the past many years, we have had it easier. Now, we see the interest rates climbing up, growth is somewhat disappointing, we are in a tougher position. So, if we were to rely on growth to take us out of the fiscal hole, we may be deeply disappointed. Without fiscal efforts, we cannot get back to fiscal sustainability. Higher core bond yields, of course, also reflect fiscal policy. With the succession of recent shocks having ballooned the public debt and with most primary deficits having remained larger than their prepandemic averages 2015 to 2019, fiscal space is crying out for replenishment. The situation demands an urgent and comprehensive set of policy responses. And let me speak to monetary policy first. In many countries, inflation exceeding target has been a headache for quite some time, in the US case, for the last five and a half years. We draw comfort from long-term inflation expectations staying anchored, but let us not forget the simple fact that inflation is only anchored until it is not. I learned the hard way from Bulgaria's hyperinflation this lesson. The AI building boom is inflationary, the energy and food shocks are inflationary, tariffs, defense spending, and high public debt can be inflationary, and memories of the recent high inflation are still fresh. Now may be a good time for prudent bias in many countries' monetary policy. And it is highly appropriate that the US Federal Reserve, the ECB, and the Bank of Japan have all raised rates and spoken firmly, Bank of Japan just yesterday, with future policy rates being repriced accordingly. Staying in the central banking lane, a few words on financial stability policy. Given rising economic and financial concentration in AI, which we would be wise to keep in mind, let's remember Amara's Law, which says we tend to overestimate a new technology in the short run and underestimate it in the long run. And it is somewhere in the transition between today's AI building boom and tomorrow's arrival of AI's benefits that we will traverse into this period of maximum risks. In other words, we have today's strong corporate earnings that driving up share prices right there and they are driving up wealthy effects. We all feel richer. Should earnings fall short, hyperscaler leverage and large and growing global holdings of US equities could turn a disappointment into a far-reaching shock. The first line of defense, of course, regulation and supervision. Now let me go to the third topic that will be top of mind for us in Bangkok: fiscal policy, a place where all pressures meet. Elevated yields are inflating the interest bill at a time of tight budget constraints and competing spending priorities, including from defense. Higher policy rates then lift the short end, feeding directly into the cost of short-term debt. And yet we don't see decisive action in the high-debt advanced economies where the need of the hour is for credible, medium-term fiscal consolidation plans, supported in some cases by upfront fiscal measures, including to take some of the pressure off monetary policy. Emerging markets can find themselves confronting not only higher funding costs from rising benchmark yields and wider spreads, but also more volatile capital flows related in part to financial innovation. These economies too should strive to expand their fiscal space while also growing their foreign exchange buffers. And what about low-income countries? They are already hurting from financing constraints, high interest-to-revenue ratios, small tax bases, and shrinking aid flows. They may be forced to cut development spending, much needed for their economies and their people, a regrettable spillover that shows the collective nature of the fiscal challenge. Putting all this together, what would be my message to the world economy policymakers next week? It will be this: we cannot keep delaying necessary policy action. You have the tools. Now have the wisdom to use them. At the IMF, we know fiscal consolidation will be a heavy lift. After a succession of shocks where each time, fiscal policy had to step in to cushion the impact, people have grown accustomed to state support. And yet, some very tough political choices stare us in the face. How could policymakers succeed? They need to do two things. First, explain to people why consolidation is needed, why it is in their interest. Take some pain today, for growth tomorrow. And second, make plans to limit the cost to future growth to the extent possible, and to protect the most vulnerable in society. They also have to do a third thing. They have to pursue complementary structural reforms. So let me go to this very simple point. Growth alone cannot solve the fiscal problem we have accumulated. But growth certainly helps. Ambitious reform programs would support the fiscal effort by delivering better workforce skills, more flexible labor markets, greater ease of corporate entry and exit, better access to patient risk capital, and energy security. In an unforgiving global environment that leaves no room for self-inflicted injuries, I will urge authorities everywhere to slash red tape, clean out the cobwebs of obsolete rules accumulated over time, and free up the power of entrepreneurship. And happily, these are the same attributes and policies needed to get the best out of AI, where the core benefits derive not from the short-run boost to demand from laying the tracks, but from the long-run boost to productivity from adoption. Our research suggests that AI, if done right, could over time deliver up to half a percentage point of extra world growth annually. Just think of it, going from 3% to 3.5% would add an economy the size of ASEAN to the world economy over a decade. We will learn as we go, but we know AI preparedness is key. Digital infrastructure, labor market readiness, and regulatory guardrails. So why do we need that? Because we need to capture the benefits and manage AI's substantial perils, and they are not trivial: large-scale labor market fallout, serious cyber and stability risks, and frontier models threatening to escape human control and run amok. Mr. President, Singapore can take pride in knowing that it ranks at the very top of the IMF's AI preparedness index. Bravo, and keep it up. Success requires everybody to be taken along to harvest the transformational power of AI, which is why it will be so important to deliver AI access around the globe, and that of course calls for cooperation. In our interconnected world, countries cooperate not out of charity, but out of self-interest. That is how trade finds its way around the obstacles thrown at it. That is why our members are keen to come together next week in Bangkok. President Tharman, you have made substantial contribution to international cooperation and as we heard, among them was leading the IMF's highest guiding body through a period of economic turmoil in Europe. Cooperation kept the crisis from festering and spilling over the rest of the world. Today, you host us here for this kickoff of our 2026 Annual Meetings. As the president of a country that has successfully transformed its economy for dramatically improving the lives of its people, how did Singapore achieve this? Prudent policies, sound institutions, and collaboration with its neighbors and the rest of the world. These are the very same qualities the global economy needs in its quest for prosperity during this time of profound change. And it is so fitting that I call on the world to embrace these qualities right here from this stage at the Lee Kuan Yew School of Public Policy in Singapore, for which I thank you. Thank you so much for such a vivid and powerful speech. My name is Danny Quah. I am Li Ka Shing Professor in Economics here at the Lee Kuan Yew School. In a minute, as the furniture gets set up, it will be my honor to be in a fireside chat to moderate that with you and the President of the Republic of Singapore. But I want to say before we begin, it's such a pleasure and an honor to be in this fireside chat with the Managing Director of the IMF, Kristalina Georgieva. And as I said, it is for us a week to have the whole world to be like Singapore. We would be in a very happy place. Thank you very much. It is now my honor to invite the President of the Republic, President Tharman Shanmugaratnam to the stage to participate in this fireside chat. Thank you, Kristalina. Thank you for your clarity and candor. You know, Kristalina's, if you don't mind Kristalina, Kristalina's curtain raiser speech as it's called traditionally, exemplifies what the IMF is about. People think of the IMF often as some sort of global rescue force. Yes, it is a very important part of the global rescue system when countries fall into trouble. It plays a very important role, but that's not the fundamental role of the IMF. The fundamental role of the IMF is what's called surveillance, telling the world, including the most powerful countries, where the storm clouds are, how they're moving, and what we need to do to avoid the hurricane. And I think you've done that exceptionally well in your curtain raiser speech. What's changed over time is who the prime actors you're referring to are. If you think of the main themes of Kristalina's speech, and particularly the challenge of resetting fiscal policy and ensuring that debts are brought down to more sustainable levels. In the 1980s, 1990s, part of the 2000s, the first decade of the new century, it was principally emerging markets. Some large, some small, but we had some very difficult problems in emerging markets. Then we entered the 2010s, when it was a group of European economies that caused the largest problems and they troubled Europe and they troubled the world and the IMF had to expend very significant resources to support their structural adjustments and their very painful transition back to being able to tap on the markets. And in fact, the global community got together to raise additional resources for the IMF for that purpose. But they were relatively small European countries. Now we're talking about something different. We're talking about the largest or the systemically important economies of the world facing the largest fiscal challenges. The IMF will not be able to rescue them, but all the more that your objective fairly hard-hitting advice has to be taken seriously. And the markets, too, need to listen carefully to what the IMF says, because you can't go there with a fire hose. You can only warn, you can advise, and we all have to be prepared. I would say the most important exhibit, sorry, I'm going on a little bit longer than I intended, but I really want to emphasize the exhibit which Kristalina showed on what's called R minus G, the interest rate compared to the rate of economic growth. And her main point was one can no longer be complacent because interest rates are no longer significantly below economic growth and in many countries they're now higher. And that's now a structural situation, it's not cyclical, it's here to stay. I would go a little further and say to those who are hoping that economic growth is going to go through a new miracle phase and therefore debt to GDP ratios come down, that there's a further twist to the problem which is that future growth may not mean future government revenue growth. And ultimately, it's what happens in your budget that matters. You've got a growing interest cost on your budget and you need revenues and it may very well be that if we succeed in the AI revolution, as I hope we do, it may very well mean a different distribution of income between capital and labor. And the same economies we're talking about, the same systemically significant economies, are ones which actually get most of their tax revenues from labor, not from capital. So that's a further structural issue and challenge and it means we have to sit back and reset fiscal policy in a far more fundamental way and not get away with the complacency of simple arithmetic ratios, debt divided by GDP, and you hope GDP growth is rapid enough and therefore the debt to GDP ratio comes down. It is not so simple. Thank you very much, Mr. President. You've raised so many issues that we need to dig into in the speech and the kind words that you have placed about Singapore. But I thought a good way to begin our fireside chat is to ask President Tharman for his reflections and his reactions to the speech. President. Thank you, Managing Director. I want to, if you don't mind, pick up and in particular the fiscal situation, the severity, and the non-complacency we need to build in. But I wanted to pick up first on an intersection point where you talked about the historical trajectory of different parts of the world. Managing Director, your speech was one that talked about, that celebrated the success of Asia, but it also did not shy away from the challenges that we face in the global economy today. One of the things you showed us was the difference between what's happening in advanced countries and what's happening in developing countries. I wanted to ask you to say a bit more on that. What are the relative priorities that these different parts of the world might need to keep in mind? When we look at the fiscal picture today, it reminds me of a stairwell. We get a shock, governments rush to help people and businesses, they borrow. Then comes a better time, they do nothing. Then comes the second shock, they rush, they borrow, they help people and businesses, they step up. We have had three of these shocks just in this decade. And when you delay action when you have some space that allows you to reduce your level of debt but you don't use it, the accumulation of debt becomes much harder to cope with. So what is my greatest worry? We are in a place where people received help when they needed it, they got accustomed to having this help. What they already got, they don't want to let go. And yet new shocks are coming and demands are going up. This is the public, the voting public. And then we have policymakers. They recognize they need to act, but they find it very difficult in this context to take action. And this is why in my speech I emphasized the importance of explaining to people why restoring sound budgets, reducing the level of deficit, the level of debt is paramount. And having the courage to drive this action because this stairwell that we have been climbing on, it is a stairwell not to heaven. And I look at the steps taken. Some countries have been able to act and they need to be an inspiration for others. Thank you, Managing Director. We come back to this fiscal situation and we're all agreed that building and rebuilding fiscal space now takes on extreme urgency. At a time when debt levels are high, borrowing costs elevated, so even more difficult to make up this shortfall. Mr. President, I wanted to pick up on that point in connection with comments you've made about a new fiscal and social compact. I wanted if you could share with us some of your latest thinking on the economic durability, the social consequences of this collision of forces. Well, first just to follow on what Kristalina said, I can't help thinking that we have to thank the bond markets. The bond markets are a blessing in disguise. We have learned that partly because of polarized and fragmented electorates, that leaders in the affected countries are not going to volunteer the truth until they have no choice. They're not going to force painful choices on the electorate until they have no choice. And the bond markets are literally the only discipline we have now. They're forcing the issue and they're telling everyone that well if you don't act when you're on this step, it's going to go to the next step. In other words, not only do you not have a free lunch, but lunch gets more and more and more expensive. So the earlier we act, the better. So thank the bond markets. It's not yet a meltdown, it's not a revolt. You don't have what we had in 2002 in the UK, not yet, but it could happen. It's a warning, it's a very clear warning, and governments need to now sit back and realize these aren't market disorders. These are actually rational signals from a multitude of players in global markets telling us you've reached the limit and you now have to make hard choices. And that can be an advantage politically. It can be an advantage. It is for a reason that the saying is fear God and the bond markets. So I think and you know to look at it positively, what has worked. One of the countries that I'm most impressed by from my experience in the past as a finance minister and when I was chairing the IMC, was Portugal. Portugal realized when it had spreads of about 1,200 basis points, in other words, 12 percentage points higher than German sovereign bonds that it had no choice. And we spent a lot of time discussing at the IMF, Portugal, Greece, Ireland, several other countries, but Portugal, with all the imperfections of the IMF program at the time, got good advice and most important, it took ownership. It took national ownership of the problem. There was no more bluffing. There's one other country which I won't name which sort of endured bluffing for quite a while. Portugal in Portugal there was no bluffing, they took ownership and they made major adjustments and you can imagine how painful it was taking a primary fiscal deficit down by six percentage points of GDP within a couple of years. It's amazing. But they went about it in a way that was socially progressive. They didn't touch pay and pensions at the lowest level. The middle class and the upper middle class had rightly to take the brunt of the adjustments. Very importantly, and this applies to many countries even now, it wasn't just about cutting expenditures and raising taxes. It is about going for efficiencies and one of the very useful things about the work of the IMF fiscal department, which doesn't get a lot of the headlines but I always read because it's really good stuff, they found just recently in fact I think it was about a year ago that if amongst this group of systemically important countries the advanced countries if each of them was to spend as efficiently as the best performer in that class, you look for the best performer and say if everyone could just spend as efficiently as the best performer, try to get the same bang for the buck for your dollar, right? You could save 30 to 40% of your budget. 30 to 40% of your budget. That's like an enormous slash in spending. So efficiency, design, fairness, these are very important principles, it's not just a brutal macroeconomic adjustment. It's about redesign of fiscal policy. Thank you, Managing Director. I very much agree that governments can learn from each other much more than they have been doing and we see this as being our job, primary job. Show what others are doing and how it works out. And I can say with pride that I look at the countries today in Europe which are the best-performing countries, those that had IMF programs a decade ago. Why are they so good? Because they owned the reforms they undertook. And if we can press during our annual meetings for this examples of good practice of best practice to spread around, we can have a softening of the pain that inevitably when your debt is high, your deficit is high and interest rates are jumping up, you have to endure. Work together, less pain. Thank you. I want to come to this idea about discipline, but I can't resist jumping in on the bond market. I want to see as an academic economist how rare it is to hear political and institutional leadership celebrate the discipline that the bond markets give, because most political leaders don't want to have that discipline confront them. One of Bill Clinton's advisors in a conversation with others, you know, in a conversation about which Marvel comic book superhero would they want to come back as? And the one who won that argument was the one who said I want to come back as the bond market. This is the one that wins them all. On the question of discipline, for the both of you, we are now living in a time of fiscal pressure. There's persistent stress in the system. In the history of these kinds of things, there's often been an unfortunate blurring of the line between fiscal policy and monetary policy. And it is important that we think about safeguards that ensure monetary policy credibility. I want to invite the two of you to share your ideas about how we might do that and what the dangers are if we don't. The most important task for monetary policy today is to focus on price stability and communicate clearly that they would act, monetary policy central banks would act, to protect price stability. They can only do it when central banks are independent. And when central banks resist pressure that may come from the fiscal side to ease the burden by basically stepping up, buying debt, making it so that the situation for the fiscal side eases. I would call this monetary cowboys, running to the rescue of the fiscal agents. And my message is, please don't. Focus on your job. It is price stability, concentrate on doing it and we are very loudly supporting the independence of central banks, acting responsibly, based on evidence to protect the economy, protect the public. Thank you, Mr. President. As Kristalina is speaking, I was just reflecting on the phases we've gone through. The key problem, and it's a political economy problem or institutional problem, is that there's always a temptation on the part of governments to want to impose their will on central banks on monetary policy and sometimes also supervision of individual banks, favored banks. It used to be viewed as an emerging country problem. And it was a fair description. Governments would change central bank governors when they didn't like what they were doing and even if they didn't, central bank governor was typically someone more junior than the finance minister. It was the norm. And across the developing world, especially, independence, even if it was there in law, was not observed in practice. Then we got to a situation after the global financial crisis where this blurring between fiscal and monetary policy became largely an advanced country problem because of QE, massive quantitative easing, that effectively regardless of what analytical framework you use, it's just common sense, the central bank is purchasing a huge proportion of the debt issued by government. It's financing the government and it takes the central bank into the fiscal policy space. Now we're at a phase hopefully where we are gradually withdrawing that large excess of QE and the large stock of government bonds on the balance sheet of the central bank, but I think going forward, we face two more subtle forms of fiscal dominance. First comes from what we were discussing earlier. Because governments are now starting from a position of large fiscal deficits and very high debts as a starting point, their ability to deal with future crises is now much constrained. Their room for maneuver is much constrained. If you get another Covid-like situation or a recession or some turbulence in the AI cycle and capex gets withdrawn very quickly, governments cannot step in the way they did in Covid because no fiscal space, right? So the burden will fall on central banks and it will fall increasingly with each crisis on central banks to have to ease monetary policy, maybe another round of QE, and that will lead over time on average to lower interest rates than should be the case, more leverage in the system and ultimately, less credibility. So that's not because governments were forcing the hand of the central bank. There was no explicit fiscal dominance, but it's by virtue of the fiscal authorities having run out of space that the burden now will fall on central banks. The second type of problem will come about, again, this is a more subtle form of fiscal dominance, we are just going to face disorderly market conditions more often. So an extreme version was the UK in 2022, famously called the Liz Truss moment, but there'll be, even in the largest bond market in the world, there'll be moments of disorder because when you're operating at very high levels of debt and when the investors purchasing your debt are now no longer just the traditional stable patient sovereign investors, but you've got a very large proportion of your investors who are hedge funds and other leverage players with much more complex trading strategies. From time to time, there's going to be disorder and again, who steps in to resolve the disorder is the central banks because they do have that responsibility of financial stability. So you'll get a situation where time and again the market will perceive the central bank as being willing and reliable in stepping in to resolve disorder and that leads again to complacency. It makes it easier to have leverage, why not? So I think these are the two more subtle forms of fiscal dominance we have to guard against in future, even if you don't have governments explicitly forcing the hand of central banks. The only glimpse of good news on this horizon is what has happened in emerging markets. Emerging markets today have more discipline, the on the monetary policy front, they are as good or better than advanced economies. On the fiscal front, it's more complicated, but also a lot of progress has been made. How did they get from there to here? Well, pain leading to course correction and then benefiting from strong fundamentals and recognizing strong fundamentals are worthy to protect. The risk you're describing is that advanced economies may be slipping into a territory where the only way to get to the same place is through lessons that are hard to learn. So this is why we appeal to our members act early on your own, not when your hand is forced. So next week in Bangkok, loud and clear, this is the message we will be communicating. Thank you both for such clear statements, price stability, the dangers of fiscal dominance, the message they are going to be giving in Bangkok. We've come to almost the end of the session, but I am very keen to take at least one question from the audience and I see a hand up already. So if we could get you to come and if you can make your question succinct, we'll give maximum time to managing director and president. Thank you so much for the discussion. I'm Natalie, I'm a student at the Lee Kuan Yew School of Public Policy and I'm from Singapore. So my question is President Tharman, Managing Director, in today's fragmented world, what does effective international cooperation look like and where can the greatest difference be made in your opinion? Thank you. Thank you. We are so interdependent. There is no way we can break the world into pieces and each one of them could go on its own. That's simply not possible. We have seen when the trade tensions began, majority of the countries in the world resisted the temptation of protectionism. They said, "Well, thank you, but no thank you." And what is the result, increase in regional, cross-regional, multilateral cooperation, massive increase in trade agreements. Not optimal, optimal is we all agree, but effective. My view is that we at the IMF have a huge responsibility to demonstrate why cooperating on issues especially of global nature is paramount and it is in everybody's interest and create space for this conversations where countries can find the path to agreement even in this more complex, more fragmented world. In my hearts of heart, I believe that we as community, we will work together. It would be sometimes not as optimal as we wish it to be, but we are all in this one boat that happens to be in very rough waters. It is suicidal if we were to, if I were to say to President Tharman, "Well, my end of the boat is fine, it's yours that is sinking." And that, I think creating the understanding of our interdependence, showing that there is value of solving problems together and accepting that our world is less perfect, but that's the only world we have. Short of Elon Musk sending us to Mars, we are right here on this planet, we have to figure it out, we have to work together. Thank you. Mr. President. Oh, actually I should have let Kristalina have the last word because you put it so well. And by the way, since you're in Asia, that analogy you used of the boat actually has a saying that's a Chinese saying, through the wind and rain, we are the same boat together and it is an old saying, but it has a lot of resonance not just between nations, but within communities themselves. I would just add one point because this is about the IMF, this is about macroeconomic surveillance and you know we talk a lot about international order when it comes to peace, when it comes to maritime law, when it comes to trade arrangements, but don't forget that macroeconomic policy is fundamental in global order. And in particular I would say what the US and China each do in their domestic macroeconomic policies has significant international repercussions. If the US doesn't address forthrightly in time, and it'll take a few years, its growing fiscal deficit, its uncontrolled fiscal deficit, there'll be implications not just for the US, because it'll crowd out other expenditures and so on, there'll be implications for the rest of the world because global bond yields are going up. Can I just say this is the program of work we have embraced at the IMF. What we see is for a first time in some time, excessive imbalances are growing. ClawLabs: Building Open Source Together | DevDay 2026

OpenAI(2.1M)·34:50·819 views·2026-10-07
ClawLabsOpen source development team
TL;DROpenAI introduced OpenClaw (OC) and OpenClaw Enterprise (OCE)—an open-source platform and enterprise control plane for multi-tenant, multiplayer AI agents that collaborate in real time with developer teams.
The project scales agent operations through secure sandboxing, automated background loops, and shared workspaces while expanding the open-source Codex ecosystem. From CLI to Distributed Agent Workloads: Peter described the progression from terminal/CLI sessions last year to...

TL;DR

OpenAI introduced OpenClaw (OC) and OpenClaw Enterprise (OCE)—an open-source platform and enterprise control plane for multi-tenant, multiplayer AI agents that collaborate in real time with developer teams. The project scales agent operations through secure sandboxing, automated background loops, and shared workspaces while expanding the open-source Codex ecosystem.

Evolution of Developer Workflows and Multiplayer Agents

  • From CLI to Distributed Agent Workloads:
    • Peter described the progression from terminal/CLI sessions last year to Codex Desktop, which enabled running large numbers of concurrent sessions with inline code review and visualization.
    • Running multiple parallel agent experiments required scaling up to 6–10 DevBoxes and cloud test runners ("Grabboxes") to handle disk space and compute load.
  • Overcoming the "Meat Proxy" Bottleneck:
    • Initially, one developer acted as the sole human interface ("meat proxy") passing messages back and forth to AI agents.
    • Moving development of OpenClaw directly into OC enabled multiplayer agent sessions, allowing entire teams to see, audit, and prompt each other's agent sessions in real time instead of relying solely on traditional pull requests.
  • The OpenClaw Foundation:
    • OpenClaw is an open-source platform for persistent agents owned by the OpenClaw Foundation, an independent nonprofit supported by OpenAI.

Collaborative Team Workspaces and "Jellyware"

  • Shared Team Servers:
    • Servers function as shared places rather than static apps, allowing teammates with identical access to observe, join, and prompt active agent sessions to prevent conflicting changes.
  • Jellyware Dashboards:
    • Teams build and iterate on live dashboards, CI metric trackers, community activity monitors, slop meters, and Discord meeting summaries using simple natural language prompts.
  • Continuous Loops vs. Defined Goals:
    • Continuous Loops: Automated monitoring loops scan Discord, Twitter, and GitHub, spin up test boxes to reproduce reported errors, apply fixes, and have secondary agents review the work before merging. A performance update loop runs daily snapshots against server releases to detect regressions.
    • Goals: Long-running targets, such as migrating a codebase from synchronous SQLite to async database access, which has been running for 18 days and landed over 100 PRs.

OpenClaw Enterprise (OCE) and Security Architecture

  • Kevin Lin's Enterprise Overview: Kevin Lin, who leads OpenClaw Enterprise at OpenAI, announced the open-sourcing of OCE as a vendor-neutral open-source control plane donated to the OpenClaw Foundation (co-developed with partners like Red Hat and NVIDIA) to provide multi-tenancy, governance, and security boundaries.
  • Four Security Fundamentals:
    • Hard Workload Boundaries: Deterministic code runs in a separate container and separate cluster from where the agent executes, preventing agents from altering their runtime.
    • Granular Sandboxing: Controls network access, file write permissions, and repository push targets to limit agent capabilities.
    • Model-Based Auto-Reviews: Uses auto-review (a feature in Codex and OpenClaw) powered by a secondary model to evaluate agent actions and block potentially dangerous executions.
    • Dedicated Service Accounts: Agents operate under their own independent service accounts and configurable connectors rather than impersonating human users, allowing independent auditing and deactivation.
  • Internal Scale:
    • Internal deployments at OpenAI have scaled to over 400 active Claws operating around the clock for code fixes, experiments, data analysis, and security escalation.
  • Presets and Integrations:
    • Enterprises use configurable presets (agent bundles) to launch agents like "Fred" or "Cooper" with pre-configured tool access, such as read-only codebases, Linear roadmap tracking (with team approval gates), and Slack integrations.

Ecosystem Support and Security Initiatives

  • Codex Open Source Program: OpenAI expanded the open-source program for Codex to ensure broader access to AI tools.
  • Patch the Planet: Partnering with security groups such as Trail of Bits to improve open-source security infrastructure and address fundamental vulnerability classes.

Notable Quotes

*"Let's not be a meat proxy. That's what you are when you are the interface to AI and you pass messages back and forth."* — Peter
*"We used to have pull requests to collaborate, but that just feels not the future anymore. Here on the server, anyone can see each other's session. You can prompt each other's session. It's meant for people that work together."* — Peter
*"Similar to how Kubernetes was the standard for deploying containers in the cloud, we envision OCE to do the same for agent deployments."* — Kevin Lin

So this is very easy for the agent to get a lot of data. And then we categorize them. And for certain categories, like update issues, the agent will read the report, will investigate. It will spin up a new test box to reproduce the error. If it can clearly reproduce it, it will come up with a fix. It will see if it's fixed. And if all of that works, there's going to be another agent that reviews that work. And then your work can automatically land. Now, what's the difference between a loop and a goal? So the loop basically keeps checking. This is something we do continuously. I have another one for performance. We do another release of the team server every day. And then my update loop will detect that. We'll take snapshots and we'll see if there's anything that regressed or it needs improvement. Now, if I do, you know, slash goal, that work is towards a defined end state. Like right now, I have a goal that slowly, bit by bit, migrates the code base from synchronous SQLite to async database access. We're still using SQLite here, but as the server grows and as we do more, we're thinking about maybe making the database itself swappable. So this has been running for 18 days. It's still running on my local Codex and it has landed over 100 PRs so far. Over time, I want an agent per project. It should have its own memory, automations, goals, loops, and I can give it those high-level commands and it maintains and improves my software. Now, should we check back what people are building so far? Who got lucky enough to actually use online? I see a few hands. Yeah, you were fast. So here we go. We made sure it's a one-click to get your subscription included and you can play along. So, we've been showing you how a small team can work with agents together. And as more and more teams started using agents, we ran into another question. How do you run, configure, and secure them all across an organization? And we definitely felt that pain. That's why we built an enterprise control plane. And today, we are open sourcing it. And with that, I'm going to bring up Kevin Lin. He's going to show you what we built. Hey, Kevin. Hi, everyone. I'm Kevin Lin. And I lead OpenClaw Enterprise at OpenAI. Now, OpenClaw Enterprise, or OCE in short, introduces an open source control plane to OpenClaw. It makes OpenClaw multi-tenant and adds governance and hard security boundaries to agent deployments. Now, we believe that a secure runtime is a necessary foundation for bringing persistent agents like dots and OpenClaw to everyone. And so, today, I'll walk you through some of the security fundamentals that we've built into the platform. First, we enforce hard boundaries between trusted and untrusted workloads. This means that deterministic code runs in a separate container, and in our case, a separate cluster from where the agent is executing. This prevents agents from changing their runtime. Second, we constrain what agents can do with sandboxing. We control their network access, what files they can write to, and what repositories they push to. This lets us give agents useful tools without giving them access to everything. Next, we add model-based reviews on top of our existing controls. We use auto-review, which is a feature in both Codex and OpenClaw that uses another model to review the agent's actions and block potentially dangerous ones before they execute. And finally, agents run as their own identities with dedicated service accounts and configurable connectors. This means that agents aren't impersonating users. They are themselves persistent entities. They can be independently controlled, audited, and if needed, turned off. Now, it's these controls that have helped us scale our internal OpenClaw deployments to over 400 Claws. They work around the clock, fixing code, doing experiments, analyzing data, and escalating security concerns. So now, I'm going to show you guys what that looks like. This is a hard Pictionary game. I don't know what that is. Okay. Because we are unveiling OC to the world today, let's create a community agent. What you're seeing right now are presets. Codex Has Left The Laptop | DevDay 2026

OpenAI(2.1M)·22:01·161 views·2026-10-07
Sam AltmanCEO, OpenAI
TL;DROpenAI announced Codex Cloud, Spaces, Dots, and integrations with Slack and Microsoft Teams at DevDay 2026, allowing developers to run long-term AI agents in cloud environments without tying up their local laptops.
Dominik demonstrated new capabilities including mobile source editing, Tailscale and OIDC networking support, an overhauled code review workflow, terminal voice mode, and a unified web interface. Codex Cloud Environments: Allows running tasks on cloud environments that...

TL;DR: OpenAI announced Codex Cloud, Spaces, Dots, and integrations with Slack and Microsoft Teams at DevDay 2026, allowing developers to run long-term AI agents in cloud environments without tying up their local laptops. Dominik demonstrated new capabilities including mobile source editing, Tailscale and OIDC networking support, an overhauled code review workflow, terminal voice mode, and a unified web interface.

Codex Cloud & Infrastructure

  • Codex Cloud Environments:
    • Allows running tasks on cloud environments that teams have created or by setting up new ones connected to selected repositories.
    • Handles environment preparation automatically by inspecting repositories, identifying dependencies, and writing startup scripts and skills.
    • Features file system snapshotting for faster subsequent task startup and background updates to keep repos current.
  • Network Integration & Security:
    • Tailscale VPN Support: Integrates via auth keys so cloud environments can securely access private resources (such as a local API running on a Raspberry Pi) without exposing them to the public internet.
    • OIDC Support: Enables accessing cloud resources through OIDC.
    • Vault & Sharing Controls: Personal environment variables can be stored in a vault to prevent sharing with the team, and environments can be restricted to individual use or shared across the entire organization.
  • Cross-Device Continuity:
    • Tasks run in the cloud, allowing developers to close their laptops and continue tracking or interacting with tasks via phone or browser.

Team Collaboration & Unified Workspaces

  • Spaces:
    • Serves as a collaborative document space for both human teams and Codex agents.
    • Eliminates the need to copy-paste information back and forth between different docs and agent chats.
    • Example given: Writing an API reference implementation plan, generating system diagrams, embedding task trackers, and having Codex update progress in real time as it executes tasks.
  • Slack and Microsoft Teams Integrations:
    • Brings Codex Cloud environments directly into team chat tools.
    • Demonstrated resolving a web app visual bug by having ChatGPT pull up the relevant pull request and Figma files, test changes via the shared cloud environment, and prepare the PR for review.

Code Review Overhaul

  • Desktop & Web Code Review Tab:
    • Redesigned code review interface inside the desktop app (with web support coming in the next coming days).
    • Displays PRs assigned to or created by the user.
    • Supports viewing code, leaving comments, reviewing peer feedback, and submitting review decisions.
    • Interactive Q&A: Allows developers to ask questions directly within the app regarding what a PR does and understand potential side effects.

Proactivity, Dots, and Terminal Enhancements

  • Dots:
    • Designed to manage existing Codex tasks and take on ongoing responsibilities.
    • Tracks product and engineering discussions, notices code changes, and spins up new tasks autonomously.
  • Terminal Updates:
    • At-mentions: Allows quick referencing of all used plugins.
    • Rich Rendering: Significantly faster text/image selection, along with direct rendering of diagrams and LaTeX inside the terminal.
    • Voice Mode: Supports speaking directly to the agent in the terminal using the GPT live model.
  • Web App Parity:
    • Chrome/browser interface mirrors the desktop experience, providing access to Codex Cloud tasks, app shots, media playback, and deployed preview sites.

Notable Quotes

"As models got better and in more capable especially around computer use and long-running work, we started handing increasingly more, uh increasingly bigger problems off to the models, often running for hours or days. And as a result, we started carrying our laptops half opened, just to keep them running between meetings." — Dominik

From Single Player to Multiplayer with Codex | DevDay 2026

OpenAI(2.1M)·23:01·32 views·2026-10-07
Sam AltmanCEO, OpenAI
TL;DRJason from OpenAI’s developer experience team explains how moving from single-player AI agents to collaborative, multiplayer systems—powered by persistent cloud agents called "dots," shared spaces, app shots, and voice integration—prevents individual burnout and distributes work across teams.
App Shots for Instant Context: Pressing the spacebar with two command buttons captures a screenshot alongside metadata, URLs, and below-the-fold content from any application. Named as a primary entry point, accounting for roughly 30% of all sessions started in Codex. ...

TL;DR

Jason from OpenAI’s developer experience team explains how moving from single-player AI agents to collaborative, multiplayer systems—powered by persistent cloud agents called "dots," shared spaces, app shots, and voice integration—prevents individual burnout and distributes work across teams.

1. Context Gathering: Meeting You Where You Are

  • App Shots for Instant Context:
    • Pressing the spacebar with two command buttons captures a screenshot alongside metadata, URLs, and below-the-fold content from any application.
    • Named as a primary entry point, accounting for roughly 30% of all sessions started in Codex.
  • Persistent Cloud Agents ("Dots"):
    • Unlike local automations that stop when a computer shuts down, dots run continuously in the cloud with their own computer, browser, and file environments.
    • They soak up background context from Slack, email, and browsers without requiring manual heartbeats or markdown vaults.
  • Embodied and Multi-Surface Access:
    • Dots can operate across phones, browsers, and local machines.
    • Users can enable settings allowing cloud agents to use the local machine as a "last mile" of work (e.g., logging into a browser, testing a local app).

2. Voice Integration and Team Collaboration

  • Agentic Voice:
    • Allows two-directional voice interaction on mobile and desktop apps, operating roughly 3 to 4 times faster than typing.
    • Enables messy, unscripted brainstorming sessions that the AI cleans up automatically.
  • Meeting Notes and Team Prompts:
    • ChatGPT meetings transcribe conversations and allow dots to pick up action items and context automatically.
    • Users can prompt agents mid-call to draft blog posts, create timelines, or send Slack messages.
  • Team Tagging:
    • Users can tag their dot in Slack or Teams (e.g., `@Jason dot, please investigate`) to delegate tasks following team design discussions.

3. Organizing Work in Shared Spaces

  • Spaces as Cloud Memory Vaults:
    • Acts as a single source of truth where agents and team members contribute without duplicating work across departments.
  • Agent MD Files and Page Instructions:
    • Directory-based agent MD files guarantee that automated writes remain human-readable with correct citations and formatting.
    • Custom artifacts, embedded HTML, and visualizations can be defined via page instructions to keep team agents aligned.
  • Cross-Departmental Use Cases:
    • *Finance:* Weekly analytics reports updated by cloud agents connected to cloud services.
    • *Engineering:* Organizing pull requests, project scope, and identifying designated DRI (Directly Responsible Individual) pages.
    • *Research:* Tracking long-running experiments.

4. Sharing Results via Plugins and Extensions

  • Plugins and Extensions:
    • Serve as shareable, updatable, and versioned instruction-based plugins distributed widely across teams.
    • Codex app acts as a cockpit; products like the meetings and code review tools are built on these exact plugin components.
  • MCP and WebMCP Integration:
    • Sites can serve Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers with authentication.
    • WebMCP allows the creation of tools that both humans and AI can use simultaneously.
  • Broader Creative and Production Workflows:
    • Used beyond engineering for production shoots (keeping sets updated during breaks) and content planning with creative and marketing teams.

Notable Quotes

"As I got more productive, I felt like at some point I was the glue for all these systems and I felt like I was a bottleneck for a lot of the things I was doing." — Jason
"At some point, your whole team should get involved... The conversations we have with my teammates ultimately become the prompts that we use for our agents that are running in the cloud." — Jason
"I want AI to do my laundry and wash my dishes so I can make art. And I'm definitely fully seeing that." — Jason
Whoa. This is cool. I feel like I recognize half the people in the audience. Thank you all for being here. Last year I was in the seating over there. It's kind of crazy to be up here now. Today I'm excited to talk about multiplayer and what that means. I'm Jason from the developer experience team at OpenAI. I've only been here about seven months. We've shipped a ton of features. As I got more productive, I felt like at some point I was the glue for all these systems and I felt like I was a bottleneck for a lot of the things I was doing. Just as a show of hands, who else feels this way now? There's so much to do, you're micromanaging. Thank God. But what ends up happening is first as you start, it's just about what I can do with AI. Then it becomes what can AI do with access to my computer and its files and the browser and these apps. But I think more than ever with these new tools, a lot of it now has become how can I make systems that bring the rest of my team along rather than just being this super-powered IC. So, when we saw tools like computer use and voice and all these cloud features, I wanted to try them all. But they were still mostly focused on scaling the individual. But now with things like dots, space, that has page instructions, team automations and plugin extensions, a lot of it now has become how we set up the team for success rather than just scaling up the individual. So, the talk is called multiplayer. So, let me also define what I think is multiplayer. It doesn't just mean that I have one chat bar with a bunch of my coworkers talking in the same thread. For me, it's about having the ability to know that I'm not the only individual that has to carry the work all the way through to the end. It means that the conversations I'm having with my teammates are just automatically picked up and so we can build systems where everything works together and comes together. And there's other tools and other AI that's helping me keep track of what's been done and know that it will lead on us to make the right decisions. So let's start with a hypothetical example. Imagine it's me, it's 8am, I'm on Twitter and there's some comments about usage limits or rate limit issues. Obviously, I don't know what happened. I don't know what broke. I don't know if anyone else is investigating. But I do know that we take these things really seriously. And if they're related, I need to keep track of everything. I need to escalate when it makes sense to. I need to make sure that none of all this content gets lost in my browser on a conversation in Slack. Six months ago, I was doing a lot of this manually. Right? Manually with Codex, at least. I would use internal tools or Codex. I would ask it to check Slack and make sense of what's going on. And on top of that, all of it still ran on my computer. And I was still the one pushing things forward, gluing these pieces together. And it was a lot of work. Three months ago, things got a lot better. Tasks could work on my behalf. I would pin a thread, set an automation, and then hope that everything was caught up on time. But the issue was that people couldn't pick up where I left off. And so everything still came through me. I just became, like, a PM and also customer support. And then what you find out is, like, half the other teams are also doing the same task and just repeating all this work instead of actually participating in a more unified push. Now I think we're entering a place where multiplayer makes a lot more sense. We have a world where if someone leaves an issue or comments on Slack, my dot can pick it up and follow along, keep track of things, independent of whether it's email or Slack or a browser or anything else. And then I can just tell my dot to keep an eye on the investigation and make sense of it and unblock itself as it interacts with other people on my team, the engineers, and even some of the customers. Right? Tools like dot and space make it much easier for people to work together without having to do all the stuff that I did, which is, like, 15-pinned automations with heartbeats and a memory vault and markdown that I'm, like, not updating well enough. And then what's going to happen is as these things work across the team and people understand these tools, you can start turning them into more shareable skills and plugins and extensions that make everyone's lives easier, not just yourself. And to be fair, like, if you use a lot of, you know, agents and tokens, I don't think it makes your life easier either. And so if you've seen me give these talks in the past, I tend to think about these things in terms of three big acts. The first one is, like, how do I bring the context into my agent? This might be context from my desktop, the browser, it might be from my mind and the mind of my coworkers and on Slack and email and all these things. Then it's about how you actually keep the work moving. How do you keep it organized? Because I can't even fit in my mind. And lastly, how do you actually share that with the team and level them up as well? So first, let's gather the context. So if you go back to this example of maybe seeing some stuff online in my browser, the first thing I would do is take an app shot. As a show of hands, like, how many people have sent or used apps in the past? Nice. I'm trying to convert you today as well. So an app shot is a great way of sending context to ChatGPT. So what you do is if you just, like, find the space bar and there's going to be two command buttons on the side. And it takes a screenshot. And this is really the idea of just having an agent meet you where you are. And tagging it, I think, is going to be a concept we think about a lot as we move forward. All right. And so let's also talk about what it means to be embodied in a couple of ways. Back to app shots. The issue is when I take an app shot, it's just my desktop context. But the app shot is sent to the cloud. My dot runs in the cloud. It has its own computer, its own browser, its own files. And so it can run this investigation regardless of whether or not your computer is turned on. But it's nice that dot still meets me where I am. It can still take the context from my laptop. And so if you just enable it in your settings, your computer can still be the last mile of work for any of your agents in the cloud. Right? The investigation can start on my computer. Higgsfield - Building Agents That Provision Their Own Compute | DevDay 2026

OpenAI(2.1M)·12:50·42 views·2026-10-07
Alex ReibmanCEO, Higgsfield
TL;DRHiggsfield built an agentic system called Supercomputer that allows AI agents to choose models, self-evaluate quality, and spin up their own GPU infrastructure to orchestrate end-to-end multimedia and interactive media campaigns.
Led by co-founder and CEO Alex Mashrabov, the platform enables small teams of 1 to 5 people to produce complex creative workflows like 10-hour live-stream world reconstructions and automated AI video editing. End-to-End Campaign Building: Higgsfield functions as a multimedia AI...

TL;DR

Higgsfield built an agentic system called Supercomputer that allows AI agents to choose models, self-evaluate quality, and spin up their own GPU infrastructure to orchestrate end-to-end multimedia and interactive media campaigns. Led by co-founder and CEO Alex Mashrabov, the platform enables small teams of 1 to 5 people to produce complex creative workflows like 10-hour live-stream world reconstructions and automated AI video editing.

Platform Capabilities & Creative Workflows

  • End-to-End Campaign Building: Higgsfield functions as a multimedia AI platform where creative marketers can build advertising campaigns from scratch.
      • Model Integration: Combines advanced image and video models provided by Higgsfield with OpenAI models like Astra to drive workflows.
      • Production Democratization: Reduces traditional production requirements—such as a 15-person team proficient in 10 to 12 different tools—down to teams of just one to three people.
  • Computer Use & Tool Orchestration: Leverages OpenAI's Astra model to unlock computer use cases, enabling agents to call 10 to 12 different creative programs on behalf of a creator.
      • Gaming Industry Parallels: Addresses rising visual quality bars and ballooning budgets by empowering smaller teams to succeed.
      • Automated Video Editing: Open-sourced an AI video editing skill designed to create AI YouTube explainer videos (which Higgsfield internally uses to build tutorials for its own products).

Autonomous Compute Provisioning & Infrastructure

  • Agentic Compute Management: To achieve consistent execution across complex use cases, Higgsfield built Supercomputer, an agent capable of launching its own GPU infrastructure on the fly.
  • Deterministic Workflows & Self-Evaluation:
      • Decision-Making: The agent makes real-time decisions on which models and use cases to deploy as it runs.
      • Quality Criteria: Utilizes self-evaluation to define specific output criteria and determine whether generation quality is acceptable.
  • Enterprise Security & Guardrails: Implements secure deployment through perimeter infrastructure with GPUs, enabling accelerated enterprise go-to-market strategies while making similar capabilities available via the Higgsfield API.

Real-World Deployments & Case Studies

  • Live AI Stream with Neon:
      • Partnership: Collaborated with popular streamer Neon and Adil (Higgsfield's spokesperson) for the first AI-generated live stream.
      • Execution: Involved walking through New York City while Astra reconstructed the entire world in real-time to feel realistic.
      • Scale: The live stream ran continuously for over 10 hours.
  • Continuous Video Generation:
      • Cost & R&D: Spent over $30,000 in compute and development to deliver continuous experiences and make a 10-minute long video consistently using the Astra model.
      • Accessibility: Paved the way for developers to ship similar interactive experiences through the Higgsfield API.

Notable Quotes

"We actually had to build agent which can launch its own GPU infrastructure... and that has been critically important for us to deliver a really consistent execution across various use cases." — Alex Mashrabov
"Astra was able to really reconstruct the whole world, so it feels realistic... the stream went over 10 hours. And imagine, like, this is new form of media, which would be impossible to imagine before that." — Alex Mashrabov
"In just 18 months, we built Higgsfield, and now is the best time to build yours." — Alex Mashrabov

How OpenAI Puts ChatGPT to Work | DevDay 2026

OpenAI(2.1M)·19:41·484 views·2026-10-07
Sam AltmanCEO, OpenAI
TL;DROpenAI showcased how its internal power users leverage ChatGPT as an agentic teammate—utilizing new features like ChatGPT Spaces and autonomous "Dots"—to automate repetitive tasks, scale expertise, and free up time for high-value work.
Nick & Danielle: Developer Experience team members at OpenAI and session hosts. Dani: Leads Developer Social at OpenAI; uses ChatGPT agents to monitor product launches, synthesize developer feedback, and track Slack channels. Adrian: Leads Competitive Research (on a...

TL;DR

OpenAI showcased how its internal power users leverage ChatGPT as an agentic teammate—utilizing new features like ChatGPT Spaces and autonomous "Dots"—to automate repetitive tasks, scale expertise, and free up time for high-value work.

Presenters & Champions

  • Nick & Danielle: Developer Experience team members at OpenAI and session hosts.
  • Dani: Leads Developer Social at OpenAI; uses ChatGPT agents to monitor product launches, synthesize developer feedback, and track Slack channels.
  • Adrian: Leads Competitive Research (on a two-person team); uses plugins, custom workflows, and automated Dots to analyze competitor revenue, review sales call transcripts, and update internal research hubs.
  • Casey: Chief People Officer at OpenAI; aggregates organizational research into shared knowledge bases and powers leadership coaching sites with GPT Live.

ChatGPT Work & Automated Dots

  • Autonomous Agent Capabilities: Dots live in the cloud with their own computer and browser environments, giving them ongoing responsibilities like monitoring feeds, updating content sheets, and pinging users on Slack when urgent attention is needed.
  • Omnichannel Reach: Cross-Platform Access: Users can text, call, or message their Dot while commuting or away from their desk, and Dots can access local files when the user is on their laptop.
  • Customization: Dot Setup: Users name their Dot, pick accessories/avatars, define ongoing responsibilities, and integrate communication channels (like Slack or text messaging), with Dots learning user preferences over time.

Collaborative Spaces & Knowledge Sharing

  • ChatGPT Spaces: Shared Workspaces: Allows teams and agents to work together on the same page simultaneously, supporting real-time comments, inline questioning, and shared page edits while individual chat threads remain private.
  • Page Instructions: Agent Guidelines: Similar to an `.md` file for codebases, users can write instructions into pages to ensure agents maintain a consistent style when organizing new research or meeting notes.
  • Scaling Expertise: Institutional Knowledge: Leaders can aggregate disparate meeting notes, papers, and articles into shared knowledge bases that feed interactive coaching sites (powered by GPT Live) so teams can access expertise without direct 1:1 meetings.
