How to find and exploit the "glitch" that can make any business take off

My First Million(961.0K)·63:16·10.3K views·2026-10-07
Shaan PuriHost, My First Million Jeff HarmonFounder, Angel Studios
TL;DRJeff Harmon shares how founders can find marketing "glitches"—untapped channels of attention—to scale businesses, drawing on his experience building viral ad campaigns for brands like Poo-Pourri and Squatty Potty, and scaling Angel Studios into a top-10 global movie studio doing nearly $500M in revenue.
Definition of a Glitch: Look for areas where you have attention from people that nobody else is paying attention to, functioning as a tactical beachhead for growth. The Ora Brush Origin Story: Jeff Harmon helped a college professor sell a tongue cleaner sitting in boxes in...

TL;DR

Jeff Harmon shares how founders can find marketing "glitches"—untapped channels of attention—to scale businesses, drawing on his experience building viral ad campaigns for brands like Poo-Pourri and Squatty Potty, and scaling Angel Studios into a top-10 global movie studio doing nearly $500M in revenue.

1. Finding Your "Glitch" in Marketing

  • Definition of a Glitch: Look for areas where you have attention from people that nobody else is paying attention to, functioning as a tactical beachhead for growth.
  • The Ora Brush Origin Story:
    • Jeff Harmon helped a college professor sell a tongue cleaner sitting in boxes in his garage by buying a Facebook page called "Kisses" (1.3M followers) and pivoting it to Ora Brush.
    • Initial ads failed because they focused on product details rather than the happy ending/benefit for the customer (e.g., kissing without bad breath shame).
  • The YouTube Ad Arbitrage Glitch:
    • Leveraged YouTube's newly launched ad platform to run a $500 script-driven video (using a spoon-scraping bad breath demonstration inspired by an I Justine video) at less than a penny per view.
    • This early-mover advantage yielded a $2-$3 return for every $1 spent on total costs (ads, shipping, product), eventually leading to a $10M company exit.

2. Product Development and the "User Method"

  • Solve Your Own Problems: Follow the "User Method" popularized by Jeff Schwarting—most top businesses are built by founders solving personal problems (e.g., Airbnb founders trying to pay rent).
  • Dr. Bob's Motivation: The inventor of Ora Brush was a mission president in the Philippines trying to solve horrible breath among his missionaries.
  • Agentic Shopping Opportunities: Shift attention away from Google search and optimize for AI agents (like Grockbot) where shopping happens via direct API integrations without users leaving the AI interface.

3. Crafting High-Conversion, Comedy-Driven Ads

  • The Harmon Brothers Formula:
    • Combine a rigid structure (problem, solution, 3 core elements, credibility, call to action) with professional comedians who understand how to build humor that drives the core message.
    • It is significantly easier to train a comedian to be a marketer than a marketer to be funny.
  • High-Stakes Creative Retreats: Lock creatives, attorneys, and key stakeholders in a cabin for 2–3 days with competing script reads until a winning concept is forged and signed off.
  • Poo-Pourri and Squatty Potty Breakthroughs: Transformed struggling or small-scale products into billion-dollar brands by leaning into absurd, high-contrast juxtapositions (e.g., a cute British girl talking about pooping on a toilet).

4. Disrupting the Movie Business with Angel Studios

  • The Angel Guild Model:
    • Built a community of ~3M members paying $12–$20/month who vote on incoming projects, receive two movie tickets per month, and actively fund/feedback-loop indie filmmakers.
    • Members have paid out $300M in profit shares to filmmakers, with Angel splitting profits 50/50 with creators.
  • The Chosen Pilot Experiment:
    • Bypassed traditional Hollywood gatekeepers by uploading a 19-minute pilot as a skippable YouTube ad, raising $11M from 17,000 investors via $800K in ad spend.
    • The resulting series achieved 200M+ global viewers and seven seasons of production.
  • Viral Stunts and Algorithm Warming:
    • Leveraged Kevin James's face in unlisted social channels and viral art-teacher personas to "warm up" algorithmic facial recognition before launching projects like *Solo Meal* and *Runner* (which secured 100M views via a comedic minivan/Owen Wilson campaign).
    • Executed a Super Bowl stunt by placing Kevin James in a tuxedo surrounded by influencers and cameras in the center of 12 empty seats without any direct commercial messaging.

5. Executive AI Workflows and Economics

  • Personal Vibe Coding: Jeff Harmon uses a personal vibe coder and late-night Claude/GPT prompts running agents overnight to prototype software tools (such as automated movie trailer AB-testing links) before handing them off to the product team.
  • AI Harnesses: Angel Studios utilizes internal brand-guide harnesses hooked into AI coding models to ensure automated output respects company rules.
  • AI and Employment Economics: Dismisses the idea of mass AI-driven unemployment, arguing that when a tool makes teams 10x more effective, businesses expand headcount to capitalize on increased productivity rather than shrinking.

Notable Quotes

*"First one is go after things that solve a problem for yourself... Out of the top businesses, the vast majority of them were made by founders who were just solving a problem for themselves."* — Jeff Harmon

>

*"In the marketing world, I learned very quickly when we were building the Harmon Brothers ad agency that it's way easier to train a comedian to be a marketer than it is to train a marketer to be funny."* — Jeff Harmon

>

*"My best friend in Hollywood was worse than my worst enemy in Las Vegas."* — Unnamed retired executive (quoted by Jeff Harmon)

>

*"If a man sues you for your cloak, give him your coat also... letting that go and just going after new ideas and having an abundance mentality allowed me to focus on the things that helped us build a big ad agency."* — Jeff Harmon
Everybody who starts a business, you have to find your glitch. Look for areas where you have attention from people that nobody else is paying attention to. Here's why I'm excited about this. I've never met you, but I've been a fan of your work because you're kind of the creative genius behind the scenes of some of the coolest marketing campaigns that I remember. I kind of grew up on the internet, and I read your book back in the day. Well, you read *Poop to Gold*. Yeah, *Poop to Gold*. On top of that, what you're doing now is fascinating. I had honestly never heard of Angel Studios, and here you are. You've built maybe one of the top 10 TV and movie studios in the world; you're doing almost 500 million in revenue. I would guess that I'm not alone and that 98% of our audience has also never heard about this. The cool thing is you did it with pretty much no experience or background in the industry, which I always like. I like somebody who doesn't self-limit around what they can and can't do based on their own track record. You went in and you did something really remarkable, and so I want to talk about all that. But I think we should start with the marketing because that was my intro to you. Can you take us back to Dr. Bob and how you helped a dentist create one of the great marketing campaigns? Where were you, what happened, and can you tell that story? I was in my last day of my last class at university and a guy named Dr. Bob came in. He was asking the class to tell him how to make his Orapup—which was a tongue cleaner that scrapes your tongue and cleans off bad breath—actually sell. He had invented it, and I don't know, he had thousands of units of it just in boxes at his house. Sitting in his garage? Yeah, his garage was full of them. The group got up and just said, "You can't really sell these and nobody wants to buy these online." They claimed like 8% of people want to buy them on the internet and that you need to go do a partnership with Procter & Gamble or Johnson & Johnson or somebody. I interrupted them and said, "8% of people is like 30 million people just in the United States. Why wouldn't you focus on them?" So the smart kid answer was that over 90% of people don't want to buy this online, and you said, "Wait a minute. If 8% said yes, that's still millions. That's enough to clear out the garage." That's right. I didn't think much of it until Dr. Bob came up to me after class and said, "I liked your comments. Can I work with you?" The first thing I did was I convinced Dr. Bob to spend $5,000 buying a Facebook page called "Kisses." There was just some guy somewhere in the country who had built this page; I think it had 1.3 million followers. We took it, we bought it, and then I started spamming them with Orapup because bad breath and kissing—that was the connection. You did get one thing right. I think one of the easy mistakes most people make is they think about their product and they just want to tell you all about it versus the benefit you get if you were to use it. The happy ending you seek. This product is just a tool that's going to get you to that happy ending—the better you, the version of you that they can kiss without the shame of bad breath. So you did have that insight. That's pretty good. Yeah. We took the 1.3 million people and we spammed them. Once we got Orapup created as an entity, I changed the name of the "Kisses" page to Orapup. It was a brand followed by 1.3 million people. Yes, we had one of the most followed brands on social media. I think Facebook changed their rules after that, and that failed miserably. We didn't sell hardly anything. We tried Google ads, but they weren't working; I think we were spending like $3 to $5 per dollar that we brought in in sales because it's just a little tool. Then I was watching YouTube late at night—I watched a lot of YouTube—and there was a YouTuber called iJustine. She was an original vlogger. In her videos, she would jump cut. She stood in front of a white background and she would just cut to get rid of all the "ums" and whatever wasn't interesting. She just cut it all out. I was like, "We could probably make something at that level." I got another roommate named Evan Graham; he was a film major. Every single night he would go out and work on film sets for free. Then I got another roommate, Joel Ari, who was a screenwriter and a comedian. I said, "Will you take this script I wrote and punch it up to make it a good script?" I took all the knowledge I had about selling stuff from serving a mission for the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints. I served in Ireland, getting doors slammed in my face for two years. You become a pretty good salesperson selling the gospel and learn how to take rejection. As a teenager, I sold Idaho potatoes door-to-door in Utah to try to earn money and I did pretty well with that. I took those two skill sets, combined them, and built a script that was about how to tell if you have bad breath. I've talked to hundreds of entrepreneurs on this podcast and I've got a bunch of takeaways, but one of the big ones that shocks a lot of the product nerds—which, frankly, I am myself—is that often, in order to turn something that's going kind of good into something that's absolutely amazing, you have to fix the marketing, not the product. In particular, fix how many people know about the product. It's called attention. Say it with me: attention. This team at Starter Story put together a free guide they call the "$1 Million Attention Guide." It's an amazing 39-page document filled with 15 tactics to get eyeballs on what you are building. There's even a 30-minute recording from Pat, who is the founder of Starter Story. He has interviewed hundreds and hundreds of entrepreneurs, so he's able to put this together through a bunch of research and experience. If you're interested in getting more attention, which frankly you should be, you should click the link below in the description or scan the QR code right here. There was a video on YouTube called Howcast. One of their videos that had like 400,000 views was "How to tell if you have bad breath." You just take a spoon, scrape the back of your tongue, smell what's on the spoon, and then you can tell if you have bad breath. Because you actually can't tell if you have bad breath if you just blow like that. If you can tell if you have bad breath when you blow, it is horrible breath. But just normal bad breath, you need the spoon. You scrape your tongue, and that's because the tongue is the source of 90% of bad breath, which I found in studies. What happens is you're taking in all this raw data: the Howcast video, the iJustine video, your roommates, your experience from the last five years of selling potatoes and the gospel, and this tongue cleaner. You're combining it all into a video that is, I think even today, still considered one of the top 25 campaigns of all time on YouTube. We spent $500 on it—Dr. Bob complained about the cost at the time—and we launched this video. I got my brother Neil to code up a site so we could actually sell these, and I set it up to where you could get a free one if you just paid shipping. I charged as much for shipping as it cost to buy the raw version. It worked, partially. Going back in time, I found a glitch in the system, and everybody who starts a business has to find their glitch. Our glitch was that YouTube launched its ad platform a few weeks before that, or maybe a couple of months. That's where the idea came from; we got on YouTube and we started buying ads, and those ads cost less than a penny per view. It was like the early days of AdSense, so we were able to spend a dollar and bring in two or three dollars—a dollar in total costs for the ads, the shipping, and the brush itself. That glitch of being the very first on YouTube is what allowed us to find enough data to take action, optimize, and become more efficient. As the cost of advertising on YouTube started to climb, we were able to get more and more efficient because we had the traffic coming in to make those changes. The glitch allowed us to get what one entrepreneur, Paul Olstrom, who wrote the book *Neil and Skelleet*, calls your "beachhead." When you land on Normandy, the first group has to go up and establish a beachhead where they have a foothold. They don't have to take the whole beach, just that one little spot, and then you can expand from that beachhead. So you put 500 bucks into making the ad. With the Orapup, what did you guys do in revenue, or how many sold? I don't remember the revenues, but I think the company sold for about $10 million. You're just stitching this whole thing together. I love the framework of "you have to find your glitch." The hard part, if you're listening to that, is that YouTube ads are not a penny anymore. So that specific glitch is always changing for different people at different times. Do you think you could generalize any of the principles or behaviors—what to be looking for, or what to do to increase your odds of noticing things? The first one is: go after things that solve a problem for yourself. Dr. Bob was just making a tongue cleaner because—this is funny—he was a mission president serving in the Philippines for the LDS church, and his missionaries had horrible breath. He was trying to figure out how to solve the problem of these missionaries having awful breath. He learned that it came from tongue cleaning, and he remembered from his professional life that they would take these surgical scrub brushes to get their hands clean. He took a surgical brush, put a tongue scraper on it, and that was his prototype. He had super-glued together a toothbrush handle, a surgical brush, and a scraper. He was solving a problem for himself, and it happened to be something that many other people wanted to solve. There's a book called *The User Method* by Jeff Schwarting, which explains that the vast majority of top businesses were made by founders who were just solving a problem for themselves. The Airbnb founders were just trying to pay rent in San Francisco. One of them was a graphic designer; he knew there was a convention coming to town, they went to Walmart, bought air mattresses, blew them up, and sold their floor space. You end up with Airbnb. They paid their rent, and it becomes bigger than any hotel chain. So that's the first thing: finding a product that can work via the user method. The second thing is a glitch in the marketplace. It becomes really obvious; the guy who invented the mouse that worked with Steve Jobs said that as soon as they invented it, everyone was like, "Obviously the mouse makes sense." In hindsight, it's always obvious. You're finding a glitch in and of itself just with your product. Then the next piece is on the marketing front: look for areas where you have attention from people that nobody else is paying attention to. A really good example: I was searching for a product, but I was using Grok, not Google. I haven't used Google for search in quite some time. Not just that, if you do headless shopping or agentic shopping—I think that's what they call it—you optimize for agents where they don't have to leave their AI. Tons of people are using Grokbot to just go do their shopping. You could be driving home in your Tesla, push a button, turn on Grokbot, and say, "Can you get me groceries on the way home?" If you're a business that has an agentic shopping system where the AI bot doesn't have to scrape everything but just talks to your API, then you can plug into this rapidly growing group of people who want to shop through their bots. They don't want to shop themselves; that's a huge opportunity and a potential glitch. So product is the user method, and marketing is paying attention to where other people are not. Maybe it's a little bit of the "innovator's dilemma"—they're doing well, or they've built their business and team all around one channel that has been working for 10 years, so they might put 1% or 10% of their attention on this new thing. You could put 100%. You could change the whole experience to just work in that new channel. It's like just getting somebody to use quick design at work can be really hard. Because of that, we are outpacing the Hollywood world by leaps and bounds in speed of innovation, especially on a distribution level, because we know how to use these tools and they're not only scared of them, they're actively fighting them. It's low-brow to use the tools for them in Hollywood right now. I want to ask you about finding a glitch, jumping ahead to Angel. This is a bit of a deep cut, but when I was doing the research, I was talking to Ben and I was like, "You know what blows me away? You did a project where you were tired of the way Hollywood movies worked. They were using sex, drugs, and rock and roll, and vulgar language, so you created something that would scrub that stuff out of an existing movie." That was a product we wanted to exist; it was a problem you had. Well, how many kids do you have? I have three kids. Then now you would agree. When I go on YouTube, I'm like, "This is a danger zone." If you go back and look at what we were looking at, *Game of Thrones* was at its peak when we started. It was called VidAngel. Every single day when *Game of Thrones* would come out, there would be, within 24 hours on the torrents, an episode uploaded without the sex, nudity, or rape scenes. It was completely scrubbed. There were millions of people worldwide downloading the version without those. We were looking at that and saying, "Not only do we want something like this for our kids, but what about the millions of people saying, 'My teenager wants to watch this. I want them to participate in the cultural conversation, but I don't want them to see rape scenes.' We're saying, 'Let's build a legal version of what millions of people are already asking for.'" You're doing the thing again where you're focused on that 8%. I guess million sounds like a big number, but it's a very small number compared to everyone else. If you talked to 100 people about *Game of Thrones*, how many would say they watched the clean torrent version? You almost would have gotten no hands. But you do a good job of paying attention to the signals, especially when it lines up with your values. Even today, I saw in your published data that you guys are doing like 90 million a quarter, so hundreds of millions in revenue, yet you only have about two million subscribers. It's the power of a small number of people paying you 13 bucks a month. That doesn't sound like an Elon Musk business plan; it sounds like an automatic remote control. You had enough conviction to go ahead and do it. Then you found this glitch in the matrix where you find this short film you're interested in. The thing I loved was how you tested it: you realized you could just upload the whole 19-minute thing as a YouTube ad. Exactly. People could have skipped it after 7 seconds, but we just said, "Let's put it on there. Maybe it's entertaining enough that people will watch it and that'll give us the signal." At the end of the 19-minute ad, it's like, "Hey, if you've been watching this whole thing, crowdfund this and help us bring this to life." I remember someone saying, "Why would you upload a 19-minute ad?" I said, "Even if only 3% of people make it to the end, if they're the ones who want it to exist, they're going to invest and we'll be able to make a TV series." Is that just your brain works differently, or do you have a rule about thinking in this "naughty" way where you're not just playing a default game trying to knock on the same crowded front door? I had the opportunity when we were doing the ad agency. We did Poo-Pourri, Squatty Potty, Purple Mattress, Lumi deodorant—these billion-dollar brands that we built from 4 million. Poo-Pourri was doing 4 million a year when we got ahold of it. Purple Mattress was almost bankrupt; one of the founders called me two weeks after we launched the campaign and said, "You saved our business. We were going to go bankrupt in three weeks." I was thinking, "We probably wouldn't have been paid if we weren't successful!" We dodged a bullet. As we did that, YouTube talked to us and asked, "How can we get more people to do things like Orapup?" They sat with our team in Provo, Utah, for three or four days, and we came up with the skip ad button. Originally, they were going to make it a 15-second or 30-second ad, and I said, "No, don't put a time limit on it. If I can maintain your interest, let me keep them." That's so counterintuitive. You're the advertiser telling the platform to let them skip after 5 seconds? It's the only way to get a long ad. If I had said, "Let me steal 5 minutes of their time for a Poo-Pourri commercial," they would have said, "No, we don't want you getting in front of the content." But I've earned the attention. When we got to the *The Chosen* TV series, we had this pilot episode. I called it a pilot, and we took the same concept we built for two- or three-minute ads and went 20 minutes. We did 20 minutes because it's entertainment. They watched 20 minutes, they were totally invested, and then we pitched them: "Come invest in this project and let's make a TV series like *Game of Thrones* for the New Testament." 17,000 people invested $11 million into *The Chosen*. How much ad spend do you think you needed to get to that $11 million? $800,000. It took a little less than 8% of the total raise to advertise it. I explained to Dallas Jenkins, the director, that if you had gone to Wall Street, they would have charged you 12% to raise that money. We're right in that world, but we're just buying ads, which is getting you awareness. We also used other things. We had a friend, Daryl Eves, who runs VidSummit and knows a lot about live streaming. He set up his switchboard, and we ran live streams to raise money at the end. For the last 24 to 48 hours of fundraising, Dallas Jenkins got on and did this marathon live stream. My team made it so every time someone invested, we would pop up and say, "This person just invested $100 from Idaho." We had social proof showing up on the screen the whole time. We cracked the nut and were able to get our first TV series built. Then the job was: can Dallas make a good enough series? He did. He crushed it. It's a top-five series in the world for television; it's got like 200 million people watching it. What? How many seasons is it in? They just finished filming season 7. That's the final, when Jesus is resurrected. It's remarkable. We did the first three seasons. You mentioned the other brands—Squatty Potty, Poo-Pourri. What was the marketing genius moment for each? The first thing was I had to like the product. I'd give the team the Squatty Potty, we'd try it, and we were like, "This is a pretty legit product." Poo-Pourri—I went in the bathroom, used it, and came out and asked my wife, "Go in after me. Can you smell anything?" She's like, "It smells good in there." That's a new experience. We created the concept with Susie Batis; I brought in Joel, the writer, and we sat there for three or four days and wrote out the Poo-Pourri ad. We had the concept: we need to get a British girl, put her on a toilet, and have these juxtapositions where you've got this cute girl talking about pooping, which is totally out of left field. You go away for 3 days and come out with this incredible ad that transforms the trajectory of the business. We all want to do that. I've taken the team away so many times, trying to make magic happen. Sometimes you do, and sometimes you don't. But I'm always winging it. I have no idea how I'm supposed to facilitate that kind of breakthrough. What do you do to increase the odds? Locking everybody in a cabin—the attorneys, the business leaders, everyone who has to sign off—helps because you can go back and forth for months and neuter a script by email, or you can just get in a cabin for 2 or 3 days and come out with something everyone is excited about. In the marketing world, I learned very quickly that it's way easier to train a comedian to be a marketer than to train a marketer to be funny. We would build the structure of an ad—problem, solution, three elements, credibility, call to action—and then the comedians would come in and build out the humor. Getting comedians is critical to the infrastructure of a marketing company because they come out of left field. I'd always tell them, "If your joke doesn't add to the message, you just have to kill it." It either adds to the message or it doesn't work. In Squatty Potty and Poo-Pourri, every joke brings you a little closer to understanding why you want that product. Do you come in with a solid idea, or do you start with sticky notes on the wall? Weeks before the retreat, three to four comedians are trained. We send them the product, they play with it, and they show up with rough scripts. The first morning, they do a script read of their vision. By the afternoon or evening, you're picking the winner—the bones of the ad. For the next three days, you're cannibalizing all the other scripts for their best elements and putting them into that lead script. Would you still do things this way, or would you do something completely different now? We still do it to some extent. If you watch "Runner," there's a campaign for Angel with Alan Richardson and Owen Wilson where our team built a campaign with comedians in a very similar process. It's a married couple trying to figure out date night, driving in their minivan to the movie, and they have their two movie tickets because Angel's model is that if you're a member of the Angel Guild, you're paying 20 bucks a month and you get two tickets to every movie Angel releases. They're racing through the streets talking about the program, and they walk into the movie and Owen Wilson is sitting in front of them. That campaign had like 100 million views. We still do that, but these are totally different than Orapup. Orapup was just $500 a day. When you're launching movies in theaters, you're launching a $10 million to $50 million startup every month. You're running teams year-round, launching every four to six weeks. It's just bonkers. If you go to *Sound of Freedom*, which came out in February, it's a rom-com. Kevin James came up with this idea where he said, "Why don't we just make the main character a personality on Instagram and TikTok?" He'd go and teach art classes in videos where he'd paint something for a few minutes and talk in the background like a Bob Ross-type character. We sent a team to Long Island to live with Kevin, and all they did was produce this character. We started warming up Kevin James's face; what really matters on social media these days is what images are in the video. The algorithm hashes faces into the algorithm. Kevin went and warmed up his face by taking older things he had done and reposting them. By the time we launched the new channel, the TikTok and Instagram algorithms recognized this was Kevin James. Because he was trending elsewhere, the moment we launched, they started going viral. Is this also the mechanism for the Andrew Tates and streamers where they pay armies of clippers to chop up their live stream content? Are they kind of doing the same thing? They're trying to warm up their faces, yes. They're trying to find a viral clip. Today, one of the main strategies is you're taking swings at bat. For Kevin James, we probably cut thousands of videos to try to get a few of them to go off. They're just doing everything they can to ride waves that already exist. If you watch the "Critical Drinker," he's a big YouTube reviewer; he's constantly showing little scenes of all these different movies, so he's basically hashing all kinds of famous faces into his reviews. He's clipping to all these famous movies and getting served up in more places. In the case with Kevin, he's already an A-list celebrity, so it's easy to hash our way into it. That campaign got over a billion views. On Super Bowl Sunday, we bought 12 seats at the Super Bowl and put Kevin in the center in his exact costume from the movie. We didn't say anything, but we bought seats all around him and put influential people with Instagram and TikTok accounts there. They were all posting, "Is this Kevin James?" That campaign never talked about anything commercial. You had a desire for a different type of content. Your content is the antithesis of nihilism. Nihilism is basically like you get to the end of *Game of Thrones* and everybody's a bad guy. There are no redeeming characters; it's all darkness. Nihilism is just the idea that there's no purpose to life. Ours is values-based. A subset of that is the faith audience. They're people who want content that has faith in it. We do faith content pretty hard at Easter and Christmas, and the rest of the year we're pushing just values-based content—westerns, action movies like *Runner*. If you watch it, it's packed full of action, but when you think about it, there's no sex or strong language. It's certified hot on Rotten Tomatoes, 90% from the audience, and not a single comment that says, "I wish there was more vulgarity." When it first came to us, it was an R-rated movie and we said, "We'll take it if you can make it PG-13 and get it to pass the Angel Guild." Our three million members—we don't call them subscribers, we call them members because they're participating—get to vote on every movie that goes to theaters. If the audience vetoes a film, we can't take it. You get a vote, you get two movie tickets, and you're supporting filmmakers at every step. You're supporting them in their rough cuts and giving feedback. These are people who are members of the community; presumably, they're market participants, more like a prediction market than a random poll on the internet. Do you have a higher hit rate? There's a study that's almost a year old, but we hit an average box office higher than any independent studio. We're top 10 in the US for the last three years. Is your business a batting average business or a slugging percentage business? Are you looking to not have flops, or are you looking for the runaway successes? I think we're a base-hits business that gets home runs every once in a while. *Sound of Freedom* was a home run—$250 million worldwide—and that was a $14 million movie. Can you explain the movie business? You spend $14 million making it and $40 million marketing it. The box office is $250 million, but the theater takes a cut. If you have a good distributor, they'll take half. In our case, we don't take part of the other half; we only take profits. We split the profits with the filmmaker. Every other distributor is going to take between 12.5% and 30% of the other half. How does the business work for the members paying 20 bucks and getting tickets? Is it a loss leader? It's more like MoviePass in that we rely on some breakage; the average person goes to three or four movies a year. So, we assume our average member isn't going to go to every single movie, and it balances out. This is something people don't understand about the movie business. Lionsgate only acquires or makes a small percentage of their movies. Every year, there are around a thousand movies made by independent filmmakers. Most of those never find a home. Those are made mostly by very wealthy entrepreneurs; I've never met a billionaire that doesn't want to make movies. Everyone has this in them—they want to tell stories. What the Angel Guild allows you to do is join about 3 million other people who are all paying 12 to 20 bucks a month and make movies like billionaires do. You've built this vertical mob of people who like the idea of getting to have their say, having influence, and putting money behind something. It's like fantasy football, but you get to play. So far, the Angel Guild has paid out $300 million to filmmakers. I was looking at your data and it says you lost 50 million bucks. But you're not paying to produce these movies, and you have these members paying you. What are you spending all the money on? Is it the marketing of the movies? Marketing is a big piece. Acquiring new members is not cheap. Is this a good business? I've been in the game for 20 years, and I've seen businesses that are knife fights with terrible margins, and some that are beautiful, recurring-revenue businesses. How is the movie business? One executive who had worked in the mob-run casinos in Las Vegas and then in Hollywood said, "My best friend in Hollywood was worse than my worst enemy in Las Vegas." It's a knife fight. I ask myself every week, "Do I really want to be in this business?" We're doing this because we want an alternative system for our kids. We want our kids to be able to watch stuff that instills the values we've had for generations that make society run. We want those instilled through excellent entertainment. I mean, our strength is the fact that there are about three million other people right now joining in with us and saying, "Let's do this. Let's just make this happen." That is our strength, and that's allowing us to just rewrite the rules. Our job as a business is to fix what’s broken in Hollywood because Hollywood hasn't been a good business. It’s not that they don't have enough revenues; they have plenty of revenues. There are more revenues coming into Hollywood than ever, and historically, media has been America's biggest export. It's movies. That's our number one export. But this industry, the Hollywood industry, has a cost problem, not a revenue problem. Our job is just to do a better job on all fronts. Angel’s job is, and I believe it is an excellent business and it’s growing right now. So, while you're growing, you're investing. Can you tell me what's going on? I guess, again, I'm a bit of an outsider. I'm a business guy, I'm a tech guy, but I'm not closely following Hollywood. Teach me a little bit about what's going on. What is A24 doing that you think is maybe smart or interesting, or what was their approach? Why do they seem to be having a different result? I've seen also like Thomas Tull, I feel like he came into the industry as kind of an outsider and did some interesting things. I guess tell me who you think has done cool or interesting work. Get me inspired; get the business nerd in me inspired by somebody who did something. A24 is really interesting because they've built a strong community. You have the AAA membership where you can sign up, and then they followed us and actually started giving tickets to people who are AAA members. I think the most interesting group probably is Crunchyroll. That's the anime. They have 20 million subscribers and they're just focused on their niche. Like we're focused on a values audience, they are focused on anime, which is a really, really big niche. I mean, 20 million is a massive number. What is that in revenue? I don't know what the revenue is because I don't know what the membership cost is. Even if it's 10 bucks a month, they're talking about... I think they're kind of the top of the tier-three streamers, the Crunchyroll. You have like MGM+ and you've got Starz and you got these groups that are in the tier-three. Tier-four is like Pure Flix, Daily Wire, that kind of a world. And then we've just recently moved into the tier-three streamers. And you have tier-two, which is your bigger streamers like Paramount+, and then you move into tier-one, which is Netflix, Disney+, and Apple. Apple might still be in tier-two, but I think Crunchyroll is super interesting. I went on an interview with Rick Rubin, the music producer, and I did a podcast with him. It was a really interesting podcast, and he told me, "I've been watching this industry for a very, very long time." He said, "I don't think I've honestly ever seen anything totally different until Angel." He's like, "Everybody else is some version of the previous stuff. You guys are different from top to bottom. You're not doing anything the same." I heard you have a personal vibe coder. Is that right? You have a guy who does vibe codes for you. Is that true? That's amazing. That might be the best I've ever seen. I basically got to the point where I would vibe code products and then I would get stuck because I'd get pulled outside to meetings, into fires, into different things as an executive in the company. But I love building out the prototypes of the products. I just asked, I was like, "I need a person who just can take it from there and just go to the next step." Eventually, once we get something like a little experiment that we've been thinking about to product-market fit—like an MVP, a minimum viable product—then we will turn it over to the product team, which can build something that will scale and get bigger. I kind of think that every executive of a kind of medium-to-large company should actually do this. It's normal to have an assistant, a chief of staff, or maybe somebody that helps you out on the personal side, but AI is so fast-moving and can do so much; the bottleneck is you. It's not the technology. It's your bandwidth, your time, your ability to keep the ball moving forward, to work through any obstacles, or keep playing with the latest and greatest. I actually think that what you're doing, having a personal kind of vibe coder with you, is the move. I think that's absolutely super helpful. I've been able to get things prototyped that I never would have imagined, because what I was doing before that was I would stay up late and I would get a plan made for Claude. I’d build out a plan and then I’d tell Claude, "I'll get as far as I can and say, 'Run this all night.'" Our team has built harnesses for every single person in the company. There's a brand guide harness for Angel that sits inside of our code systems so that you can take any model, whether it's Claude or GPT or Grok or whatever, and you hook it to the harness. Then that harness goes through and does all the checks and makes sure that you're not breaking the rules of the system. What I do is I end up vibe coding until I break something, and then the engineering team builds a new harness to fix what I broke. At night, I get to the end of the evening and I have a plan, and then I just launch the agents at that plan and just leave my computer running. I find myself even like in the Tesla, just having it self-driving, and then I'm watching it code while I'm driving because if I shut the laptop, it will stop. I have it hooked up to my hotspot on my phone, and then I'm just clicking continue, continue while I... Well, that's the worst. I set it up before I sleep, I wake up ready to see it, and it's like the very first thing is, "Can you allow me to open a browser?" It's like, "Yes, open the browser." Was it stuck on that all night? You just switch it on, bypass everything, and get dangerous with it. A couple of things you learn: one is these are machines; they're not humans. The veneer of it feeling like a human wears off really quickly, and you realize this is definitely a machine. But number two is they kind of blow your mind all the time just by what they're capable of doing. Similar to any type of engineering feat, you just go, "Wow, this is very impressive." What's something you had it build that was cool? I had it build a system where I can just test trailers and scenes of movies and run them like to where you can just send out links to friends and family. It AB tests like trailer A, trailer B, trailer C, and if you send the link, it will just randomly give them a different trailer. Then you just gather data and find out which ones are working. Oh, that's pretty cool. I like that. Yeah, that's one that I think is interesting. You can model out all of your attrition rates and you can model out your lifetime values so much faster now just using AI, as long as you have clean data. People say that they should be firing people, but I don't think they're just looking for excuses to fire people, because economics work like this. When you get something that makes everybody 10 times more effective, you're going to become so much more productive that you need more people. You don't say, "I want less people that are 10 times more effective." You say, "I want more people that are 10 times more effective." It's so stupid. You're multiplying two numbers together. Why would you want one to be smaller? The ones who are cutting off people, if they're truly cutting it off because AI is coming into play, that's a short-term play where you're just cutting costs, like you're almost like a company that's cutting costs right before an acquisition. You're not building long-term value the same way. If your company is getting to a point where, because of AI, you need to hire more people, that's when it's the right time to hire. Because this AI has made us so much more effective, we've broken down so many more walls that we need more people to service what we're doing and to build. That's what's going to be happening all over. I don't believe for a second that we're going to have a massive unemployment because of AI. It doesn't make sense on an economics level. If you were going back to talk to a room full of people like you—so, younger you—what message would you be trying to get across? Well, the first thing I'd say is that no matter what you do in the workplace, nothing will compare with getting married and having kids, having kids sooner than you think you should, and having more of them than you think you should. Nothing in the professional workplace will even compare. That's where my real success comes from: marriage and kids. And you can do both; I think that's critical. I think that there's no idea that's so precious to get in huge fights over. An example would be where we had some really big campaigns at the agency where clients said, "I'm not paying you," and you have all the success, they owe you a significant amount of money, and they don't want to pay you for what you did. They're threatening lawsuits. A mentor of mine, Steve Oscu, told me, he said, "Unless it's worth over $5 million, it's not even worth fighting a battle over, because you're going to end up fighting for 18 months to three years, you're going to drain the life out of yourself, and you may win, probably win, but then where did you get?" He's like, "But in that period of time, somebody who's innovative is going to make more value than that's worth." And not just that; it's just not a happy life. So after listening to him, I went back to my brother Neil, because we were thinking about whether or not we should sue for this amount that was owed to us. It was significant to us because we didn't have any money. Neil had been in the Bible, he flipped it open, and it landed on the verse that says, "If a man sues you for your cloak, give him your coat also." And so between my mentor saying what he said and then my brother seeing that, I was like, "Okay, let's just let it go." And letting that go changed the trajectory of my life. Just letting go of what was worth probably a couple million, a few million dollars, letting that go and just going after new ideas and having an abundance mentality allowed me to focus on the things that helped us build a big ad agency. If you're the golden goose, you're going to lay more eggs and you can be, and so you just keep pushing and don't take the idea that, "Oh, this will be my only thing." As soon as you switch gears and say there will be more things, you're going to get more things and you're going to have more opportunities and you'll break through. I love that, Jeff. Thank you for coming on. Thank you for sharing the wisdom. Thank you for sharing the stories. I appreciate you. Yeah. Thank you.

I Sat Down With ASML's CEO - Here's What He Told Me
