TL;DR

Driven by an AI boom requiring 16 times more transistors every two years, ASML CEO Christophe Fouquet and industry analyst Dr. Ian Cutress discussed deep tech collaboration, the evolution of Moore’s Law, and Europe's scaling and funding challenges at the Hello Tomorrow conference in Amsterdam.

ASML's Unconventional Origin Story & Ecosystem Strategy

  • Origins as a Spin-off: Spun out of Philips 40 years ago at a time when major semiconductor players like IBM pursued full vertical integration.
  • System Integration Model: Unlike Japanese competitors Nikon and Canon, ASML chose system integration rather than vertical integration, allowing them to partner with the world's best suppliers for every part of the tool.
  • Key Partnerships: Collaborates with Zeiss on optics (a 40-year partnership), VDL, Trumpf (for EUV), and Mistral (for AI).
  • Customer Co-Investment: During the development of EUV when R&D funding was tight, ASML had customers co-invest in the technology to help share the financial burden and accelerate scaling.

Redefining Moore's Law and the AI Infrastructure Boom

  • The Classical vs. AI Definition: While the traditional definition of Moore's Law is the number of transistors per chip doubling every 2 years, AI has put Moore's Law "on steroids."
  • AI Transistor Demand: Advanced AI products require a 16 times increase every 2 years across both logic and memory.
  • Density Drivers: Achieving required density demands scaling complemented by 3D integration (advanced packaging and stacking wafers/dies).
  • Market Visibility: AI now accounts for about half of total semiconductor production. The industry is currently catching up on a significant backlog with a visibility window of 2 to 3 years.

The Hard Reality of Deep Tech Innovation

  • Solving Real Problems: Fouquet emphasized that creativity must serve a concrete purpose—identifying the exact problem to solve and finding customers who deal with that problem daily.
  • The EUV Development Cycle: Theorized in the 1980s, EUV took roughly 20 years to develop, with the first 15 to 18 years consisting largely of a "succession of very painful mistakes" and resilience in solving source power issues.
  • Scaling Over Ideas: Having a great idea is not enough; survival requires operational execution, robust supply chains, and the ability to scale production from one prototype to hundreds of units shipped globally.

AI Infrastructure and the Mistral Partnership

  • Data as the Core Asset: Fouquet stated that the most important part of AI is data, noting that companies must generate unique data to feed powerful customized models rather than relying solely on internet-scale LLMs.
  • Mistral Partnership: ASML partnered with European AI startup Mistral because ASML lacks a DNA in model development despite generating terabytes of data. Mistral is helping build customized models for ASML's products, operations, and R&D.
  • Open Source Alignment: Mistral supports open source because they believe the real value of AI lies in downstream applications and customization.

Europe's Trillion-Dollar Company Challenge

  • European Strengths: Europe maintains strong education systems and well-funded research facilities, consistently producing great ideas and deep tech startups.
  • Scaling Bottlenecks: European startups struggle with access to capital when scaling up (hiring, securing space, purchasing equipment).
  • Regulatory Hurdles: While US and Chinese governments act as enabling bodies with supportive regulations, European governments and the European Commission often create regulatory drag. Fouquet noted that the EU Chips Act 2.0 has improved market focus, but the Commission still needs to trust the industry to lead itself.

Notable Quotes

"When you look at the most advanced chips, the most advanced product, AI product, you're not looking at a two times every 2 years, you're looking at a 16 time every 2 years. So, the industry, the AI industry needs a lot more transistor both for logic and memory than Moore's Law used to provide." — Christophe Fouquet
"We're a bit like that. EUV is a great story, of course, today. Everyone look at EUV as a you call it a miracle... But, it took us most probably 20 years to develop EUV. And maybe the first 15, maybe the first even 18 years were just a succession of very painful mistakes." — Christophe Fouquet
"The one mistake you can make as a company, especially as a successful company, is to believe that the recipe of your past success is the recipe of your future success." — Christophe Fouquet
So, it's not every day ASML gives you a phone call, but that's what happened to me a couple of months ago. I was minding my own business when I got an email that said, "Hey, Ian, you're an analyst. Can you host a fireside chat with our CEO at a conference for early stage investors and startup companies in Amsterdam?" I'm like, "Yeah, why not?" This is that talk. What's your minimum specification? Thank you all for coming. Thank you to Hello Tomorrow and ASML for inviting me. For those of you who don't know who I am, my name is Dr. Ian Cutress. I am an industry analyst. It's my job to look at all the silicon in play in the semiconductor space. My background is physical chemistry. I became a journalist for a little while, and today I now also run a YouTube channel called Tech Potato. My background has always been about programming silicon and the makeup of silicon architecture. I remember dealing with CUDA back when it was version 0.1. Those were terrible days, but it got me a thesis, so I'm quite happy. I spend a lot of my time speaking to companies like ASML, but also their clients in the foundry space, and all of the companies who build chips at those foundries. But we can't talk about chips these days without mentioning the little thing called AI. It's living, it's breathing, and I know a lot of you are working in there today. But the AI boom is real, not just in hardware, but in software, and we're coming up against a few new evolutions of the technology where it matters most. Sometimes, if you, for example, track the software space in this, it feels like it's moving so fast. I remember a few years ago we were talking about having convolutional neural networks, then transformers were the thing, and now it's all about agentic AI. I just came from a trade show in Taiwan last week called Computex, 150,000 people. Every question was about agentic AI. And then the other conversation was about the macroeconomics around memory, and of course we'd all like cheaper memory. But the thing is, the companies I deal with all have a hard problem. They're trying to integrate themselves into a very complex infrastructure, and realistically, it's the infrastructure that's helping build AI today. It's not just the chips, it's the installation, it's the use case, it's the software built on top, and the interconnects between them. We have thousands of companies working in each of those spaces, all of which—from big companies like the Intels, the Broadcoms, down to the startups—all have the same issue. How do we bring hard technology and hard science to the masses, and because everything's moving so fast, how do we make it relevant today, not in 3 years? I think a lot of you in this room are having those conversations right now. But how do we bring this deep technology into the market? Now, there's a lot about power and investment that also play into this. You can read about water usage of fabs and foundries as well. It's always a very interesting topic. But my guest today, Kristof from ASML, he is one of the first to showcase how they implemented this sort of deep hard science into their infrastructure. And today, at a conference a couple weeks ago, he was presenting how they're now using AI across their infrastructure to help optimize their production tools so that their foundry partners can maximize their revenue. If you've ever been to a semiconductor fab, you'll know these things have to operate at 80-90% utilization because every minute they're burning thousands and thousands of dollars just in opex to keep up and running. Now, I've spoken for long enough. I know Kristof's got a few things to say, so please let me welcome Kristof Fouquet from ASML. Good to see you, my friend. >> Thank you, Ian. >> Well, good afternoon, everyone. It's a great pleasure to join you today. I think you know that the industry ASML is in, semiconductor, which now is quite easily extended to AI, is living a very, very exciting time. It is a time where we seem to believe that the number of opportunities when it comes to innovation is going to grow exponentially because basically of what AI will enable in the next few years. So, I'm very happy to be here because, you know, what people forget also a bit about AI and about semiconductor is that in order for that dream to become true, to realize, we are going to need major, major innovations in energy, in material, in health care, in all the things some of you have been working on. All of you, I think, are trying very, very hard to come up with new ideas and new concepts to support basically this extraordinary dream. I'm also happy to be here because I understood that there are about 48 different countries represented. So, it's not only about the Netherlands, it's not only about Europe; it's much broader than that. And in ASML, we strongly believe that for innovation to reach everyone everywhere, it's extremely important to be able to collaborate and build some very strong ecosystems around the world. So, I had a chance to see these very diverse origins around the show. And I was very, very impressed by the amount of creativity, the amount of energy, and the passion of some of the people I've talked to around the topic of innovation. So, this is great. And you know, of course, like in any ecosystem, there are a few big, established companies: Nvidia, TSMC, ASML is one of them. But I think that we all know that without all of you, without more innovation, more creativity coming to life in the next few years, then maybe at some point the dream doesn't become fully reality. So, again, very happy to be here. I know that Ian has prepared a lot of questions. I will do my best to try to answer those. But again, it's a real pleasure to be here. Thank you very much. >> So, Kristof, I'll be honest. I'm kind of a bit of an ASML fanboy. Right? As an engineer, the stuff your company puts together feels like magic. And I know you've had the arguments internally about how much magic is involved in getting this stuff. But one of the overriding things when it comes to semiconductors over the past 30-plus years has been this concept of Moore's Law. Getting a lot of performance for free. And you're a company that's at the leading edge of building the latest generation transistors. When I speak to Intel, the home of the law, they say it's still alive. If I speak to Jensen Huang at Nvidia, he says it's dead. If I speak to Dr. Kevin Zhang, our friend over at TSMC, he said, "I don't care." So, where do you sit? >> Well, I think what you see through the different opinions people may have around Moore's Law is that there are a lot of different versions of Moore's Law, in fact. It means many, many things. The one I like the most is the law that says that every 2 years the number of transistors per chip should double. And that has driven the industry for many, many, many years. But to be honest, if you look at what's happening today with AI, Jensen's comments make sense; AI needs a lot more than that. So, when you look at the most advanced chips, the most advanced AI product, you're not looking at a two times increase every 2 years, you're looking at a 16 times increase every 2 years. So, the industry, the AI industry, needs a lot more transistors both for logic and memory than Moore's Law used to provide. This is why Jensen said, "Moore's Law is dead." Well, you could say also that Moore's Law has been put on steroids by AI because we need to do a lot more than that. So, what's really happened is, if anything, a huge acceleration of Moore's Law. A huge acceleration of the appetite for transistors and semiconductors. And, you know, this is a bit of what the industry is going through right now. And this is very exciting, of course, for all of us. >> I'm sure you saw the news last week. Huawei announced their reclassification of Moore's Law as "Tao scaling," to be this idea that it's not just the transistor, it's also the package, it's also the interconnect, it's also the system. Do you agree with that view? >> Well, I think if you go back to the very day when Dr. Moore presented Moore's Law as we know it, he already said that density would be achieved by scaling, so putting more transistors per unit of area, but also by packaging some of the transistors together. So, I think when you look at how you can achieve more density, scaling is obviously one way to do it, which is being complemented more and more by what we call in ASML "3D integration." That can be advanced packaging, but that can be also stacking wafers together or dies together. So, when you move so fast on density, the truth is you're going to need both. If you cannot scale, which is a bit what Huawei told us a few weeks ago, then you have to do more of the other things. You have to do more integration. So, you know, it's two axes, and the multiplication of the two axes gives you the density. And if you need to go 16 times more transistors every 2 years, you have to move very, very fast on both axes. >> Changing tack a little bit, ASML has a long history in the advancement of lithography. But the way the company was founded is a little bit different from most. I wonder if you can help explain that. >> Well, you know, this goes back 40 years ago. So, I was still a very young kid; I will tell you what I heard about it. But ASML is a spin-off from Philips. The spin-off happened at a time when the semiconductor industry was just happening. If you look 40 years ago, a few companies doing semiconductors—I think IBM is a good example—were looking for complete integration of everything. IBM, for example, was doing its own lithography machine and everything else. The industry started to grow, and basically Philips understood that maybe it was a good idea to provide lithography tools to the industry. The Japanese, Nikon and Canon, had the same idea. It started by saying, "Okay, let's specialize on this." And then, I think what was very important in the way ASML has grown—and this has been a theme for the last 40 years—was to always do that in collaboration with customers, because customers were the only people who could tell us what to do, what we had to do, and with suppliers. The second part is important because ASML, to the contrary of, for example, the Japanese companies, decided to go for system integration rather than vertical integration. By choosing system integration, we were capable of working with the very best partners for every single part of the tool. So, this is how we ended up working with Zeiss on the optics, and we've been together for 40 years. VDL, for example, is a good example, very well known in the Netherlands. More recently, Trumpf for EUV. More recently, Mistral when it comes to AI. Every time we say we're going to just go and work with the best. And the job of ASML will be to understand from the customer what to do and to do it by putting all of that together. This is really the philosophy of the company, a philosophy that is still going very strong today as we speak. >> It's interesting you bring up everybody trying to build their own lithography tool, because the way I also understand it is that at the time when ASML was formed, a lot of those competing companies also co-invested in ASML. How much of an impact do you think that had? >> Well, it was very important because like any company, I'm sure lots of the people in the room will recognize that, you can have the technology but sometimes you cannot scale. When we were developing EUV, the company was healthy because we had a good business on our DUV technology, but not wealthy enough in order to put the right amount of money in R&D. This is where co-investment became important. We told our customers, "You're going to need this product. We are going to do it. But if we do it with our own means, it will take too long." On the way, we also integrated a company like Cymer, which was critical to the success of EUV. It's very important when you have something critical to be able to scale, and I think one good way to do that is to make sure that the people who will use your technology contribute to that scaling. That's a pretty good model. >> With those collaborations, can you describe what goes into them when the roadmap for EUV is defined by decades and involves really hard science? For those of you who don't know the story of EUV, it was theorized in the '80s. People assumed it would be ready in the '90s, and it took another 20 years beyond that. So, can you go into what goes into those collaborations? >> Yeah, so to be able to do what you describe, you need to be willing to share information, plans, sometimes dreams you have for the next 5, 10, or 15 years. Without knowing, of course, exactly what's going to happen—because who knows what's really going to happen in 15 years from now—but to do that, you need to have a huge amount of trust with the people you are going to work with. So, you need to know that the people you share the information with are not going to use this information against you to create leverage, to create products they may sell for too much money, or to create a technology they will cut you off from. That's been one of the key elements of the success of ASML and the ability to look at the long term. It has to build this trust. That's always what we put first in the company. There are a lot of things we're willing to compromise, maybe lose sometimes, but the one thing we never want to lose is the trust of our customers and our partners, because if we lose that, we know the horizon is going to shrink dramatically. We won't be able to develop the next great product because as you said, those products have a 10-15 year lead time, which for a lot of people is very scary. But for us, it's what it takes to bring materials, technology, software, and algorithms together and get a product going. We're about to move our High-NA system into production at our customer's site. We started the program in 2012-13. >> It's great that as a company ASML is in that position where you offer something so highly differentiated that nobody else can realistically build it without all these collaborations. Does that not open yourselves up to entertaining a lot of bad ideas about how to solve some of these hard science problems? How do you determine what accelerates the project and what separates a brilliant idea from one that ultimately fails? >> I think, first, you have to really decide what you really need. It goes back to: what is the problem you're trying to solve? Just being creative for the purpose of being creative is not enough. You need to know what problem you're going to solve. That usually calls for a lot of innovation, creativity, and I would say a lot of mistakes. You have to be willing to learn by making mistakes, to learn by failing. I mean, EUV is a great story today. Everyone looks at EUV as a miracle. It took us most probably 20 years to develop EUV. Maybe the first 15, maybe the first 18 years were just a succession of very painful mistakes. For a long time, we couldn't find the solution to key problems, one of them being how to get enough power on the EUV source. The resilience of the engineers at ASML was such that we got some breakthroughs. If you knew the amount of bad designs, disappointments, and major disasters we had on the way, this was enormous. The people just kept going. You have to be willing very quickly to realize that maybe whatever you thought didn't work out, and you have to go to a completely different approach. This requires a lot of attention and focus. There's not too much space for random ideas; you need to stay focused on the problem you want to solve. >> Flip that a little bit. I know a lot of people in the room are on the other side of that. They're the ones building their ideas or investing in the deep technology that could be a solution for a bigger business. How much does that change when you're on the other side, and what advice would you give? >> I think it never changes. The key for everyone in the room is: what is the problem you are trying to solve? Question number two is: can you really identify people that deal with those problems every day? Someone that will welcome you to solve that problem for them. That's what later you will call customers. If you have a good idea of what the problem is and a very good idea of the people you want to solve it for, then start to work with those people and put all your energy on that problem. Anyone in this room at some point will have to do that, because if they don't, whatever great idea they had will go nowhere. >> Does the best idea always win? >> I think no, because the other element is scalability. There's always a point where you have to do things more than once. For example, we can make one EUV machine, but the challenge is to make hundreds of them, ship them all over the world, and make sure they run all the time. To do that, you have to operationalize what you do. You have to make sure the supply chain is there. People realize nowadays how important the supply chain is. I can tell you that most companies get in real trouble if they don't take care of their supply chain. There's a stage in the life of a company where the people who had the idea and made the prototype realize they also need to understand how operations work so they can scale the idea into a business. Operation is very, very important. >> Moving on to the word of the year, AI and machine learning. I've noticed over the last couple of months we're talking about it not just in pure software, but in physical and infrastructure challenges. How does that compare to the pre-AI era for the people working underneath it? >> For me, one thing to realize is that most probably today and tomorrow, the most important part of AI is not the model; it's the data. The reason why AI came to life a few years ago is because people developed some models, but they only could do that because there was a huge amount of data collected for many years that became available. When you think about AI, especially as a startup, the first thing to think about is: what data do you generate that is unique to yourself? You could use it to feed some of those powerful algorithms in order to develop things faster, improve product performance, or boost everything you do today. You need to develop customized AI models that will be more adapted to what you do. I think the transition to agentic AI or physical models is a huge value. I believe the biggest value of AI for the industry will be the ability to accelerate innovation and improve performance in many places—semiconductor, health care, education, energy, you name it. >> Recently you announced a partnership with Mistral. Mistral is known for having open models, and you just spoke about the importance of having a data set specific to the industry. How is ASML integrating those tools, and what exactly is it improving? >> I talked about our model of integrating the best possible partners. Mistral fits that model very well. ASML is not an AI company. We have engineers who understand litho, optics, and mechanics, but we don't have a DNA in developing models. We don't even have a DNA in understanding exactly how to make the best possible use of our data, even though we generate terabytes of data per minute. The idea of the partnership with Mistral was to bring in a company willing to work with us deeply to use our data, help us create an architecture, and develop customized models for our products and operations. Mistral supports open source because they believe the real value of AI is the application you drive out of the model. >> Did the fact that Mistral is European factor into that decision? >> Well, it's a question we get a lot. We like that they are European, but as I've said many times, the project was a real industrial project. We looked at many potential partners in Europe and outside Europe, and we picked the group of people that we thought was the best fit to work with us. It was a real industrial decision. >> How important are startups to ASML? >> The key question is: if you're going to work with ASML or any big company, how can they help you? There's the funding part, which in Europe is still a challenge. You need to want more than that. In ASML, we look for real opportunities to engage with startups, but we only do that if we believe the competencies we have—engineering, operations, etc.—can help. Our experience is that if we can help a few companies skip a few of those mistakes, that's a benefit. For a startup, the real question is how that company can help you, because a big company could also kill you if they aren't a good fit. >> Do you think that philosophy is similar across other large companies? >> I think it should be. Sometimes people stop at the investment itself, which is especially true in Europe because everyone here must be struggling to get access to capital. We need to make that easier. But once the money is there, you have all the rest, and all the rest is as important to make the success of a company. >> Europe is good at deep tech, but we're not so good at developing deep industry. Everyone keeps asking, "When's the trillion-dollar European company going to come?" What does Europe need to do to capture more value, and does the new EU Chips Act help? >> That's a good question. To keep the good things we have: education in Europe is great, we have well-funded research facilities, and we have many people with great ideas. That's the starting point. Now, this has to be scaled. To scale, you have to hire, find space, and buy machines. You need access to capital. The environment should help you do that rather than slow you down. Access to capital and having government act as an enabling body are very important. One of the big differences between the US, China, and Europe is that the US and China act as enabling bodies; they make sure regulations allow the industry to flourish. Europe is the only place where we seem to believe that the goal of government is to do the exact opposite. It's a very difficult change. Leaders agree with that, but the challenge is to go from what we have built to what we need for deep-tech startups to flourish. You are going to end up competing with similar companies from the US and China. That's something everyone in this room has to be prepared to do. >> Is it getting better? >> I think it's getting better in terms of understanding, and a bit of that is out of urgency. That's a bit of a challenge with Europe. You need a war in Ukraine to realize you need an army; you need your startups to move to the United States to understand you have a problem. The realization is here, but the speed of change is still a challenge. Chips Act 2.0 has seen improvements, with a wider focus on the ecosystem. The part that is not good enough is the willingness of the commission to be involved in all those products; we need to convince them that the industry should lead the industry, not the commission. >> We have a macroeconomic environment where memory is sold out through 2028, all driven by machine learning. How have you changed working with your supply chain? >> The changes happened in about 6 months. 6 months ago, we were looking at a healthy but flattish market. In the last 6 months, everyone started to realize that this AI opportunity is going to translate into a major build-up of infrastructure that will take 3, 4, or 5 years. People were doubtful, and then this flipped. The whole industry is trying to catch up on a significant backlog. >> How much visibility do you have into the future? >> I think we have about a 2-3 year view, because that's what our customers can see. After that, we try to model what demand could be, but AI has changed it dramatically. It used to be mobile phones and PCs, and now AI is taking about half of the production of semiconductors. >> One of the consistent narratives for big companies is that they have to "bet the company" every generation to survive. What is your take on that? >> The one mistake you can make as a successful company is to believe that the recipe of your past success is the recipe for your future success. If most companies don't jump on AI, they may fall behind dramatically. You always have to look at what's coming next and prepare for that, even if what you need for the next 10 years is very different from the last 10. In our industry, we're lucky because the tools we produced 20 years ago are still used today, but we look at new opportunities like 3D integration. >> To wrap up, I have a surprise. The machine that your company makes, somewhere around 350-400 million euros, fires liquid tin 50,000 times a second using multiple 20 kilowatt lasers. It goes through optics where the mirror error is 20 picometers. This is your partners at Zeiss. I have a little gift for you. I took a photo of that machine and commissioned a 3D cartoony design. I have a pair of socks with the EUV machine on them. >> My wife is going to tell me, "What the hell have you done?" >> There you go. There's how complex your machines are. Thank you, Kristof, and thank you all for attending. >> Thank you. >> That interaction was a little bit surreal. Kristof really liked the socks. We're going to leave a link to them in the Tech Potato store. The goal of the talk was to be educational for the audience of startups and investors. There are a few more like this coming up. If you want to see more, leave a comment down below.

John Armitage: What Makes a Company Worth Investing In? | Podcast | In Good Company

Norges Bank Investment Management(132.0K)·54:22·16.6K views·2026-10-07
John ArmitageInvestor, Egerton Capital Nicolai TangenCEO, Norges Bank Investment Management
TL;DRJohn Armitage, co-founder of Egerton Capital, outlines his 30-year investment philosophy emphasizing pricing power, rigorous qualitative and numerical modeling, realistic management teams, and downside valuation protection.
Hosted by Nicolai Tangen, CEO of Norges Bank Investment Management, the discussion covers stock selection, the risks of AI and over-information, and the realities of running a long/short equity firm. - Moats Versus Management: Moats are critical but insufficient. A company with...

TL;DR

John Armitage, co-founder of Egerton Capital, outlines his 30-year investment philosophy emphasizing pricing power, rigorous qualitative and numerical modeling, realistic management teams, and downside valuation protection. Hosted by Nicolai Tangen, CEO of Norges Bank Investment Management, the discussion covers stock selection, the risks of AI and over-information, and the realities of running a long/short equity firm.

Defining a Great Company & Investment

  • Moats Versus Management: Moats are critical but insufficient. A company with a moat will fail under poor management (e.g., Boeing under its previous CEO, or Rolls-Royce before its new chief executive).
  • Capital Allocation & Culture: Culture and capital allocation drive long-term value. Berkshire Hathaway transformed from a closed New England textile mill into a powerhouse through capital allocation, while Intel lost its way due to a culture that did not dare to innovate.
  • Filtering New Ideas: Initial idea assessment requires time-value and understandability. Armitage avoids fairly priced businesses to save time and avoids complex industries where management themselves cannot predict outcomes (citing a drug company CFO who treated phase 3 clinical trials like "a series of options").
  • Great Companies vs. Great Investments: Historical greatness does not guarantee future outperformance. Coca-Cola remains a great company with no substitute, but it likely underperformed the market over the trailing 25 years due to a lack of successful new product expansion ("new Coke" failed).

Pricing Power, Margins & Industry Views

  • Definition and Necessity: Pricing power is the ability to raise prices despite competition. It is essential because all companies face cost inflation driven by lower trade barriers and technology-enabled competition. Sustaining pricing power structurally drives margin growth.
  • Cyclicality and Style: The firm invests across cycles without a rigid style definition. Armitage prefers businesses where downside valuation is protected, the market could close without causing worry, and management is realistic rather than overly optimistic or reliant on aggressive accounting.
  • Sectors Avoided: Drug companies are generally avoided. Over a 30-year career, investments have spanned Russian metals, oil and gas, oil services, technology, and retail, but certain industries are avoided today due to high technology-driven competition.
  • Worldview: Macro views need not be overly sophisticated. Armitage views current markets as high, US bond yields and oil prices up, and two wars ongoing, noting that the global economy relies heavily on AI-driven growth and strong US corporate earnings.

Financial Modeling & Analysis

  • The Art of Modeling: Models are for juniors, but fine-art modeling requires focus on key factors. Too much detail leads to "garbage in, garbage out" where minor assumption changes break the model.
  • Private Data and Inference: Many company metrics are private and require detective work.
    • *Airbus (major holding):* Modeled by evaluating units sold times contractually private prices, incremental unit margins, order backlog revenue timing, lumpy R&D costs, and FX.
    • *Reinsurance:* Contracts are private; analysts must evaluate historical reserve development to assess how past losses were estimated.
    • *Nvidia:* Modeled by piecing together bill of materials composition and the impact of memory price inflation on 75% gross margins.
    • *Visa:* A simpler model with clear costs, 67% margins, and less relevant mix effects.
  • Profit & Loss and Balance Sheets: Incremental margins reveal potential outperformance. High incremental margins combined with high starting margins generate major profit beats during sales overshoots. Balance sheet health requires tracking inventory changes, trade terms (payables/receivables), and artificial cash-boosting tactics like factoring receivables.

Evaluating Management & Red Flags

  • Assessing Management: Look for executives who admit mistakes. Michael O'Leary (CEO of Ryanair) is praised for his intense focus on detail, self-awareness, and relentless execution despite treating operations as a series of fixes. In contrast, Pat Gelsinger at Intel was criticized for adding back stock-based compensation to earnings.
  • Red Flags: Consistently missing expectations and unearned optimism. Armitage avoids management teams that present a constantly rosy picture and cannot admit when things go wrong.
  • Case Study (Altis): Selling without margin for error. Altis was a French cable/telecom company utilizing leverage and cost-cutting with flat top-line growth. Armitage sold the position after a slight quarterly miss because a stagnant top-line leaves no room for operational mistakes.
  • Paralysis by Analysis: Information overload increases the risk of over-analyzing. Proliferating global data and analyst commentary risk creating psychological imprinting and decision paralysis.

Valuation & Portfolio Construction

  • Valuation Metrics: Conventional metrics are mandatory; EV-to-EBITDA is dismissed. Armitage relies on P/E ratios, free cash flow multiples, and EV-to-EBIT. He rejects EV-to-EBITDA for capital-intensive or serial acquirers because depreciation and amortization are real costs required to replace capital.
  • Multiples Threshold: Growth-adjusted P/E tolerance. Buying a fast-growing company with double-digit growth at roughly 30 times next year's earnings is considered attractive in the current environment, whereas 35 times earnings (like GE) feels rich.
  • Permanent Capital Rejection: Lockups and permanent capital are potential traps. Armitage rejects permanent capital because it removes the operational tension and discipline required to grapple with proper investment decisions, risking complacency during drawdowns.
  • Portfolio Construction: Concentration carries catastrophic risk. Egerton holds roughly 40 stocks, with top positions sized at 6% to 8%. Armitage rejects extreme portfolio concentration, arguing that while it rewards success, failure leads to disaster. The firm maintains thematic diversification and avoids financial leverage.

AI and Market Efficiency

  • Impact of AI: AI provides instant knowledge access but threatens cognitive ability. AI serves as a tool for knowledge synthesis, but Armitage warns that over-reliance causes mental arithmetic skills to atrophy ("your brain is going to rot").
  • Herd Effects and Alpha: Alpha is harder to capture as markets grow more efficient. AI risks creating a single source of truth and herd behavior, though potential herd effects may create opportunities to trade against the crowd.

Notable Quotes

"If you use AI too much, your brain is going to rot. And it's quite simple. People who don't do mental arithmetic lose the ability to do it... We want AI to help us and to short-circuit things, but we don't want it to think for us." — John Armitage
"Managements need to admit their mistakes. One of the most outstanding executives in the world is Michael O'Leary... sometimes you'd think he hadn't created the world's most profitable airline, but he just made a series of mistakes." — John Armitage
"I hate with a passion EV to EBITDAR valuations. I think they're meaningless. Because depreciation and amortization is a cost. Why should you add that back? It's a real cost." — John Armitage

So, we want AI to help us and to short circuit things, but we don't want it to think for us. Hi everybody. John Armitage has been the most important person for my professional development. I traveled with him for several years when he was my largest client back when I was an analyst and then I worked for him for 5 years at Egerton Capital. Nobody has taught me as much about investing in markets as John has. So John, it's an honor and long overdue to have you here. It's going to be kind of a master class: how to look at a company, how to build a position, and how to run a firm for 30 years without losing your edge. John, welcome. Thank you very much, and it's a great honor to be here. Let's start with the basics. What is a good company? Well, you know, I think first of all, obviously, there's a difference between a good company and a good investment. And second—and I would say look—many things go into something being a good company and today a lot of people talk about moats. Moats are critically important but at the same time if you have a moat without the right management you get destroyed and look at Boeing under its previous CEO. Look at Rolls-Royce before the brilliant new chief executive who's running it. Rolls-Royce had a moat but somehow, as he put it, it was a burning platform. I would also say that if you think about the theoretical value of a company, it's the value of the future cash flows. Therefore, how those future cash flows are invested is important. So, one of the things that's important is capital allocation. And if you think what Berkshire Hathaway originally was, it was a New England textile mill which got closed and look what it turned into. And that's because of capital allocation and culture. And I think culture is critically important in a company. Look at the way Intel lost its way for such a long period and that was because they didn't dare to innovate. They didn't dare to enter new fields and there was a business with incredible market shares getting eroded. So I think culture, capital allocation and moats are all very important. What is the first thing you look at? So here I give you, okay this is a company, this is a new idea. What is kind of the first thing you look at? Well, the first thing I look at is: is it worth the time? How do you decide? Well, many businesses are fairly priced. There's no point in spending a lot of time on something which is fairly priced. What is the point? And the second thing I think about is, you know, can I understand it? Will I understand it? I once had a very interesting meeting with the CFO of a drug company and I asked him how they were able to assess whether a phase 3 clinical trial would be successful or not. And he said, "Look, we can't assess it. We just think of them as a series of options." And I sort of thought to myself, well look, if you're the CFO of one of the largest drug companies in the world and you don't know whether a phase three trial is going to be successful, how the hell am I going to? What are the types of companies you can understand? Well, we try to understand as many types of companies as is possible and we look to—I'm not a physicist, I can't write code—but I'd like to try and feel that I can understand why Nvidia might be successful and what's very important for us is to understand the inherent operating leverage in a business, by which I mean what is it that will make it massively out or underperform expectations. And if you can't grasp that, I think you're on a hiding to nothing. How many great companies are there? Probably more than you think. And we own some of them. And there's a lot we don't own and haven't owned. But the nature of great companies changes over time. Look, Coca-Cola is a great company and there isn't a substitute for Coke, but the fact is it's probably underperformed the market in the last 25 years. And why is that? Because they haven't come up with the new Coke. Well, when they did, it didn't quite work, did it? That's true. One of the things that you taught me was the importance of pricing power. Why is pricing power important? What is it, first of all? Well, pricing power is the ability to have a product whose price you can raise despite competition. And why is it important? Because all companies face cost inflation. Because the world is more competitive for companies today than it was before. Because trade barriers are lower. Because technology is something which opens up competition. And if you can sustain your pricing power, it is a sign that you have something truly marvelous about your business. Because if you can price more than your cost inflation, you're going to structurally achieve margin growth. How does cyclicality come into play here? Well, we are not a style-defined firm. In some ways I wish we were because I think if you're style-defined, you probably have greater staying power than if you're not—i.e., clients like investing in defined styles. So we do invest in cycles and it's very important to get those right because if you invest with a cyclical company it's often you often feel the best before a downturn. So cycles are important, particularly if you're like us and you try to sort of traffic in whatever is doing well. Are you sure you haven't got a style at the firm? I mean, I think we have a style which is to do with investing in—I like to define it. I like to invest in a company where if the stock market closed I wouldn't feel worried. I like to invest—that is some sort of style—and I like to invest in a company where I feel I have valuation protection on the downside. I like to invest with management which are realistic and I hate to invest with management which use accounting policies to make their results seem better than they in practice are. And I hate to invest with management where you feel they have a sort of—where life is always rosy. What are the types of industries you would never touch? We're not very big on drugs and I guess to define it otherwise—in my time I've invested in most things ranging from a metals company located in the forbidden zone of Russia to oil and gas to oil services to technology to retail. But I think today some industries are more exposed to competition than others. And that's because of the impact of technology. Do you think you have to have a world view to be a good investor? I think you have to have a view about economic conditions, but I don't think it has to be more sophisticated than: is this a time when companies are swimming upstream against the current as opposed to downstream with the current? Unless you are trying to be a macro investor and that's not what we can do and it's not what we would do and I would leave that to geniuses like Stanley Druckenmiller. What is your world view now? My world view is, look, markets are high, interest/bond yields are high in the US. We've got two wars, the oil price is up and the markets are odd. The markets are high. It's strange. How do you read it? How I read it is that if we didn't have the stimulus of AI-driven growth and if we didn't have strong earnings in the US, the world would be in a different place. Now, you didn't study finance, you studied modern history. How does that shape your way of looking at the world, do you think? Well, I don't think it necessarily shapes my way of looking at companies, although I do think history is about investigating things. And you have quite a sort of good training at doing that if you do it properly. I happen to think that I've never found it easy to tell whether a good investor benefited or not from reading business studies. You know, I know you have an MBA from Wharton. No, undergrad, but I know plenty of people who have MBAs and I know plenty of people who read art subjects and I can't—you wouldn't know it from the way they invest. Do you hire MBAs? Yes, but I never really look at it. We have hired MBAs. My father when I was young told me that universities were about education rather than being a technical school and you should focus on that and that's what I believe in. So now you have a company here so you are into modeling, right? I mean you believe in, you're into modeling and I think although there's a lot of very good work from the sell side concentrated in individual firms and a lot of very good work, I think you know too many of the sort of leading analysts. The models are for juniors and I think there is a fine art to modeling which is about the right sort of detail but not too much of it. Tell me about it. Well, you know, if you have too much detail, garbage in, garbage out. Too much detail, minor changes of assumptions, everything goes haywire. You know, when you're forecasting how a company will do, you need to be able to isolate what are the absolute key factors and focus on them. Okay, let's apply a qualitative lens to that. Let's model a company. How do you—what goes into the top line? Well, I mean, if we talk about a company like Airbus, which we have a big holding in there, it's very simple, isn't it? It's the number of planes you sell times the price. You don't know what the price is 'cause it's all contractual. So the way you have to think about Airbus is: what might the incremental margins be on each unit? How will those develop? When will pricing trends in the order backlog manifest themselves in revenues? What about the big lumpy cost items that they can forecast like R&D and that they do forecast? And how will FX work? And the answer is with a company like Airbus, a lot of the information is simply private. You have no way of knowing what the price on an order is. So how do you model it? You try to piece together inferences from what they've told you. If you take a reinsurance company, and we had big holdings in reinsurance companies, reinsurance contracts are private between the insurer and the reinsurer. You have absolutely no evidence about a contract and about the level at which losses kick in and about how the losses are divided. And again, you sort of have to take their word on it. And then you have to look at how their reserves have developed over time because how their reserves have developed over time tells you how they've estimated losses in the past. And one of the things about insurance is that good companies tend to do better and bad companies tend to do worse. So you know, in general, much about companies is private and you have to use inferences from companies to apply them to numerical output. Look, if you take Nvidia, one of the big issues about Nvidia is memory cost inflation and what it does to their bill of materials because Nvidia makes 75% gross margins. Now they've, as it happens, just guided to gross margins in the near term, but you kind of have to piece together what the composition of their bill of materials is, how memory price contracts have worked, when they will impact, and then try to work out the sensitivity of what a further spike in memory cost inflation would do to them. There is a lot in companies which is very private, but then there are some companies like we happen to own Visa, where they make 67% margins; you kind of know roughly what drives the top line and the costs are pretty clear and you know mix effects are a lot less relevant. So, I'd say modeling a company, the skills involved range from being quite numerical to applying a sort of detective overlay to what you're told and drawing inferences from what you're told and thinking about it and stress testing it as much as you can. One of the points at the profit and loss statement which you taught me to focus a lot on was the margin at the margin, the incremental margins. Why is that so important? Because if companies have sales overshoots or undershoots, a very high incremental margin relative to a lower stated margin can cause a big profit beat or a big profit loss. So, should it be high or low? Well, you want high incremental margins, but you also want to start with high margins. Depends what you're shooting for. If you think you found a recovery story which no one else has found, you want massively high incremental margins because no one really factors in the benefits of what a really good incremental margin can be. People don't really dare to do that. So that's why Nvidia is so good because they sell a few things and bang, profits. But Nvidia has a very high margin to start with. What are the other things you particularly look at down the profit and loss account? I look at accounting policies. I look at depreciation rates. But you know, once you've got to the EBIT profit—the operating profit—everything else is pretty simple. What about cash flow? Very important. You know, what's the point of profitless prosperity? What about balance sheets? Very important. And someone I know who's a finance director once said to me that accrual accounting, which is the way standard profit and loss accounting works, is a balance sheet output, and I think that's correct. And so you can't disaggregate balance sheet moves from P&L moves and you have to focus on them. So here is a balance sheet. What do you look at? You look at what's happened to the inventories because that will impact revenues they've recognized and costs they've put on the balance sheet. You look at their terms of trade, how their payables are doing, how their receivables are, and you look at whether the cash has gone up and then you look at artificial measures they could have taken to boost cash like factoring receivables. You look at all kinds of things. Now you talked about clients. One of the things you taught me was that it's important to have many clients so that one client is not too important and then that your sales—you shouldn't sell everything in one month. For instance, running a ski lift is not a very good idea because you don't control the snow and—depends whether it's a monopoly or not. Do you like monopolies and market power? How do you look at these things? Well, I think monopolies are generally good if they've been honestly produced, you know, and I think market power is a very nice thing to have if it's been honestly achieved. You talked about management and you mentioned some which were outstanding and really important. Just how do you judge management? Well, one of the things I pay a lot of attention to is: do managements admit their mistakes? One of the most outstanding executives in the world is Michael O'Leary. If you listen to Michael O'Leary, sometimes you'd think he hadn't created the world's most profitable airline, but just made a series of mistakes. And here is someone with an incredible focus on detail. He's quite relentless, also very funny. He has a team of really super people around him and he's highly aware of what he does right and wrong. When Pat Gelsinger came into Intel, one of the first things he did was change their accounting so that stock-based comp was added back to earnings. And you kind of think, "Like really, what does that tell you?" Stock-based comp is not part of compensation? It's not a cost? Like really, is that realism or not? I look for management which is realistic and grounded and can see that things aren't always rosy. What are the red flags? Consistently missing expectations. And that feeling that someone is always presenting a picture which is slightly biased to be positive. That feeling that people don't like or can't admit something might be wrong. But isn't that also an intuitive thing? Yes. But isn't that also a geographical thing about it? I mean, not always. One of the best sales I ever did was of a company called Altice and that was run by a very, very compelling person who was brilliant at cutting costs. It was a French cable company and then it became a telecom company. They used a lot of leverage and the model really worked. The model was not much growth in the top line, but a lot of cost cuts, a lot of leveraged acquisitions. I remember there was one quarter when the numbers came in just slightly light, and I'm always quite suspicious when companies don't have much top-line growth because I think if they don't have much top-line growth, what's the room for a mistake? I remember I just didn't really like the fact there was a very slight miss. It had nothing to do with the medium-term numbers the way we modeled them, but I just decided we should sell it. And so we did. And you know, I was just a bit suspicious of the model. What did you base the decision on? I based the decision on the fact that I thought the business model didn't have much room for something to go wrong. And that's very important. What's the benefit of a high margin? The benefit of a high margin is that if you have a sales shortfall, your P&L isn't shot to pieces. Do you think there's a risk of getting too close to managements? Yes, I do. And my partner Ralph calls it "paralysis by analysis." I think it's a bigger risk today than it used to be because today information has proliferated around the world. Think of the number of analysts who write on a stock. Anytime any company executive speaks anywhere in the world, there will be people listening to him and reacting to it. So you get deluged by information. One of the difficult things about investing today is on the one hand you always have to cover the bases. It's a bit like panning for gold; you just never know. And you have to cover the bases. You have to listen and be aware because there might be something valuable in it. And also because other people are, and if you're not and someone else is and they decide something's going wrong, you're screwed if you haven't realized. So one of the big differences from when I was young is you have to absorb so much more. The problem with absorbing so much more is that it gets imprinted on your brain and it can be difficult to get away from it. And so we do sometimes suffer from this phenomenon of paralysis by analysis. How important is valuation? Well, valuation is really important to me and it's probably been too important in my career because I've probably missed lots of great growth stocks. But I like owning things where there is some valuation support on a conventional basis. And I hate with a passion EV to EBITDA valuations. I think they're meaningless. When you say conventional, what are the types of matrices you look at? PEs, free cash flow multiples, EV to EBIT maybe, but EV to EBITDA—you get these neoca-ds today that are valued on EBITDA and the biggest cost they have is depreciation. Yeah, that really makes sense. Tell me about PE—what's high, what's low. Well, when I was young, 20 times was high. I guess now it depends on the growth rate. I tend to think of—if I can buy a fast-growing company on a multiple next year of... PE, by the way, is a price divided by earnings. So, it's kind of how many years it would take for a company to make its own share price in earnings. I have less comfort than some people for high earnings multiples. And someone I have a huge amount of respect for, who I think's got an incredibly powerful intellect, thinks GE is cheap valuation. To me, it's a bit rich, 35 times earnings. I feel like if you can buy a good company with decent double-digit growth near 30 times next year, that's attractive in today's environment. Some people start by spending a lot of time on valuation and then end up spending less time on it, like Warren Buffett, for instance, more into high-quality stocks than they were earlier on. Have you had that? I feel the issue with that is that you can get too attached to the quality and not notice something's underperforming, and he has the luxury of permanent capital and we have weekly redemptions. Would you have thought differently about it if you had permanent capital? I've never thought about permanent capital. I don't particularly want it. I think the risk with permanent capital and lockups is that they're a trap. The risk is you say to yourself, "Well, I've had a really bad six months. This stock's collapsed, but I really like it. It'll work itself out over the two- or three-year period. My investors have got to stick with me." And then you realize it won't. And you allow permanent capital to be a luxury which removes the tension of needing to grapple with proper investment decisions. It can make—I worry it would make me relax and be complacent. Well, because you can't be complacent in the stock market. I think it takes more than that to get you to relax, John. But you were dismissive of EV/EBITDA. Yes. Which is a different way of looking at valuations. Just why are you dismissive about that? Because depreciation and amortization is a cost. Why should you add that back? It's a real cost. Companies need to replace their capital. And then you get serial acquirers which always have some amortization. And quite often serial acquirers put more costs into intangible assets than they should, and the amortization of those costs is added back to EBIT. Why would you do that? Let's move on to good ideas. Where do good ideas come from? Well, they come from all the people around me and they come from—look, the stock markets yield lots of profits for people. Therefore, there are lots of people who are incentivized to give you good ideas and I think you have to be eclectic about where you get those ideas from. I have a lot of very clever partners. I read lots of research. I troll through what is produced by the major investment banks to decide whether I think something looks interesting, and ideas sort of dawn on me over time. Do you find good ideas from the sell side? Yeah, often, and sometimes very good, and in pockets some outstanding research. And who are the investors you talk to? I'm not one of these people who's always on the phone and I'm not a particular networker, but there are some people I deeply admire and like catching up with a few times a year. I think Chris Hohn has an amazingly powerful intellect. I really like some friends in New York like my friends at Lone Pine, like Marc Gonka at Sergey Cap, like Eric Mandelblatt at Soroban, and the world is full of clever people, but I'm not permanently on the phone. I feel like the New York investment community is quite incestuous and they talk to each other all the time, and I'm not really like that. I would be a bit embarrassed ringing people up to ask them what they thought about things. So from that point of view, is London a bit of a backyard? The difficulty with being in London is that if the important things happen in America, there's a risk you are late, and sometimes it's important to follow the fashion, and sometimes it's important to be where the dominant economy is. Often it is. It would have been in 2007-08 and we probably would have done better if we had been there this year. But equally, you can get too close to things and have not enough detachment, and you can be in too small a world. Was London the center of the universe when you started? Yes, it was, but it is not now. That's a different discussion. Now you are not employing data scientists. Tell me about it. I've never wanted to invest in companies where I was reliant on the latest channel check. In any case, channel check results are a function of something else which is more powerful, which is the business itself. I'd rather try and understand that. I'm a skeptic about opinion polls. I know that data scientists do more than look at opinion polls, but I'd rather not invest in something where, "Oh gosh, the latest credit card data shows the following." That's one thing. But the world has moved on, and we do have some data scientists, technologists today who are helping us get to grips with AI and AI as a tool for our investment process. How does AI change the art of investment for the owners? I think AI changes a lot. I'm not sure whether it's a good thing, but I think it changes a lot. At the moment, what does AI enable you to do? Well, if you're in the business that we're in, as opposed to the Jane Street Capital business or probably the Citadel and Millennium business, if you're in the business that we're in, what does AI give you? It gives you instant access to knowledge which can be formatted any way you want. So you get conclusions. That's what it can give you. I do think there are two risks with that. One is that if too many people ask the same questions, are they going to get the same answers? No one's really sure. Is it going to turn out there's a single source of truth? The second thing I worry about personally and for all our people is that if you use AI too much, your brain is going to rot. It's quite simple. People who don't do mental arithmetic lose the ability to do it. So we want AI to help us and to short-circuit things, but we don't want it to think for us. Is it going to make markets more efficient? I think markets are infinitely more efficient than they were when I was young, and clearly AI will speed things up. I don't think anyone knows the extent to which large language models train on search results. Google will surface answers based on the questions that have been asked. If more people ask a question on Google, it's more likely to surface the same answer to me, based on the nuance that it's based on my knowledge. Is that true of AI? Will it produce a herd effect? I don't think anyone knows. If it produces a herd effect, then maybe you'll have an opportunity to get away from the herd. Do you think alpha is a thing of the past? I think it's definitely much harder. And what are the implications for people like you and some of your peers? Well, less good. How do you get alpha today? You can get alpha today by being highly concentrated and you can get alpha today both in single stocks and thematically. I think very few people are capable of doing that. Well, I really do think that, and the professional buyers of investment products, they love concentrated portfolios. Why? Because they run lots of funds, so they like concentration. I don't think they really understand that concentration is just marvelous when it goes right, but when it goes wrong, boy, it's a disaster. So I don't want to go the route of just putting everything on red. I don't know, we just have to keep doing what we do and see whether we can do it effectively. In this world where there is more momentum and AI, how do you keep this sense of intrinsic value and stick to your guns? Numbers are numbers, and earlier this year—well, we're having a pretty poor year this year—there was a time when I really felt like, "My god, maybe I should jump off a cliff." I came to realize that we'd missed a humongous great trade. What was important for us was: did we think the numbers that underpinned our investments were valid? And if they were valid, would we make money? So we stuck to that, and that was bloody lucky. Because if we'd chased the crowd at the time I was most depressed, it would have been a disaster. What kind of situation are you talking about now? Well, there was a time when you couldn't give away Visa, and luckily it's up 20% since then. That's true of other stocks when many of the real high-flyers dropped a lot. You have to be true to yourself and work out: are the numbers, the valuation numbers that underpin your position, are they valid? Will they work out? If they do, is the position attractive? And you have to just stick to that. You've been through most types of market situations. In what type of markets are you doing the best? Trending markets and trending stocks. We don't like—we're very bad at violent movements. Violent movements tend to be emotional. They tend to often presage a big change in the environment. They tend to be caused by geopolitics or big economic shifts. If you're stock pickers like we are, or like we try to be, what resonates with you are the numbers in your spreadsheet. What resonates with you is the conversation you had with that company a few weeks ago. You think to yourself, "It can't have changed that much." And so you can be far too late to realize there's a change. When I think back to 2008, I think we were handicapped by sitting in London, because when people told me that US house prices had gone up a lot and they compounded at 7% per annum, I thought, "Well, that's quite low," and I just didn't have a clue about how the linkages would work into the broader economy. And I think that is the danger of being a stock picker. You can be too focused on the individual investment and not really realize that it's part of a jigsaw in the economy. If you take one of the legs of the stool away, it can fall to the floor. When you have these volatile markets, how do you cope with stress? Presently, I wake up every morning wondering whether I'll ever be successful again. And that's the truth. My wife and I have this discussion: "God, is it all over?" I walk around the office feeling a bit black. And then I try and count my blessings, because I've lived in a great time. When my father's generation were young—not that I'm young now—life was all a bit worse. When I did my master's degree in social psychology, I interviewed you to talk about pattern recognition. When do you use pattern recognition and when do you use analysis? When we don't understand something which has, on the face of it, had a minor impact on earnings, we are reasonably quick to pull the trigger. We had a long-term holding in a company called Fiserv and it was a very good investment for us. They had some results which weren't quite what we thought they were. We didn't understand it and we sold the position, and subsequently it was one of the names which did go down a lot. It was the same with Altice. I tend to—I like confirmation of a thesis rather than contradiction. So when people do a bit better, do you buy often? You have an incredible ability to not think about where the share price was. Well, you have to, because nearly every good investment you ever make has gone up a huge amount. The stock doesn't know you're trading in it. How do you build a position then? We start small and we try and do a lot of work. How quickly do you put on a position? Well, it takes time for it to get big. Obviously we can put a position on very quickly. Typically, either I will suggest something or one of my partners will. If they do, I have the Sisyphian task of trying to keep up with them. One of my partners will suggest something and I will spend the weekend working on it. There is a process of working out the thesis: I ask questions, get answers, ask more questions, all the time with the company. We build up a model, we think about it, we interrogate each other, and if we generate greater conviction, we add. It can really vary. A lot of very good investors start small to kind of create that—I think that's an easy way to do it. The other thing is a new investment has to displace an old one and it's just psychologically very difficult to tear up an intellectual presupposition. You own this company, it's done well for you. It's psychologically just very different. It's harder than it should be to substitute something else for it. Has Egerton changed a lot since you started roughly 30 years ago? Well, thanks to Jeff, our CEO, we're much better run. Not that we were badly run, but we didn't have to be a modern business when you were in it. Now you do have to be. There are all kinds of regulatory demands. But in terms of the investment philosophy? Yes, it is the same. Do you have the same focus? When I had the pleasure of working for you, we moved into new fancy offices. You had never visited the office and when you came in in the morning, you didn't even walk around; you went straight into your cubicle and started to invest. Well, I have quite a lot of focus. I am not on the management committee. I have never been on the management committee. I do not read the minutes. The other day there was an internal presentation on something which I listened to, and I said to one of my partners, "Why weren't you there?" and she said, "Oh, I wasn't invited." I asked someone else, "Why wasn't Jelena on in the presentation?" and this guy said, "Well, it was the management committee and you." And I didn't know who was on the management committee. So, luckily, I don't have to do any of that. Is experience an advantage in today's investing world? I'm not sure. Sometimes I think you have to be young and naive to embrace visions. I do think that as you get older, you get more conservative. That could be for two reasons: one, the fact that if other people know more, you have to know more; and it could be a feeling that when I started investing, inflation was coming down, interest rates were coming down, we had the fall of the Berlin Wall, we had 40 years of peace. We thought free markets and liberalism would win. What's happened now? Economies are maxed out, corporates have done a lot to raise their earnings, we have two wars, and the West is under threat. So maybe I am more cautious as a result of it. How many stocks do you hold? We hold about 40 and the biggest positions tend to be sort of 8 to 6 percent. I like having small positions which can grow if we like them. I like being thematically diversified, which is very unfashionable today because professional buyers of independent funds value concentration. I don't think they know what can happen when concentration goes wrong. I don't really like financial leverage or portfolio leverage because longs and shorts have positive values and both can go against you at once. How do you read the AI concentration in the market just now? I don't like it but it's very powerful. Are you participating? Yes, we are. We own Nvidia and a brilliant company which supplies into it, Amphenol. We own ASML. We own two hyperscalers and so many things in the US economy are keyed off AI that inevitably we and every other portfolio will have more of it than they think. Historically you were mainly a hedge fund. Now you're a bigger long-only. Why? Do you think long-only is easier? I had a view after the Global Financial Crisis that we would end up being too conservative and that we wouldn't make the most possible returns for our clients. Therefore I wanted our clients to have a mixture of long-only and long-short. That's been correct. In the long run, if you think that markets go up, not paying a performance fee should help, particularly if you're a fund which doesn't go leverage-long. Why is shorting so difficult? Many stocks go up over time. It's been less difficult recently since interest rates went up, but for a very long period when interest rates were low, everything floated up and in periods of euphoria, people believe all kinds of things and any company can raise capital. When you started back in 1994, you were fund number two in Europe, I think. Well, we were one of the very early ones. It was a bad time because our first opening we raised 1.25 million euros, which was not a lot of money. It was a good time because there weren't many people doing it. Somehow we had a good start and it was a good time because there were just fewer funds. Today, how many funds are there all doing the same thing? Loads and loads. How do you differentiate and how do you raise enough money to be viable and to get attention when costs are higher? It's a bigger risk. How did you come up with the idea? You were very successful at Morgan Grenfell. I'd been very lucky at Morgan Grenfell working for a brilliant and amazing guy called Mike Dobson who helped me a lot in ways which you probably didn't even realize. I'd been given a unit trust to run, a mutual fund. That was a huge break for me because it meant that I had an independent track record which was totally my own. That fund ran for six years, it beat the index 10% a year, and it was the number one out of 85 funds over its period. That gave me the confidence that trying to go on my own would be a viable thing. I met a very good man who I thought would be highly complimentary to what I did, Bill Bollinger, and that I felt would get us off to a good start. You call Egerton a benevolent autocracy. What does that mean? That means that two people must love a position for it to be large. And that if I don't love it, it won't be large. Two people must endorse something for it to be of significance. It doesn't mean that I have anything to do with the management of the company, which I don't. What's the one thing about you that most people don't know? As I'm not really a public figure, a hell of a lot, I'd have thought. But I suppose a new thing in my life which I've rather enjoyed is sponsoring the publication of nice books. I've been lucky enough to come into contact with a wonderful academic and a wonderful book designer and a new book is about to come out on Wordsworth's manuscripts. So I like private press books. How do you relax? Reading those books, I guess. My wife would say I never relaxed. But I do the usual things. I walk, I talk to friends, I find people interesting. I go on holiday, sit by the sea, and work. I find it relaxing to be in control of my work and to be working on interesting things. If I was just leaving behind 300 emails a day and lots of stuff which I had to do when I came back, I wouldn't find that relaxing. And I like reading. What keeps you going? I feel today that you are younger at my age than you used to be. I've got nothing else to do and no value to bring to anything else. I don't think I would get bored if I stopped and went to a bookshop every day, but I'd get bored with doing the same thing every day. The markets are interesting. You feel like you never achieve enough. When you're doing badly, you feel like you can't go out on a low. And when you do well, it's kind of too much fun. I really like the people around me in our firm. I really like my partners and I really like everyone who works there. What is your advice to young people? Well, first of all, I would say study what you like because you'll be good at it and you'll be better at it than if you study something you don't like. In my opinion, there's absolutely no point in studying something on business. It's not going to help you. The second thing I'd say is nearly everything is more interesting than you think if you follow it closely. The third thing is that up until you're 21, you can get by by being very clever, but after that you actually have to work. To succeed in life in your career, you need a lot of luck—being in the right place at the right time. You need enthusiasm and you need drive. You need a modicum of intelligence, but it's not like working at Anthropic. You need to be happy in your personal life. I once met Scott Nuttall, who runs KKR. The stock had done really well and it was the beginning of the year. I was still feeling the afterglow of the previous year. I said to him, "Scott, thanks for KKR, but I want to ask you: how do I get to be like you—effortlessly successful at the top of KKR?" And he said to me, "Marry well." And I have married well. It's important to be happy in your life. If you're not happy in your personal life, you're not going to be happy at work. You're not going to do well at work. John, it's been fantastic. Well, big thank you. You're very sweet, Nikolai, but it's all down to you. Really hoped you liked the episode. If you did, please like it, subscribe, and put on the notification.
