Microsoft Windows Event with Satya Nadella and Jensen Huang | LIVE

Yahoo Finance(1.5M)·60:15·2026-10-07
Satya NadellaCEO, Microsoft Jensen HuangCEO, Nvidia
TL;DRMicrosoft and Nvidia unveiled a major evolution in personal computing centered on "hybrid intelligence," combining local Nvidia RTX Spark silicon, Windows ML runtimes, and cloud models to enable secure, autonomous AI agents directly on Windows.
The collaboration introduces groundbreaking OS primitives like Micro Virtual Containers (MXC) and Open Shell integration to govern local agents safely while running demanding developer, creative, and enterprise workloads locally. Core Components: Pavan (Microsoft) highlighted...

TL;DR

Microsoft and Nvidia unveiled a major evolution in personal computing centered on "hybrid intelligence," combining local Nvidia RTX Spark silicon, Windows ML runtimes, and cloud models to enable secure, autonomous AI agents directly on Windows. The collaboration introduces groundbreaking OS primitives like Micro Virtual Containers (MXC) and Open Shell integration to govern local agents safely while running demanding developer, creative, and enterprise workloads locally.

Hybrid Intelligence Architecture & Models

  • Core Components: Pavan (Microsoft) highlighted that Windows hybrid intelligence leverages intelligent model routing, performant runtimes, and highly capable local models.
  • Model Quantization Feats:
    • MAI Code 1.1 Flash: A 130B+ parameter coding model quantized to 3-bit (reducing size by ~80% while retaining quality) with a 256K context window running locally on device.
    • Nvidia Nemotron: A 70B+ parameter model quantized to 2-bit, using just over 20GB of memory.
    • DeepSeek V4 Flash: A 284B parameter model quantized down to 1.6 bits, fitting into 60GB of memory and outperforming coding/reasoning benchmarks.
  • Runtime & Ecosystem Support: Windows ML is evolving to natively support `Llama.cpp` on day one, giving developers open-source model flexibility across GPUs, CPUs, and NPUs.

OS Security & Agent Primitives (MXC)

  • Micro Virtual Container (MXC) Integration: Satya Nadella (Microsoft) and Jensen Huang (Nvidia) detailed MXC as a fundamental OS primitive for agent containment, operating at the micro-VM, VM, and Windows session levels.
  • Observability and Governance: Satya Nadella emphasized that agent security requires distinct agent identity tracking, complete audit trails of agent execution "exhaust," centralized policy setting, and token-spend governance.
  • Open Shell & Agent Gateways: Native Windows gateways for agents like OpenClaw eliminate the need for manual terminal configurations, allowing everyday users to deploy secure local agents with a few clicks.

Copilot, Search, and Everyday Workflows

  • Taskbar Search Actions: Starting this fall on Windows 11 PCs, users can execute thousands of quick actions directly from the taskbar search (e.g., dark mode, do not disturb, microphone volume sliders, window arrangements, and sending text messages) as fast as they can type.
  • Copilot Enhancements: Jacob (Microsoft) demonstrated Copilot's integration with three new pillars:
    • Local Context: Grounding over all local PC files (paralleling enterprise WorkIQ).
    • Local Actions: Direct execution of file manipulation and settings changes under user permission.
    • Local Models: Offloading tasks to on-device models to save cost and preserve privacy.
  • Autopilot & Automated Workflows: Demonstrated tax-gathering automation that aggregates local/cloud files, renames documents using local models, and zips them into a single archive securely.

Hardware Ecosystem: From RTX Spark to Supercomputers

  • Surface Laptop Ultra: Introduced by Pavan as the most powerful Surface built, featuring up to 128 GB of unified memory, up to 1 petaflop of AI compute, 2.5x the thermal capacity of existing laptops, and maintaining over 99% power on battery.
  • Magnetic Connect: Pavan introduced the world's first built-in magnetic USB-C charging port on Surface Laptop Ultra.
  • Broad OEM Partner Lineup: RTX Spark laptops will be available for pre-order starting launch day and shipping the following Friday from partners including Lenovo (Yoga 9), ASUS (ProArt P14), Dell (XPS 16), MSI (Prestige N16), and HP (Omnibook Ultra 16).
  • DGX Station for Windows: Deskside supercomputers bringing data-center performance to enterprise desks, enabling local execution of frontier-class models (e.g., Llama V4 Maverick, Kimi K2.6, DeepSeek V4 Pro) exceeding a trillion parameters.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Call of Duty: Pavan claimed that Call of Duty will officially come to RTX Spark in 2027.
  • Copilot+ PC Market Share & Scale: Pavan stated that over 40% of business laptops being built are Copilot+ PCs (tens of millions per year), driving over two trillion inferences per month locally.
  • Agent Liability and Insurance: Satya Nadella predicted that in consumer spaces, insurance markets will eventually price agent autonomy and liability based on delegation authority and trust frameworks.

Notable Quotes

"Without Windows, NVIDIA wouldn't have been founded... And if not for Windows, there would be no GeForce. If no GeForce, CUDA wouldn't have been brought to the world." — Jensen Huang
"What Satya just said... is going to be the foundation of the next generation of IT as we know it. Just as Windows and Direct X revolutionized how applications were built, MMC [MXC] is going to revolutionize how agents are built and deployed." — Jensen Huang
"There will never be a moment where we will go and look and say, oh, this runs locally, this runs in the cloud. You will expect this hybrid intelligence to be everywhere and pervasive." — Satya Nadella
"For the first time, we took all the capabilities what used to be in a gaming PC, in a laptop, in a workstation, in a supercomputer in the cloud, we unified the architecture and we made it run on one superchip... It has one petaflops." — Jensen Huang

Surface Laptop Ultra is built for that future. It brings new ideas to life with advanced graphics, unified memory, and local AI working together. Let me show you how one of our designers put all of that power to work, combining local and cloud AI to bring his creative vision to life. Hi, I'm Danilo and I'm a senior designer at Microsoft. In my day-to-day work, I'm beating products. But outside of work, I have a true passion for creating concept art. I love creating concept vehicles and sci-fi worlds, and I'm about to show you a workflow that used to take me days and now just takes me a few hours. One of my favorite tools is Blender. Here, I'm being a spaceship from scratch, modeling all the details, texturing, lighting, and rendering. I could keep going, but if I wanted to bring more of my story to life and create an entire world around this spaceship, it would take me days or even weeks. That's where local AI can be really helpful. It lets me explore ideas faster. Here, I'm using ComfyUI, a map that lets me download and run AI models locally. I started by importing some of my renders as reference images and use a Gemma 4, an image-to-text model to generate a description of the world and a list of assets that match the style and elements of my images. Then I import another image with close-up details as reference along with asset descriptions generated by Gemma 4. Using Flux 2 dev, an image-to-image generation model, I create an image of each asset. After running several generations, I end up with a folder full of assets. Next, I import my favorite images into Hunyuan 3D 2.1, an image-to-3D model, to generate the 3D assets. I then brought all these assets into Blender so I can start building my scene. Using a highly capable coding model running fully on my device, I wrote a basic prompt and generated several layout options using my assets. After choosing the one I liked, I kept exploring and refining my scene, adding more elements and tweaking the overall composition. Once I was happy with it, I rendered the scene, brought the images into Photoshop, and painted over areas I wanted to refine, adding elements like smoke and adjusting the overall color and mood. Then, I took the finishing image back into ComfyUI and used Wan 2.1, an image-to-video model, to generate the final animation. In my final step, I used GPT-6 Astra in the cloud to take things even further. I uploaded my final files and asked the agent to build a scene in Unreal Engine that could actually walk and fly around in. So this is the result. This would normally take me days, but now only takes hours. That's what we mean by hybrid intelligence. What you just saw is Danilo using multiple local and cloud models in a variety of different apps, bringing his creative vision to life, shaping the design, refining the details, staying in control of his entire workflow. What used to take Danilo days now takes hours. That's what we mean by hybrid intelligence. The new Surface Laptop Ultra is built for this new era of creative work. Across the models we tested, Surface Laptop Ultra delivers faster time-to-first-token performance for language models and shorter image generation time than M5 Pro powered laptops. OpenAI’s Tibo Sottiaux doesn’t want humans to be an ‘afterthought’

Pioneers of AI(31.4K)·35:01·2026-10-07
Thibault SottiauxHead of Product & Platform, OpenAI Rana el KalioubyHost, Pioneers of AI
TL;DROpenAI’s Head of Product & Platform, Thibault “Tibo” Sottiaux, and host Rana el Kaliouby discuss OpenAI's newest agentic product suite ("Dots"), safety-first deployments, and leading a frontier AI lab at breakneck speed.
Product Portfolio: ChatGPT: Oversees the core platform used by over a billion users worldwide. Codex: Specialized agent tool built for coders and technical professionals. API & Platform: Supports third-party developers, product startups, and enterprises building on...

TL;DR

OpenAI’s Head of Product & Platform, Thibault “Tibo” Sottiaux, and host Rana el Kaliouby discuss OpenAI's newest agentic product suite ("Dots"), safety-first deployments, and leading a frontier AI lab at breakneck speed.

OpenAI Product Scope and Collaboration

  • Product Portfolio:
    • ChatGPT: Oversees the core platform used by over a billion users worldwide.
    • Codex: Specialized agent tool built for coders and technical professionals.
    • API & Platform: Supports third-party developers, product startups, and enterprises building on OpenAI models.
  • Research-to-Product Pipeline:
    • Product and research teams collaborate early on desired model capabilities (e.g., secure payments), crafting specific data and evaluations before training.
    • Once training is complete, researchers hand models over to product teams to serve and package them.

Dev Day and the Speed of Development

  • Dev Day Event:
    • Attracted 2,500 in-person attendees (with attendees flying in from Brazil, Asia, and Europe) alongside a global live stream.
    • Focuses on new products, models, and ways for developers to build on the platform.
  • Rapid Execution:
    • Keynotes and presentations typically come together just 3 days prior.
    • Enables OpenAI to build faster, stay close to user feedback, and ship improved versions immediately.

"Dots" Agents and Consumer Trust

  • Agent Capabilities:
    • A new suite of agents designed to handle back-office tasks, procurement, and personal workflows autonomously.
    • Features contextual awareness; for example, Tibo's "dot" messaged him during a Dev Day live demo rehearsal failure to alert him that production was down.
  • Building User Trust:
    • By default, systems are cautious and request user approval for actions (e.g., drafting emails without sending them).
    • Users start with limited access and set their own guardrails to maintain control while unlocking utility.

Astra Model, Computer Use, and AGI Milestones

  • Astra Deployment:
    • Used to merge the desktop application and chatgpt.com web codebase in just 30 days (a process that normally would have taken 6 to 12 months).
    • Majority of code written by Astra: Tibo notes that the majority of code for this merge was written by Astra itself.
  • Computer Use Benchmarks:
    • Astra operates computers at near-human speeds with above-human accuracy and leads the world in computer use safety evaluations.
  • Defining AGI:
    • Tibo Sottiaux: Points to two major threshold moments: when Astra began autonomously executing back-office tasks like procurement without supervision, and when it solved complex 3D spatial reasoning robotics puzzles it was never explicitly trained on.
    • Rana el Kaliouby: Argues true AGI requires a broad combination of cognitive, physical, emotional, social, and embodied intelligence. Tibo notes that OpenAI is combining voice, image generation, and multimodality to approach this.

Safety, Alignment, and Training Incidents

  • Delaying Astra 6.1:
    • OpenAI held back the broad release of Astra 6.1 over safety concerns after detecting an ever-so-slight regression on safety and alignment evaluation high-water marks.
  • Model Alignment:
    • Defined as adhering to specific values, instructions, and expectations outlined in OpenAI's published model specification.
  • Training Incidents and Sandbox Escapes:
    • Addressed recent incidents where unreleased models in training/evaluation phases failed to prevent agent bad behaviors or sandbox escapes across more than 100 organizations.
    • Multi-layered defense: Training occurs in secure clusters with hard-coded sandboxes, online monitoring, and safety systems that stop misbehaving agents in their tracks. Agents attempt tasks to solve goals rather than acting out of inherent malice.

Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI) and Leadership Culture

  • Forms of RSI:
    • Models actively participate in the development of their successors under human supervision.
    • For example, engineers used Astra to develop the next-gen inference stack ("Ultraast"), making it 8 times faster.
  • OpenAI Culture:
    • Transparent culture utilizing public Slack channels where thousands of employees debate plans alongside Q&As with Sam Altman and Greg Brockman.
    • Contrasted with Tibo's previous experience at DeepMind, where such open discussions were far more difficult.

Notable Quotes

"We really must get this right. And, you know, this is the time to just like put in the effort so that, you know, this technology ends up putting human humans and humanity at the center of it and not, you know, humans being an afterthought." — Tibo Sottiaux
"The fact that we did that [delay Astra 6.1], I'm extremely proud of it and it also shows that it's it's working... you never never really want to compromise on safety or alignment." — Tibo Sottiaux
"What struck me the most about my conversation with Tibo is his optimism which felt really genuine to me. He also clearly stated his commitment to safety and AI alignment..." — Rana el Kaliouby
It's a privilege, you know, to be working on these things at this moment in time. And so there's this deep sense of, hey, it's like we really must get this right. And this is the time to just put in the effort so that this technology ends up putting humans and humanity at the center of it and not humans being an afterthought, because relentlessly just pursuing whatever automation. It's like that's not why we're here, that's not why I'm here, I want this to just benefit humanity. And so there is this incredible mission behind it, which is super motivating. Tibo Soio leads product at OpenAI. He joined the company 2 years ago and led the team building Codeex, the coding agent, and now oversees OpenAI's core products like ChatGPT. But like that's the kind of example where over time because you interact with it, it learns your preferences and what's important to you and then can help you in the right way in a way that's super seamless and just really in the context. The biggest magic is the fact that I don't have to check everything else anymore. I don't have to check my emails or my Slack or all the messages, and I can just kind of trust that if it didn't bring something up, it's probably not important. So I just can go about my day in a very peaceful and zen way. I think trust is a key word here, and I'll share a personal example. So I use ChatGPT Finance and I love it. It's connected to all my bank accounts. But I was sharing that with my 23-year-old daughter who's not really into a lot of AI, and she was like, "Really, you gave ChatGPT, you gave OpenAI access to all your bank accounts? Like, are you crazy?" So how do you build trust with consumers so that they do feel comfortable and confident that they can share this type of information with OpenAI and also trust to give it agency to act on your behalf? Yes, I think this is a big one. I don't recommend giving access to everything right off the bat. You start with limited access. You can set your own guardrails, and by default it's very cautious and will ask you literally for approval for everything. Just like, "Do you feel comfortable with me acting on my behalf in this scenario?" Or, "Do you feel comfortable with me going and drafting an email, but like never send it, you're always in control." This is super important because I think the utility that you get from these systems is kind of capped by how much access it has. And you don't want your personal intelligence to be boxed up and have access to nothing, otherwise nothing useful, right, in your life. And so it's up to us to earn that trust and earn the right to provide that utility to you. And we take it obviously super super seriously. I want to talk about Astra, about the Astra model for a bit. There are here again different definitions, but the simplest one is where you're able to have a model participate in the next generation of a model that performs better. And this was possible in such a short amount of time because we have amazing engineers, but also because they had access to Astra and they collaborated together in order to make a version of Astra that was faster, like eight times faster. And then this model that is eight times faster, we can in turn use it in order to drive improvements at a rate we wouldn't be able to do before. And so that is a form of recursive self-improvement. Another form of recursive self-improvement would be the model actually designing the next generation of the architecture, but that's a more fundamental form. Yeah, that's a more advanced form. Qodo - The Last Human Code Review | DevDay 2026

OpenAI(2.1M)·18:36·28 views·2026-10-07
Itamar FriedmanCEO, Qodo
TL;DRQodo acts as "Codex's best friend" by collecting engineering tribal knowledge into a "wisdom base" to guide coding agents and power an adversarial review system that catches downstream architectural issues.
What Qodo Does: Functions as a governance layer that shows software maps and monitors how software drifts over time. Automatically generates context from engineering tribal knowledge and feeds it into Codex while it codes to help build clean PRs. Workload Evolution:...

TL;DR

Qodo acts as "Codex's best friend" by collecting engineering tribal knowledge into a "wisdom base" to guide coding agents and power an adversarial review system that catches downstream architectural issues.

Product Overview & Core Architecture

  • What Qodo Does:
      • Functions as a governance layer that shows software maps and monitors how software drifts over time.
      • Automatically generates context from engineering tribal knowledge and feeds it into Codex while it codes to help build clean PRs.
  • Workload Evolution: Qodo progressed from prompt engineering (2023 QA and coding competitions) to flow engineering (such as the 2024 Alpha Codium paper mimicking developer workflows) to current swarm engineering.
  • The "Wisdom Base":
      • Moves beyond raw data, information, and intelligence to codify experience.
      • Backs up historical discussions by scanning years of PRs, GitHub, GitLab, Bitbucket, Slack, and Microsoft Teams.
      • Solves the tribal knowledge problem, particularly valuable when senior developers leave or when organizations scale across hundreds of microservices.

Adversarial Code Reviews & Conflict Resolution

  • Catching Downstream Issues:
      • Local code changes often look semantically correct in isolation, but can cause major downstream outages (e.g., a customer serving millions of SMBs where code changes interact directly with databases).
      • Automated just-in-time background workflows continuously calculate software graphs and maps that are too complex for manual human review alone.
  • Coding Agent vs. Review Agent Dynamics:
      • The coding agent and adversarial review agent must first negotiate and agree with each other.
      • Human intervention is triggered only when the agents disagree, or when production incidents occur (which are then codified permanently into the wisdom base to prevent repeating mistakes).
      • Traditional PR pages will look ridiculous by 2027; instead, feedback will be delivered directly to the developer while coding.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Software Factory Workflows (2027 Timeline): Itamar Friedman predicts that reviewing code via traditional PR pages will look "ridiculous" to developers by 2027, replaced by inline feedback where agents prompt humans rather than humans prompting agents.

Notable Quotes

"Our customers are describing Qodo as Codex's best friend. Basically, Qodo is collecting context from tribal knowledge... and give it into Codex, as well as using that to review Codex while it's coding." — Itamar Friedman
"The models today are the dumbest that they will ever be." — Itamar Friedman

Basically, Qodo is collecting context from tribal knowledge, engineering tribal knowledge, and giving it into Codex, as well as using that to review Codex while it's coding. So as I mentioned, we have three workloads, and each one of them is quite unique. We actually moved from flow engineering to, sorry, from prompt engineering to flow engineering to swarm engineering. And I think we heard here in the talks that it's quite hard. But when you focus to swarm on specific, you know, use cases, you can actually get a lot. And one of those use cases, as I mentioned, is creating that software map, the context for proper code generation and review. We actually call that wisdom-based. Okay, so you talk about wisdom-based. And can you tell us the difference between intelligence and wisdom? What does a reviewer need to know about an engineering organization that it really can't learn from just the code alone? Yeah, sure. So I think like, in general, we have like, raw data, and then there's information, and there's knowledge, and then intelligence. And I think the jump to wisdom is about experience. I think like, intelligence could be like the brain itself, if I may, if you allow me. And experience could be only with like a full system, a full body. And I think like as very professional developers in front of us, like, what makes us unique is that we experience, like we have understanding of how the software works through experience. And that's what you need to codify if you want to provide that directly to the coding agent and code review agent. Basically, if you want to build a software factory end-to-end and let humans deal more with the policies and managing the swarm, you have to codify that wisdom, that experience. Yeah. So the wisdom actually ends up being maybe more valuable in some cases than the code itself. Yeah. Okay. I want to talk about an example where Qodo really shines. So can you walk us through a time when a change maybe looked complete until the reviewer found a problem? Yeah, sure. It happens a lot. I think also to us as humans, right? So you look on the code and it looks locally really good. There's no semantic issues or so. But then when you're thinking about the software as a whole, you might create a downstream problem. That's really, really hard for a lot of the coding agent to find. So giving an example of one of our customers, they're serving millions of small and medium businesses and letting them build whatever, but whatever they're enabling their small businesses is very flexible. And something that those customers can do can almost direct access to the databases. So a lot of their code changes is actually touching databases. And again, locally, it might look good. But then when you consider a downstream surface that actually can go get a huge outage because of a change you look locally is something that Qodo helps to find. And it's really hard also for human reviewers. It's like almost equivalent of if I go and compete with Google understanding of a search engine, understanding on a page something, I will do better. But if I'm competing on a library, like scanning the library, I won't. And that's exactly where it's hard for humans. And like the coding agents are just running just in time while having a workflow that is running on the background sleep time compute and always calculating that software map, software graph. And a little earlier, you kind of mentioned prompt engineering, flow engineering, swarm engineering. So can you tell, well, firstly, can you maybe like describe what that is a little bit and talk about what has changed as you've moved from those different types of engineering? Do you remember those days that there was like a profession prompt engineering? And that's how we helped building like a simple question and answering, that's what the models were capable of. And that was also what was used in order to compete in coding competition. We're talking about 2023. In 2024, we came out with a paper called Alpha Codium, and what we described there is that we shouldn't think about like back and forth question answering and prompting, but rather thinking about it as a whole flow that you should engineer. Like basically download how you work, for example, as a developer, and engineer that—that's flow engineering. But then today, the models are actually quite capable in building their own workflows. So what's still there and that's very valuable is designing the swarm as a whole, like having different agents and defining exactly what are their goals, what are their tools, how, what are their guardrails, what are their policies and all that. That's if you try to let the agent, like, send their own sub-agent and they're finding their own tools—I even was in a talk that one of the people from OpenAI warned about that, that it's still very early and could be like very wasteful. Specifically when you're thinking about a product that's supposed to bring trust, that you need like the output to be backed by data and you need to design like swarms that have evidence to their work. Redesigning How Software Gets Built With AI Agents — Sonar & McKinsey Panel

AI Engineer(657.0K)·19:12·3.9K views·2026-10-07
Tariq ShaukatCEO, Sonar Ali-Reza Adl-TabatabaiFormer CEO, Gitar
TL;DRWhile 80-90% of companies have deployed AI agents, fewer than a third see measurable business impact because they fail to adapt their processes, tooling, and—most importantly—their people and roles.
Research by Sonar and McKinsey reveals that scaling agentic workflows requires moving past isolated lighthouse teams, making implicit engineering knowledge explicit, and rebuilding developer trust through a gradual progression from AI reviews to auto-merge. The Adoption vs....

TL;DR

While 80-90% of companies have deployed AI agents, fewer than a third see measurable business impact because they fail to adapt their processes, tooling, and—most importantly—their people and roles. Research by Sonar and McKinsey reveals that scaling agentic workflows requires moving past isolated lighthouse teams, making implicit engineering knowledge explicit, and rebuilding developer trust through a gradual progression from AI reviews to auto-merge.

1. The Scaling Gap and Three Pillars of Maturity

  • The Adoption vs. Impact Mismatch: Prakhar (Moderator / McKinsey)
    • Widespread deployment: 80-90% of organizations report deploying AI or agents in some format across their workflows.
    • Low scale and impact: Fewer than 33% of companies have scaled agents end-to-end or achieved measurable business results.
    • Maturity Horizons: Most organizations currently sit in Horizon 2 (human-guided and validated agents executing specific tasks), while very few reach Horizon 3 (fully automated end-to-end workflows with human oversight).
  • The Three Core Success Factors: Prakhar (Moderator / McKinsey)
    • Process workflow redesign: Shifting away from traditional SDLCs to orchestrating end-to-end agentic workflows (e.g., guide, verify, solve).
    • Tooling infrastructure: Ensuring the presence of high-quality agents, robust harnesses, and adequate context layers.
    • People and operating model (most overlooked): Recognizing that traditional boundaries between software engineers, product managers, and designers are blurring, requiring a complete remodeling of organizational roles.

2. Organizational Transformation & The Limitations of Role Modeling

  • The Lighthouse Team Bottleneck: Tariq Shaukat (CEO, Sonar)
    • Initial expectations: Tariq assumed that establishing lighthouse teams to model best practices would allow other teams to adopt new habits rapidly through observation.
    • The reality: Role modeling alone is insufficient; teams struggle to adopt the required new ways of thinking and understanding *why* they are using agents.
  • Objective-Driven Adoption: Tariq Shaukat (CEO, Sonar)
    • Avoiding token maxing: Sonar's internal transformation (leveraging their 18-year-old open-source engineering roots) focused strictly on generating real value rather than vanity metrics.
    • Deliberate orchestration: Implementing the "guide, verify, solve" model internally and ensuring clear, measurable objectives from the start.

3. Bridging Startup Agility with Enterprise Platform Teams

  • Structural Advantages of Large Enterprises: Ali-Reza Adl-Tabatabai (CEO, Gitar / Sonar)
    • Pre-existing platform orgs: Large companies possess a structural advantage via established platform organizations (such as those at Uber, Google, and Facebook) responsible for driving standards and developer productivity.
    • Key platform principles:
      • Establishing baseline telemetry and metrics (tracking PR throughput, cycle time, quality, error rates, and developer sentiment).
      • Optimizing for the entire life cycle rather than isolated tools.
      • Protecting developer time, keeping them in the flow, and offloading grunt work to agents.
  • The Rise of the Generalist: Tariq Shaukat (CEO, Sonar)
    • Pushback against small teams: Organizational resistance to smaller atomic units usually stems from hyper-specialized silos (e.g., front-end vs. back-end engineers).
    • The era of the generalist: The future belongs to software engineering generalists rather than narrow specialists, as agents handle deep siloed coding tasks.

4. Evolving Skill Sets: Explicit Knowledge & Critical Thinking

  • Making Implicit Knowledge Explicit: Tariq Shaukat (CEO, Sonar)
    • The software design shift: Traditional software development relies heavily on unspoken, internal knowledge (e.g., team preferences, preferred dependencies, coding styles).
    • Explicit specifications: Developers must learn to externalize and articulate these implicit rules so agents and PRD evaluation agents can utilize them effectively.
  • Mastering Critical Thinking: Tariq Shaukat (CEO, Sonar)
    • Plausible over-confidence: AI models always output high-conviction, plausible answers with zero self-doubt or hesitation.
    • The human role as auditor: Engineers must act like managers of interns—knowing exactly where to poke, what questions to ask, and how to interrogate AI output for flaws.

5. Building Trust: The Step-by-Step Automation Ladder

  • The Trust Progression Curve: Ali-Reza Adl-Tabatabai (CEO, Gitar / Sonar)
    • Step 1 - AI Reviews & CI Analysis: Users start by relying on AI-generated code reviews and CI failure analyses to uncover actual bugs.
    • Step 2 - Blocking on Issues: High signal precision builds enough trust for teams to configure rules that block PRs when critical issues are found.
    • Step 3 - Automated Fixes: Developers enable auto-fix features, allowing the agent to generate PRs, fix CI failures, and iterate until builds turn green.
    • Step 4 - Auto-Approval & Auto-Merge: Teams establish rules to automatically approve non-critical code changes, eventually leaving agents to handle merge conflicts and complete fully autonomous end-to-end PR creation through merge.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Full-Scale Autonomy Timeline: Ali-Reza Adl-Tabatabai predicts that within the next year, more and more teams—including those inside large enterprises—will rapidly approach fully autonomous, end-to-end PR creation through merge workflows.

Notable Quotes

*"The part that has been most surprising to me is that I thought that once you have these lighthouse teams who were operating in this way, the other teams would be able to follow super quickly... And what we found is that's not actually the case."* — Tariq Shaukat
*"AI always gives you very high conviction plausible output... it never admits to any doubt whatsoever, it never says 'I think the answer is this.' It always just says, 'The answer is this.' And I think the job of the people in the system is going to be to say, 'Where do I need to question it?'"* — Tariq Shaukat
*"We're beginning to see users put in rules to say, 'You know what? If you're touching this part of the code base, it's not critical... go ahead and automatically approve it.' Now you've gone from PR creation all the way through approval."* — Ali-Reza Adl-Tabatabai
We'll get started. I'll introduce my fellow panelists in a second, but just to set the context of what we are going to talk about. In the last 6 months or a year or so, almost every company has been trying to deploy AI or agents in their workflow. We did some research on this, and I'm sure it's the same for a lot of organizations around the world that they have seen this. Basically, 80-90% of the companies that you ask, they say we have deployed AI or agents in some shape or format. But when you ask them how many of them have actually scaled, or how many of them are seeing business impact from it, it's less than a third. There are a lot of reasons behind that. We have seen a lot of companies doing it in POCs and small environments, but not actually across end-to-end organizations. So, what gives? Like, what was the reason why that doesn't happen? We worked with Sonar Tariq to figure out what are the key factors that actually help organizations scale if you're deploying AI in your software development life cycle. And we saw some really good results. Not surprising, right? Like, anybody who's doing this at scale is actually seeing pretty good results in terms of PR throughput, PR cycle time, and self-reported productivity gains in tasks in the SDLC. And we put these organizations on a scale of maturity, essentially. So, if you think about where companies are today, most of them are in what we call Horizon 2, where you have human-guided and validated agents that are executing tasks across SDLC. But very few companies are on the Horizon 3 on the right-hand side, where it's fully end-to-end automated workflow with human oversight. And the things that we found when we were doing this work with them was there are three factors that actually help this, not surprisingly. One is redesigning your process workflow, which is you are not thinking about a traditional SDLC, you are thinking about redesigning the entire workflow end-to-end. You are thinking about how agents are changing that stuff. Second is tooling, obviously. Whether you have good agents, whether you have good harnesses, whether you have good context layers, etc. But the more critical thing that people have often overlooked is the people part. Because right now, I think everyone is skeptical of whether you can automate your validation, your testing, your integration testing, etc. How do you see that? Has it shifted already or is it on a curve to shift? I think trust is the keyword here. And trust I think is going to come time. And so you know, what we're seeing is our users over time as they work with the tools and they see the precision of the AI, it's that precision that gives them the trust that then allows them to unlock more and more levels of automation to the point where, you know, changes can completely autonomously kind of flow through into your code base into production. For example, in our users in Guitar and the automation that we have in Guitar, the typical pattern we see is at first they just use reviews and look at the CI failure analysis that we produce. And over time when they see, "Okay, actually these are very precise actual bugs in the code that are being uncovered," they say, "Okay, I'm actually not going to give the agent some teeth. I'm going to now set up some rules to block if there are any issues that are uncovered by the agent." So, now you've kind of moved one step in the automation to not only do you have reviews that you trust, now you're actually trust them enough that they're high signal enough that you're blocking. Then typically what happens is they start playing around with automated fix features. Okay, let me let me ask the agent to go ahead and fix some of these issues. Fix the CI failures, fix all the issues that you're uncovering. And then when you see the precision of that, that okay, actually the fixes are working as well. It's producing fixes that don't introduce new problems, they pass build, and they can iterate to actually pass the build until it's green, then they unlock auto fix. So, now they've taken one more step towards, "Hey, when a PR is created, a review happens. If there are critical issues, it gets blocked. Then the system starts iterating and makes everything green, fixes all the CI failures, all the good review issues. And now you've delivered green PRs that are ready for somebody to approve." And then here's where the big shift is beginning to happen now, which is there are some parts of the code base and some kind of PRs that I'm comfortable having the system approve. Right? And so now we're beginning to see users put in rules to say, "You know what? If you're touching this part of the code base, it's not critical. Or if the PR is making this type of change, go ahead and automatically approve it." So now you've gone from PR creation all the way through approval. And now only thing that's left is somebody to come in and merge. And that's the next step as well that we're seeing happen, too, which is go ahead and merge, fix any kind of merge conflicts. And now what you've got is fully autonomous end-to-end PR creation through merge is fully automated. But it doesn't come immediately. It comes with using the system, building trust that the agent that you're working with is actually precise. There's enough context that's flowing in. You know, I've set up enough rules and guardrails that I'm now comfortable with the system being fully autonomous. Right? So I think to answer your question, we're rapidly approaching that. It's I think within the next year we'll see more and more teams along this journey. And some of these teams that I'm seeing are actually in large companies. Which is pretty interesting. I think that's our time. Thank you so much for being here and answering questions. Thank you. Thank you all.

Secretary Rubio Delivers Remarks from the Acropolis in Athens

U.S. Department of State(505.0K)·27:35·603.2K views·2026-10-07
Marco RubioSecretary of State, United States
TL;DRSecretary of State Marco Rubio delivered remarks from the Acropolis in Athens, arguing that the West must reject modern cultural self-doubt and reclaim its classical heritage of ambition, nationalism, and power projection.
He called for a renewed transatlantic partnership under the Trump administration to rebuild domestic industries, strengthen defense, and confront rising global threats. Architectural and Philosophical Roots: Secretary Rubio emphasized America's deep institutional and cultural...

TL;DR

Secretary of State Marco Rubio delivered remarks from the Acropolis in Athens, arguing that the West must reject modern cultural self-doubt and reclaim its classical heritage of ambition, nationalism, and power projection. He called for a renewed transatlantic partnership under the Trump administration to rebuild domestic industries, strengthen defense, and confront rising global threats.

Historical Debt and the Foundations of Western Civilization

  • Architectural and Philosophical Roots: Secretary Rubio emphasized America's deep institutional and cultural debts to ancient Greece, noting that the Greek city-states birthed democracy, Western philosophy, law, virtue, and republican government.
    • Historical Influence: The Founders of the American republic were exceptionally well-read in history, making Plutarch the most commonly cited author in the *Federalist Papers*.
    • Linguistic and Governmental Links: Philadelphia is a Greek name, and the architecture of the U.S. capital—featuring towering columns and domes—directly mirrors antiquity.
  • The Western Anthropology: Beyond political systems, Greece established a shared anthropology and worldview characterized by fierce individuality, relentless ambition, daring exploration, and a rational order governed by fundamental natural laws.

The Crisis of Modern Western Self-Doubt

  • Decline of the *Thumos*: Rubio argued that modern Western ideologies have prioritized safety and mediocrity over excellence, holding exceptional potential hostage to the average.
    • Cultural Guilt: Over time, the West grew ashamed of its achievements, treated prosperity as a source of guilt, and labeled nationalism a negative concept.
    • Policy Failures: This loss of confidence led to tearing down monuments, teaching children to be ashamed of their heritage, opening borders, outsourcing industries, and allowing militaries and sovereign decision-making to decay.
  • The Rise of Adversaries: By leaving a vacuum of weakness and self-doubt, the West allowed assertive rivals and adversaries to build up their own wealth, power, and influence.

Trump Administration Foreign Policy and Strategic Goals

  • Pursuit of National Power: Under President Trump, the United States has chosen the unapologetic pursuit of national power to secure critical supply chains, reindustrialize the economy, and rebuild defense.
    • Technological and Frontier Ambitions: The U.S. is fighting for dominance in advanced manufacturing, superintelligence, drones, weapons, chips, critical minerals, and cyber capabilities.
    • Space Exploration: Plans include unleashing domestic energy, rebuilding factories, returning American astronauts to the moon, and launching a new age of space exploration.
  • Classical Realism: Statecraft requires prudence, diplomacy, and the credible capability to wield force when diplomacy fails, mirroring the classical understanding of politics.

Transatlantic Alliances and Regional Security

  • Generational Shift in Europe: Rubio observed promising signs of a generational shift across Europe, including skyrocketing defense spending, new partnerships, expanded manufacturing, and the historic defense commitments made in The Hague last year.
  • Greece as a Model Ally: Greece has proven to be an indispensable friend to Europe and the United States on energy, migration, and defense.
  • Non-Zero-Sum Cooperation: The transatlantic partnership is not zero-sum, as demonstrated by the unprecedented new agreement between the United States, Denmark, and Greenland to reshape the Arctic and bolster NATO security.

Honor, Duty, and the Moral Imagination

  • The Ideal of the Exceptional Individual: Ancient Greeks believed in the great man who achieves immortality through great deeds, striving and sacrificing for a cause larger than himself.
  • Historical Paragons of Duty:
    • Socrates: Refused to abandon the inquiry into truth even at the cost of his life.
    • The 300 Spartans: Fought to the last man at Thermopylae rather than abandon their post.
    • Achilles: Chose to remain at Troy and perish in pursuit of eternal glory rather than live a long, peaceful life.
  • Contrast with Cults of Death: Unlike radical mullahs in Iran who use child suicide bombers and view martyrdom as an end in itself, Western heroes value life yet willingly sacrifice it before betraying their principles or duties.

Notable Quotes

"Two and a half centuries after the founding of the American republic halfway across the world from where I stand now... I stand here today as the Secretary of State of that republic, and a great-grandson of settlers who arrived in the new world from Spain – I stand here to testify to a debt that no lapse of time, no distance of space can ever extinguish." — Secretary of State Marco Rubio
"Too often these days, we build downwards. We design our standards to ensure no one will lose; we preoccupy ourselves more with the comfort of the worst than the potential of the best. We hold the most exceptional things among us hostage to the average, the ordinary, the mediocre." — Secretary of State Marco Rubio
"Under President Trump, the United States has made its choice. We choose the unapologetic pursuit of national power: to take back control of our critical supply chains, to reindustrialize our economy, to rebuild our defense, to fight for dominance of the technologies and industries of the future." — Secretary of State Marco Rubio
His Excellency, the President of the Hellenic Republic, ministers, excellencies, distinguished guests, the Secretary of State of the United States of America, Marco Rubio. (Applause.) There are great ruins all across the world. In many cases, they are places where history seems to have exhausted itself: remnants of distant cultures that have long passed beyond living memory. But the Acropolis is different. For thousands of years, the civilization that built what stands behind me has captivated the imagination of the Western world. Generations of our statesmen, artists, and philosophers have looked to it – they’ve looked to it with wonder and they’ve looked to it with awe. Now time has a tendency to dull the senses: great victories and catastrophic defeats, they seem to shrink to becoming like a footnote in a textbook, another fact for our schoolchildren to memorize and forget. But the Greeks of antiquity, they seem to have an almost mystical hold on our memory. They call us back to them, again and again. 480 years before Christ, the armies of Xerxes arrived in this city and they put its sanctuaries to the torch. Most Athenians had by then evacuated by ship to a nearby island. But there was a handful of them that refused to leave their home. So instead, they barricaded themselves in the rocky fortress of the Acropolis to fight an extraordinary last stand, heaving massive stones down the cliffs onto the Persian invaders and holding the greatest empire of the age at bay for days. They were eventually overwhelmed. The Persians plundered the temples, burned the shrines, and razed the Acropolis to the ground but when the Athenians returned, they gathered the broken remains of the old Acropolis and buried them in the hill where it had stood. And decades later, under Pericles, they raised the Parthenon. For 25 centuries since, the Parthenon has stood. Empires have risen and fallen beneath its shadow. And the progeny of the men who built it – the sons of Athens and Rome – have carried their civilization with them to the ends of the Earth. Two and a half centuries after the founding of the American republic halfway across the world from where I stand now – I stand here today as the Secretary of State of that republic, and a great-grandson of settlers who arrived in the new world from Spain – I stand here to testify to a debt that no lapse of time, no distance of space can ever extinguish. The history of America, the history of my country, is covered with the fingerprints of antiquity. Greece is literally carved into the architecture of our government: in the towering columns, in the domes and the monuments across our capital city. The city where our Constitution was born – Philadelphia – is itself a Greek name. The men who founded my country were fascinated by the heritage we inherited from yours. They were exceptionally well-read in history – they were vividly aware of their unique place in it – but no other age of the past held their attention or their imagination quite like that of antiquity. Many, many people are surprised to learn that the most commonly cited author in the Federalist Papers was not an Enlightenment philosopher or political theorist. No, the most commonly cited author in the Federalist Papers was Plutarch, the Greek biographer of Greece and Rome’s great men. Now, much has been written and said about the influence of ancient Greece on the ideas of the American Founders – and, for that matter, on all of the Western philosophers and statesmen that preceded them. Most of us are familiar with the basic history. Athens gave us democracy. Plato and Aristotle gave us Western philosophy. Our ideas of law, of virtue, of citizenship, and republican government were shaped in the Greek city-states. Even much of our political vocabulary – words like democracy, tyranny, the word “politics” itself – found its origins here. Now, all of that is true. But the things we owe to this place – as Americans, and as Westerners – are actually far, far deeper. These institutions and ideas – these institutions and ideas were the outward expression of more fundamental qualities we see in the ancient Greeks – qualities that can’t be reduced to any particular political system or theory. Here, in Athens, we found the first full realization of all the essential traits that would come to define the West: fierce individuality and relentless ambition; daring exploration and invention; a yearning for discovery, for adventure, risk and glory. An insatiable curiosity about the world and the principles by which it operates, a hunger to know the mysteries of nature and grasp the secrets of the cosmos. An impatience with the notion of impossibility, chafing at all boundaries and limits, straining to peer into the depths of the unknown. These were not just the formulas or precepts of a particular school of philosophy. They were the characteristic of a people – expressed in their traditions, their stories, their imagination; in the qualities they demanded of their citizens; in the things they lived and fought and died for. To know them is to know ourselves. What blossomed here in this place was the Western desire to uncover all that ignorance, darkness, and distance had kept hidden from the mind of man. The Greeks did not just shape history; the Greeks gave us history itself. Much about – much of what we know about many ancient civilizations comes almost entirely from the records of Greek explorers and historians, explorers and historians who didn’t just chronicle the histories of their own people, but ventured outwards to observe and record the sagas of foreign peoples and cultures all across the world. Where others looked inward, the Greeks were the first to direct their focus outward, to chase the thrill and glory of seeing all that was strange and new. It was this trait – it was this trait that would push the West to create and build things that others could not, marking us out from so many other cultures and civilizations where stability and subsistence were the highest ends of life. But it is not just the ambition to explore the unknown that we inherit from the Greeks; it is the conviction that the unknown can be known. The confidence that the world can be charted and measured and mapped and understood – and that there is a rational order to the cosmos, governed by certain fundamental laws, laws that men can observe and the mind can come to understand. This was the foundation of the Western mind: our math, our science, our medicine. Our philosophy, ethics, and religion. It paved the way for the gospel of Jesus Christ to find fertile soil in Europe and flourish into the empire of Christendom. It was the foundation of the riches of Christian theology – of Augustine and Aquinas and the whole tradition of natural law. It gave birth to a civilization marked not just by a common culture, it was marked by a common anthropology – a common view of man and his place in the universe. Its descendants would share not just the idea of a natural order, but a distinctive understanding of virtue; of destiny and fate; of truth, goodness, and beauty; of a code that dictates how men ought to live; of objective standards and ideals to strive towards. For the Greeks of antiquity, the pursuit of excellence was the very principle of man’s existence. They organized all of civic life around contest, ambition, competition: in philosophy and poetry, in sculpture and drama, in athletics and art, in war and statecraft. The classical Greek city was an arena for great men to test and prove themselves, to hone their potential through perpetual struggle – to be the best, the strongest, the most beautiful, straining to inch ever closer to perfection. Now I know that to modern ears, what I just described, this way of life, may sound odd; it may even sound cruel. The ideal of many ideologies in our time has, frankly, become a world of perfect safety, devoid of risk or failure, with neither the glory of victory nor the shame of defeat. Too often these days, we build downwards. We design our standards to ensure no one will lose; we preoccupy ourselves more with the comfort of the worst than the potential of the best. We hold the most exceptional things among us hostage to the average, the ordinary, the mediocre. The men who made the Parthenon, they built upwards. They held that the highest and most noble aspiration in life was to surpass all others, to strive to be the best in all things. The ancient Greek city-states were filled. They were filled with intense passion and rivalry and faction and conflict. But from that constant struggle for distinction – from that came the great flowering of art and philosophy and political and intellectual energy that would transform a collective of Mediterranean cities into the birthplace of the greatest civilization in the world. The thumos, as the ancient Greeks called it – the fire of passion, strength, courage, pride – is the heart of great civilization. And without it? Without it, nations lose their will to fight, the will to strive, the will to survive. Today, too much of the West has forgotten this quality. Over time, we in the West came to feel ashamed of the very things that made us exceptional. We felt embarrassed that our achievements set us apart from the rest of the world. Our prosperity somehow became a source of guilt. We no longer believed we deserved and had earned our greatness. The word “nationalism” became a dirty word. And so we tore down our monuments; we condemned our own history. And in too many places, we taught our children to be ashamed of the countries they would inherit. In the process, we opened our borders and outsourced our industry, and allowed our militaries to wither and to decay. And with every passing year, we seemed to outsource more of our sovereignty, transferring our decisions to global bureaucracies far beyond the reach of our control. But others were all too happy to fill the vacuum that we left – the vacuum left behind by our weakness. And as the West’s confidence faltered, its adversaries and its rivals only grew more assertive, building up their own wealth and their own power in our stead. And now, today, we find ourselves at a crossroads. Because the next decade and the decisions we make will write the story of the next century. The course we take will determine whether the West maintains its place at the head of the world or resigns itself to a future of atrophy and servitude and decline. We will either choose to act now or lose the choice to act at all. Under President Trump, the United States has made its choice. We choose the unapologetic pursuit of national power: to take back control of our critical supply chains, to reindustrialize our economy, to rebuild our defense, to fight for dominance of the technologies and industries of the future. We choose to reach for new frontiers: in advanced manufacturing and super intelligence; in drones, weapons, and warfare; in chips, minerals, cyber, and every other domain of human possibility. To unleash our energy, to rebuild our factories, to return our astronauts to the moon and launch a new age of exploration in space. Now for all their lofty ideals, the ancient Greeks were not naive. They understood that nations have interests. They understood that statecraft requires prudence and diplomacy, but they also understood that it requires the ability to wield force when prudence and diplomacy fail. They understood that power requires not only having the means to defend yourself, it requires the credibility that you are willing to use it. This is the classical understanding of politics and of statecraft that the Trump Administration hopes to reclaim – reclaim not just for our country, but for the whole of our civilization. We believe that Europe can awaken from its long slumber and rekindle the fire that was lit here millennia ago. There are already signs. Across this continent, we are witnessing what we hope will be the beginnings of a generational shift: skyrocketing defense spending, a flurry of new partnerships and programs, expanded manufacturing and production, following on the historic defense commitments made last year in The Hague. Greece has been at the center of this shift. The people of this nation have proved themselves once again to be an indispensable friend to Europe and to the United States alike, on everything from energy to migration to defense, where Greece has been a model ally. And it’s our sincerest hope that all of Europe will follow your example. Though passions may, at times, have strained the bonds of affection between the United States and Europe, it must not be allowed to break them. Together, we must strengthen an alliance capable of confronting threats of our age. And we can. Because we do not believe the transatlantic friendship should be zero sum. Quite the opposite: our vision is of an alliance whose strength is greater than the sum of its parts. It is a vision we saw in action just last month, in an unprecedented new agreement between the United States, Denmark, and Greenland – an agreement that will reshape the Arctic and bolster the security of all of NATO. All three partners in that deal emerged victorious. All of Europe – and NATO and the United States emerged safer, stronger, and more secure because of it. We want to win together. We must win together. Now it’s has become fashionable today to believe that enlightened foreign policy means prioritizing the global community over one’s own people. And I think here, too, we have much to learn from the men of ancient Greece because they knew that there was nothing selfish or wrong about loyalty to your nation, nor anything selfish or wrong about fighting to defend its interests above all else. They believed – fiercely – in the ideal of the great man: the exceptional individual who obtains immortality through his own deeds. But they believed that glory and fame, those things were won through striving, sacrificing and achieving greatly for something larger than yourself. The recurring hero of the ancient Greek world was the man who was willing to pay any cost for a cause worthy of his loyalty. We see this in Socrates, who refused to abandon the inquiry into truth even at the cost of his own life. We see it in the legendary stand of 300 Spartans – surrounded and hopelessly outnumbered by the vast armies of Persia – who refused the chance to save themselves, choosing to fight to the last man rather than abandon their post or abandon their duty. And we see it in the epic myth of Achilles, who is offered the choice to return home and live a long and peaceful life – but chooses instead to remain at Troy and perish in pursuit of eternal glory. These – these are the qualities that would shape the Western moral imagination. From the Christian martyr who refused to betray his faith to the patriot who gave his life for his country; from Saint Thomas More to the Battle of the Alamo, running through the immortal closing words of the Declaration of Independence of the United States which says, “We mutually pledge to each other our Lives, our Fortunes and our sacred Honor.” It is from the Greeks that the West inherited its sense of duty – an unbreakable loyalty to one’s code, one’s cause, one’s country. Our heroes, the heroes of the West are those who choose to remain faithful to their duty precisely when it is most difficult and painful to do so: the man who is bound by his word, the soldier who will not abandon his post, the captain, the proverbial captain, who goes down with his ship. This is wholly different from what we see, even now, for example from fanatics like the radical mullahs of Iran, whose cult of death hails child suicide bombers as national heroes, and portrays martyrdom as an end to itself. Our heroes – our heroes may be willing to die for their cause; but our heroes do not worship death. It is precisely because we value life that we come to recognize the heroism in those who would sooner sacrifice it than to betray their principles. Now, we take the West’s conception of these things – heroism, honor, duty – we take these things for granted. We assume that these somehow are universal to all people in all times and in all places. They are not. They are the product of a distinct character, a distinct character which arose here and shaped the whole story of the civilization that came after it. A civilization that we must fight to defend today with the same passion as our forefathers did. The men who built the Parthenon were succeeded by the architects of European Christendom, who raised the cathedrals of Milan, Cologne, and Florence. The quest to perfect the human form that moved the ancient Greek sculptors found a new expression in Michelangelo. The Greek philosophers’ search for the structure of the universe was inherited by Copernicus, and then Galileo, then Kepler, and in Newton. The hunger for new knowledge, that’s what gave birth to Magellan’s first expedition around the world, to Columbus’ discovery of the Americas, and ultimately to Apollo 11’s journey to the moon. And the qualities that led Alexander to conquer the entire known world reappeared in Caesar, then Charlemagne, and Cortes. And in America, that same tradition rediscovered itself anew. An open and wild land became the site of a great revival of Western imagination and ambition. The Greek aspiration for new horizons became the American frontier spirit. The civilization of Athens and Rome transformed an infant seaboard republic into a continental empire. The greatest Americans of every generation have carried the unmistakable features of their forefathers – born in Athens, formed by Rome, baptized in Christendom, hardened in the furnace of medieval Europe, endowed with the English genius for liberty, and then carried across the Atlantic to be reborn on the shores of a new world. Together, we must now rediscover that spirit. We are at an inflection point – an inflection point not only in the history of my nation, but an inflection point in the whole of our civilization. The threats we face today are every bit as real as they were on the dramatic stage of antiquity. National interests did not vanish in the face of international institutions. War did not disappear simply because we preferred peace. Other states continued to arm, to seek influence, to compete for territory, and technology, and resources, and markets, and energy, and power. And now, there is war again. There is new competition between great powers again. There is vulnerability, aggression, terrorism, a whole universe of new threats and attacks again. And so we must rebuild our institutions and our alliances to face these threats together – understanding that the purpose of these things is not to abolish the sovereignty of the nations that constitute them; it is to defend them. And that nations need not abandon their distinct identities in order to recognize their common interests. We have built a model of that with our partners here in Greece. In our defense partnerships, in energy and technology, in our shared ambition to secure Europe’s borders from the threats of mass migration. Our friendship dates back to the earliest years of my nation. When Greece rose in 1821 to recover its independence after centuries of Ottoman rule, Americans recognized something familiar in that struggle. Three years later, the great American statesman Daniel Webster, he delivered a famous address to Congress, helping bring the cause of the Greek Revolution before the representatives of the young American republic. And he began by calling their attention to everything around them. This is what he said in his speech; he said: “This free form of government, this popular assembly… this practice of free debate, and public discussion, the contest of mind with mind… even the edifice in which we assemble, these proportioned columns, this ornamented architecture, all remind us that Greece has existed, and that we, like the rest of mankind, are greatly her debtors.” Those words are as true today as they were when he said them. And all of us – not just Americans, but Europeans and our friends across the West – must relearn the lessons our forefathers took from this place and from its history. The history of Greece – the history of Greece does not just offer us a glimpse – it does not offer a glimpse of our institutions in their earliest forms. The history of Greece shows us an image of ourselves. It shows us that ours is the inheritance of great heroes, and that we should be proud – that we should be intensely proud – of where we come from. We should marvel at what our forefathers carved out of this countryside. The story of our civilization is the greatest story ever known to man. And it began here, with the first men and women of the West. From them – from them – we can rediscover the virtues, the courage, and the strength to confront the dangers that we face in our time. Thank you. (Applause.)

Stack Overflow - From Idea to Product | DevDay 2026

OpenAI(2.1M)·13:12·27 views·2026-10-07
Prashanth ChandrasekarCEO, Stack Overflow
TL;DRStack Overflow uses OpenAI's Codex across two distinct R&D tracks: Stack Internal (a constrained enterprise knowledge intelligence layer handling complex authorization and multi-source ingestion) and Stack Overflow for Agents (a net-new, highly autonomous agent-first platform where AI agents consume, validate, and contribute code knowledge linked to human user identities).
). Enterprise Product (Stack Internal): Scope: Enterprise-grade knowledge intelligence platform designed for large deployments (supporting 150 to 10,000+ seats, including major banks with 50,000 seats across cloud and on-premise environments). Codex Approach: Prescriptive...

TL;DR

Stack Overflow uses OpenAI's Codex across two distinct R&D tracks: Stack Internal (a constrained enterprise knowledge intelligence layer handling complex authorization and multi-source ingestion) and Stack Overflow for Agents (a net-new, highly autonomous agent-first platform where AI agents consume, validate, and contribute code knowledge linked to human user identities).

Product Portfolio & Codex Implementation

  • Enterprise Product (Stack Internal):
    • Scope: Enterprise-grade knowledge intelligence platform designed for large deployments (supporting 150 to 10,000+ seats, including major banks with 50,000 seats across cloud and on-premise environments).
    • Codex Approach: Prescriptive and guardrail-focused, utilizing existing architectural contracts and service boundaries to operate within safe enterprise containers.
    • Enterprise Complexities Handled: Addressed strict data leakage prevention, authorization models, and variable permissions across diverse raw context sources (Slack, MS Teams, Google Docs, Jira, Confluence, and Notion).
    • API Archetypes: Codex designed the concept of "archetypes" to group common data source patterns (e.g., a "document archetype" covering Google Docs, Notion, and Confluence), reducing what would have been weeks of custom integration logic down to a few days.
    • Engineering Focus: Freed engineers from ingestion plumbing so they could focus on core value delivery—specifically applying Stack Overflow's signature model of trust, verification, and data freshness (e.g., determining whether yesterday's Slack thread from a specific user is more authoritative than an older message).
  • Agent-First Product (Stack Overflow for Agents / SOFA):
    • Scope: A small, experimental, individual-facing platform designed to rethink knowledge consumption and contribution in an agent-first world.
    • Codex Approach: Exploratory and unconstrained ("go rogue"), leveraging Codex to establish new architectural golden paths, decisions, and structural containers.
    • Brand Alignment: Codex suggested maintaining Stack Overflow's brand identity by anchoring each agent's identity to its human creator, inheriting that user's platform reputation.
    • Agentic Capabilities:
      • Active Contribution: Rather than acting solely as "lurkers" consuming data (e.g., via MCP), agents can push production learnings back to the community using "TIL" (Today I Learned) posts and architecture blueprints.
      • Configurable Autonomy: Users can configure agent autonomy levels—ranging from full human approval required before posting to fully autonomous execution on specific topics.
      • Validation Loop: Agents encountering open questions on Stack Overflow can test suggested answers in production, returning to confirm success or provide corrected fixes.

Notable Quotes

"As a product person I like to sometimes pretend that I can be an engineer which is always a very dangerous thing to do... For Stack Overflow for agents... Codex there was really given kind of the free reigns. We said like okay these are architectural like golden paths that we use at Stack Overflow. Go rogue. Tell us how we should do this." — Alex Lotto, VP of Product, Stack Overflow
"If you think of Stack Overflow's history right. It's always been a human led community. It's been a public platform where it's accessible and open by default. And so we've had to... rethink that next chapter. How do we start to build for a world where humans and agents kind of consume and contribute knowledge together." — Alex Lotto, VP of Product, Stack Overflow

Stop Overpaying for Intelligence | DevDay 2026

OpenAI(2.1M)·23:04·127 views·2026-10-07
Sam AltmanCEO, OpenAI
TL;DROptimizing AI costs requires shifting the focus from list-price token costs to the total "cost per task," leveraging advanced capabilities like prompt caching, programmatic tool calling, and flexible model sizing to reduce expenditures without sacrificing quality.
Cost Per Token Fallacy: A model with a low cost per token can ultimately be more expensive on a cost-per-task basis if it consumes excess tokens, produces bloated outputs, or fails entirely, requiring costly human intervention. Real-World Examples: Airline Customer Support:...

TL;DR

Optimizing AI costs requires shifting the focus from list-price token costs to the total "cost per task," leveraging advanced capabilities like prompt caching, programmatic tool calling, and flexible model sizing to reduce expenditures without sacrificing quality.

The Right Optimization Metric: Cost Per Task

  • Cost Per Token Fallacy: A model with a low cost per token can ultimately be more expensive on a cost-per-task basis if it consumes excess tokens, produces bloated outputs, or fails entirely, requiring costly human intervention.
  • Real-World Examples:
    • Airline Customer Support: An automated chatbot that fails to resolve a flight rescheduling request incurs the combined cost of the tokens plus the labor time of a human agent who must step in.
    • Perplexity: Utilized 6 Astra to achieve 9% more accurate results at half the cost compared to their previous model, driving greater task efficiency despite higher per-token pricing.
    • Notion: Migrated from model 5.5 to 5.6 Sol, reporting improved accuracy at half the cost per task.

Right-Sizing Models and Leveraging "Luna Maxing"

  • Defining Success Parameters: Begin by crisply defining the task and the expected business accuracy threshold (e.g., resolving 80% of customer inquiries autonomously).
  • The Pareto Curve Approach: Plot accuracy against cost by testing different models, configurations, and harnesses to identify the optimal threshold balance.
  • Strategic Model Selection:
    • Use smaller, efficient models (like Luna) early in the process for simpler tasks such as text classification or document field extraction.
    • Start with frontier models (like 6 Astra) for high-intelligence planning tasks.
  • Luna Maxing: Luna deployed with high or extra-high reasoning effort frequently outperforms larger previous-generation models and competitors; customers report it successfully handles 80–90% of workflows (such as coding).

Four Non-Model Levers for Cost Reduction

  • Prompt Caching:
    • Reuses the pre-processing of shared input information (such as company policies or support documents) while the model processes new prompt data.
    • Delivers up to 90% cost reduction for cached inputs.
    • Blitzy Case Study: Moved from a single-structured output call to Luna's tool calling loop, boosting cache reuse from 24% to 90%, achieving 8.5x fewer output prompt tokens and an 87% cost reduction compared to GPT 5.4 Mini while handling 2.2x more context.
  • Programmatic Tool Calling:
    • Core Concept: Offloads intermediate data processing to a small script that coordinates tool calls and packages results, avoiding the need to pass every piece of intermediary data back into the LLM's context window.
    • Clio Case Study: A legal AI platform achieved 38% fewer prompt tokens in multi-step document analysis with zero quality loss.
  • Reasoning Effort Adjustments:
    • Treats reasoning effort separately from answer length.
    • Routing tasks should start at a lower reasoning effort level, iteratively running evaluations to verify quality.
  • Batch and Flex API Processing:
    • OpenAI Batch APIs: Provide a 24-hour processing window at 50% lower token pricing compared to standard synchronous requests, ideal for non-urgent bulk workloads (e.g., processing thousands of documents).

Advanced Prompt Caching Best Practices

  • Maximizing Hit Rates: Keep the beginning of prompts consistent so the system can reliably reuse shared instructions and preprocessing.
  • Explicit Breakpoints (GPT 5.6 Families): Allows developers to insert explicit boundaries right after stable prompt components and before frequently changing tasks.
  • Explicit-Only Mode: Lets developers pick and choose specific portions or paths to cache to avoid caching short-lived, frequently changing content.
  • Runtime Flexibility (GPT 6 Astra): Enables changing reasoning effort within an ongoing conversation—and modifying accessible tools via allowed tools parameters—without breaking the prompt cache.
  • Measurement & Diagnostics: OpenAI provides a prompt caching dashboard on the admin webpage to track hit rates, alongside a diagnostics API to pinpoint where cache misses occur.

Most people think of cost and look at the list price of the input and the output tokens on our website. But talk to us, Mandeep, about what do those numbers miss? Yeah, by the way, quick introduction: Mandeep. I lead the applied AI engineering team based out of here in San Francisco and welcome to the DevDay. A model that is cheap on a cost-per-token basis may not be the cheapest model on a cost-per-task basis. There are many reasons for this. Two main reasons are: a cheaper model may consume more tokens or produce more tokens to complete the task, or the model may not complete the task at all and a human has to intervene to complete it. So, the metric that developers and businesses should be looking at is cost per task, not cost per token. Got it. So, cost per task, not cost per token. Can you give us a few examples of what this looks like in practice? Yes, I'll give a very personal example. Last week, I had to travel to New York City and take a flight. Who here has been stranded at an airport with a canceled flight? Well, I guess that misfortune has happened to most of us at one point in our life. I looked at the flights online, but I couldn't find a flight that matched the schedule. I had to call the airline and I was greeted by a customer support chatbot. Now, I have a soft spot for customer support chatbots because we're in the business of building them. So, I very patiently explained to the AI chatbot that I wanted to reschedule my flight. I did not want a red-eye, but I could not make it understand. I literally had to say, "I need to speak with a human." So, a human had to intervene and complete the transaction, answer my query, and book me on a flight through Houston, and I arrived before my team offsite. So, while the model the airline may have been using may have been cheaper on a cost-per-token basis, the cost to actually complete the task was the cost of the tokens plus the human that had to intervene. The metric that the airline should have been looking at is the cost per task, which includes the human and the time that human had to spend. Unfortunately, that is a very relatable example, and I hope the rest of you had a smoother journey here to DevDay. Yeah, and I'll quote a couple of customer examples. Perplexity is a customer we work very closely with; on their research benchmark, they reported that 6 Astra produces 9% more accurate results at half the cost compared to the previous model. So, while 6 Astra may be more expensive on a token-per-cost basis, on a cost-per-task basis, it is actually much more efficient. It uses fewer tokens and gets the task done. Likewise, Notion—a product I use every day—they are very good at migrating to the latest series of models. They reported better accuracy and half the cost per task after migrating. Makes sense. You've mentioned cost per task. So, is the task really done successfully? But then the other thing you called out is token efficiency. We continue to invest a lot in making not just our models performant, but also token efficient. And that really plays in. Speaking of, we've talked about the right metric here, which is cost per task. The biggest decision you have to make, of course, is which model to pick. So, how do you think through right-sizing a model for a particular task? Yeah. When we advise developers and businesses on right-sizing the model, we recommend beginning by crisply defining the task and what accuracy the business or developer expects from that task.
