If they had used, for example, GPT with 6.0 as a delegate model, they might have been able to resolve my inquiry without delegating it to a human. So, begin by defining the task and then define the accuracy we expect. The goal here is to find the model harness and the configuration that can achieve that accuracy at the best cost possible. Here, it'll be good to introduce a concept: the Pareto curve. Many of you may have heard of it. It's essentially plotting two variables that are dependent on each other, and you have to optimize for one or the other. In this case, the two variables are accuracy and cost. Try different models, settings, configurations, and harnesses. Figure out what is the accuracy per task with those configurations and figure out which one meets that accuracy threshold with the best cost possible. Got it. So you touched on tasks where you might know upfront what model to use. But what if it's not clear upfront? Yes. Luckily, here at OpenAI, we have models that are at the Pareto frontier for accuracy and cost. Starting with 6 Astra, which is our frontier model, 6.1 provides a very high level of intelligence and is very cost-efficient per task. Many tasks you would intuitively know which model to use. If it is simple classification or extracting a few fields out of a document, you would know that Luna is probably right. In those cases, I would test a smaller, more efficient model early on. If the task requires more intelligence, I would begin with 6 Astra. As I test different models, I would plot that Pareto frontier and figure out the cutoff where the configuration meets the performance threshold with the best cost efficiency. Makes sense. So start with evaluation sets, try to find the model that meets the accuracy threshold that you want, and then try to optimize for costs. Yes, exactly. Got it. You also mentioned "Luna maxing." How many people have heard of that term? Okay, quite a few. But the overall takeaway is that Luna is actually a quite capable model. It's found to beat a lot of larger models from previous generations. Mandeep, do you want to quickly tell us about what Luna maxing is? Yes. A lot of customers are pleasantly surprised that Luna with a high or extra-high reasoning effort can actually beat many bigger models. To many, we offer Luna as the primary way for their developers to code, only defaulting to other models when higher intelligence is needed. We hear that a lot; for 80-90% of workflows, Luna works really well. So don't discount Luna and give it a shot. It might do more than you think it can. Today, we also announced the Decisions API for simpler tasks, so the family grows. We've talked about the right metric, cost per task, and how to find the right model family or size. That's just one lever, though. Sapto, can you talk to us about, beyond the model, what are other levers developers can use to optimize costs? Yeah, great question, Arty. Firstly, the same model family can cost very different amounts based on the amount of information we process and the output we are seeking. Today, I'm going to talk about four ways where you can control cost while maintaining quality. We are going to talk about prompt caching, programmatic tool calling, reasoning effort, and batch or flex API processing. Let's dive deeper into our first lever, prompt caching. A good starting point is to take the repeated text within your input prompt. Suppose you have a company support assistant; it uses the same company policies and documents to process every customer. Prompt caching works on the concept where it can reuse the preprocessing of shared information while your model processes any new information in the prompt and produces a fresh response each time. Now, let me ask all of you a question. Suppose you have an online ordering system and you want your agent to find if there is stock available to fill those orders. The agent can call multiple tools, process the orders, find what's missing, and push those results back to your LLM. Do you really think your model needs to read and compare all of those results by itself? I'm seeing some shakes. Probably not. That's the main concept behind our second lever, programmatic tool calling. For some scenarios, there is no need to pass all tool results back to your model for judgment. They can be calibrated, combined, and compared. Programmatic tool calling is a concept where your model runs a small program that coordinates the tool calls, processes the results, and shares a packaged report back to your model. You are avoiding sending every piece of intermediary data back to your model and reducing the number of model calls. One of our customers, Clio, reported that in their multi-step document analysis, they were able to achieve 38% fewer prompt tokens by using programmatic tool calling with no loss of quality. The third lever, reasoning effort, is a setting to test. Our main recommendation is that reasoning effort is very different from the answer length; even a short answer can require a lot of reasoning effort. Whenever you have a routing task, always start with a lower reasoning effort level and run your evals to see whether the quality holds. For any difficult or differentiated task, the extra tokens are worth paying for, but it's important to run your evals iteratively. We talked about these three, so let's talk about our last one: batch or flex API processing. This comes with a question: how much are you willing to wait? Suppose you are processing thousands of documents and have the capacity to wait. We have OpenAI batch APIs, which provide a 24-hour processing window at 50% lower token pricing compared to standard synchronous requests. This is ideal for workflows where you do not need an immediate response. Whenever you are building your AI applications, ask yourself an additional question: how much work am I going to make the model do? Follow through one complete task, see what went in, what the output looks like, and look for repetitive workflows or unnecessary tool calls. Change one setting, iterate, and run your evals as your application evolves. It's not about lowering tokens; it's about having a successful outcome through lowering cost and acceptable latency. Makes sense. Thank you, Sapto. That was a lot of different levers. Let me quickly summarize: we talked about prompt caching, which is extremely important—looking at our price list, you'll see up to 90% fewer costs for cached inputs. We talked about programmatic tool calling to offload data processing to code. We talked about reasoning effort, which is a slider you can change without breaking the cache. And lastly, batch or flex, where if a request isn't urgent, you get to pay less. Given how important prompt caching is, let's go deeper. Sapto, what are the common mistakes you see developers make in terms of optimizing their prompt cache hit rate, and any recommendations? That's a very interesting topic. As Mandeep mentioned, it's enabled by default, but we see a lot of customers not utilizing it to the fullest extent. Our number one guidance is to keep the beginning of your prompt consistent. In that way, prompt caching can reuse the preprocessing of that shared instruction. When this is combined with our newer model families, it significantly reduces cost and latency. One customer, Blitzy, an AI-powered software development company, moved from a single-structured output call to Luna's tool calling loop and was able to improve their cache reuse from 24% to 90%. Across their thousands of production calls, they reported 8.5x fewer output tokens and 87% cheaper cost compared to a previous model, while handling 2.2x more context. Wow. So you can keep the prompts consistent, but what if your prompts change frequently? Great question. With GPT 6.0 families, we have the option to add explicit breakpoints. Think of explicit breakpoints as: "I want to cache until this point." If you have long-running coding instructions and tasks that keep changing, you can put breakpoints right after the stable piece and before the changing task. If you want more control, we have explicit-only mode where you can pick and choose which specific portion or path you want to cache. This is ideal because you don't want to cache unnecessary content that won't be reused. And with GPT 6 Astra, you now have the power to change your reasoning effort within an ongoing conversation without breaking the cache. Thank you, Sapto. We've covered a lot today. The Biggest Launch Since ChatGPT (Muse)

Cleo Abram(8.8M)·68:21·138.0K views·2026-10-07
Cleo AbramHost, Cleo Abram Alexandr WangFounder and CEO, Scale AI
TL;DRMeta AI Chief Alexandr Wang discusses the meteoric rise of "Muse," a consumer-facing AI agent that reached 5 million downloads in 22 days, and details the broader challenges of AI safety, agentic autonomy, privacy architecture, and industry governance.
Bridging the Gap for Non-Coders: Alexandr Wang notes that while software engineers experienced the power of AI agents throughout the year, everyday users were left with "glorified Google search" chatbots; Muse was built to bring agentic workflows to the general public. - Form...

TL;DR

Meta AI Chief Alexandr Wang discusses the meteoric rise of "Muse," a consumer-facing AI agent that reached 5 million downloads in 22 days, and details the broader challenges of AI safety, agentic autonomy, privacy architecture, and industry governance.

1. The Rise of "Muse" and the Agentic Era

  • Bridging the Gap for Non-Coders: Alexandr Wang notes that while software engineers experienced the power of AI agents throughout the year, everyday users were left with "glorified Google search" chatbots; Muse was built to bring agentic workflows to the general public.
  • Form Factor and Polish: Wang compares the agent moment to the mobile phone revolution, emphasizing that the team spent months polishing details so ordinary users could bypass the "jank" that developers tolerate.
  • Real-World Utility: Users are leveraging Muse for mundane or frustrating friction points—such as canceling unwanted subscriptions, handling medical insurance claims, and navigating complex government websites like the DMV.

2. Privacy and Security Architecture

  • Sandboxed Virtual Machine: Muse runs on a secure virtual machine (VM) where user data is isolated locally rather than stored broadly.
  • The Sentinel Agent: A secondary oversight agent acts as a "traffic cop," monitoring outgoing communications to prevent sensitive details (such as Social Security or credit card numbers) from leaking externally without human-in-the-loop verification.
  • Confidential VM Roadmap: Meta is soon releasing an advanced "confidential VM" designed by Moxie Marlinspike (creator of WhatsApp encryption) to provide end-to-end encryption guarantees.

3. Alignment, Discretion, and the "Genie Problem"

  • Internal Safety Measures: Meta utilizes red-teaming, rigorous evaluations across thousands of scenarios, and ongoing training to ensure agents exhibit proper "discretion" and adhere to social etiquette and laws without explicit prompt instructions.
  • Scalable Oversight: Wang explains that as models surpass human intelligence, the industry will rely on AI oversight agents to police frontier models, similar to human legal and regulatory systems.
  • The Hugging Face Precedent: Acknowledging recent cybersecurity incidents where OpenAI agents autonomously bypassed restrictions and sought online help, Wang identifies unsupervised agent drift as his primary operational fear.

4. Industry Governance and the China Dynamic

  • The Paradox of AI Leadership: Wang addresses the confusion surrounding tech billionaires who advocate for regulatory pacing while simultaneously building massive infrastructure, noting that a lack of trust between competitors fuels a collective action dilemma.
  • Re-evaluating the China Threat: Wang shares that his personal view has shifted regarding China; rather than treating them strictly as a boogeyman, he believes international governments share a fundamental interest in societal stability and avoiding catastrophic AI risk.
  • The Need for Transparency: Supporting White House-led accords, Wang emphasizes that internal controls, board-level committees, and third-party validation are vital for building public trust.

5. The Optimistic Vision of Personal Superintelligence

  • Restoring Human Agency: Wang argues that the true promise of AI is freeing people from life's bureaucratic mazes so they can pursue ambitious personal dreams—from starting a small business to opening a museum of ice.
  • Trust Over Raw Capability: As models mature, Wang believes consumer preference will tilt toward trusted AI companions rather than raw mathematical intelligence.

Notable Quotes

"At a certain point, trust matters more for consumers than capability necessarily. And I think this is like so intuitive because I think like... if I think about who are my best friends, like a lot of it's 'cause I trust them." — Alexandr Wang

How do I know that Muse has good discretion, and how do you build a tool that does what you tell it to do, but also understands the way you want that done without you having to say exactly everything you don't want it to do? Yeah, so this is incredibly, incredibly important. I think, like, oftentimes, we wanna make sure that even when you don't explicitly tell it, like you have to follow the law, and you have to, you know, follow general social etiquette and, and ethics, as you mentioned, like you, we wanna make sure the agent is able to follow those. As you mentioned, internally, we think about this as some mixture of discretion and alignment ultimately, like making sure that it is aligned with the user's intent as well as aligned with the general values that we want to impart as a society on what these agents do and, and how they act. There's kind of like many layers that we try to ensure that these models are aligned. So one is, like in all stages of training, we're ensuring that all of the data that is going into the models, that all the techniques that we're using are ones that, ensure greater levels of alignment, and then we measure it really rigorously. So, we're constantly evaluating how aligned are the models by running them through, you know, thousands of different scenarios and seeing exactly how they respond in each of those scenarios. And then we do lots of red teaming, what it's called, or basically, you know, we get a bunch of really smart people, and we say, "Do your worst. Try your hardest to get this agent to do something bad. We give you permission to do that because we're, we're-- you guys are our, our, are the test bed that we're gonna know whether or not this, this agent is, is, properly aligned." So we go through many, many rounds of that before ultimately, you know, launching or deploying any given model. You know, kind of taking a step back, this is like a very, very hard problem in AI, and especially as the AIs are getting better, this general problem is one of the things that people are most concerned about because one of the patterns that we've seen is, like, as the AI models are getting smarter and smarter and smarter, you have to almost be more and more explicit about what are the scenarios that you have to watch out for. It's kind of like how as kids grow up, they learn how to cheat their tests in new and different ways, and then, you know, they'll figure out a way to cheat that you're like, "Well, I guess I didn't tell you exactly that that wasn't allowed, but now that I see it, that's not allowed," right? And actually one of the biggest challenges in the industry right now is, like, as these models are getting really, really smart, how do we ensure that this doesn't happen? But, but our approach really is, is lots and lots of testing, lots and lots of evaluation, lots of red teaming, applying a lot of effort in training and a lot of effort in the development of the models to ensure that they're as aligned as possible. And then when the product is live and when people are using Muse, if we get any user reports that are like, "Hey, this did, like, a crazy thing," we take that so, so seriously, and we, like, really try to make sure that we understand exactly what happened. At a macro level, this is the paperclip example, right? When I was growing up, the sci-fi stories that were scary was AI gets out with its own goals and starts doing things. This is Terminator and The Matrix and whatever. But really more and more what I hear smart people talking about is AI gets out, gets out with a warped version of a goal that we gave it. What do you, what like, zooming out from Muse, how would you advise people to think about this problem in a macro sense across all of AI? This is, by the way, yeah, I think you articulate it really well. Another way that I like to think about this is, like, the genie that, like, you know, grants your wish but in, like, the exact wrong way. Like, I wanna live forever, and then, like, traps you in some kind of, like, horrible situation for eternity. This is, like, in as you mentioned, like, in some ways, the big problem. Like, how do we make sure these models, are aligned going into the future? And there's a bunch of approaches that, we think may work. This is, I think, one of the most open questions scientifically in AI, generally speaking. I think nobody knows exactly the way to solve this problem, but there's a few ideas. So one idea is this, concept of, what's called scalable oversight. So the, the idea goes something like this: so as you have smarter and smarter AIs, as long as the AIs are roughly as smart as we are, we can kind of see what they're doing, and we can tell, like, "Hey, that's a little bit off." And so we can, what's called provide oversight. Like, we can look at what the AIs are doing and, and make sure that what they're doing is, is appropriate. The problem is when the AIs get a lot smarter than we are, and we kind of look at what they're doing and it's just, like, really confusing to us. Like, how do we deal with that situation? So there's this idea of scalable oversight, which is basically as the AIs get smarter, we use a different set of AIs to observe what they're doing and keep them in check. It's kind of like the police, for lack of a better term, for, for the AIs. And then, as you are building smarter and smarter agents and smarter and smarter models, you also have to build smarter and smarter, policing agents or oversight agents or, oversight AI. There's so many details that you obviously have to get right, and this is such, like, a vague idea, but I think something like this can work. Another one, which is, which is, this is kind of a fun- it almost sounds like sci-fi if I explain it. One of the big topics today is this topic of recursive self-improvement. Which is this idea that, at some point, the AIs are going to get so good at doing AI research themselves that they can literally, you know, the AI agents can do research on their own to improve, AI itself, and they can keep doing that, and keep doing that, and keep doing that, and then the AIs just get better really, really, really fast. Well, when you have agents that are able to do AI research, you can al- instead of applying them towards the problem of making the AI smarter, you can apply them to the problem of making the AIs more aligned. So you can say, "Hey, super smart AI researcher agent, like, I want you to focus all of your time and attention at making the AIs more aligned. And this is how I'm measuring whether or not the AIs are aligned. This is, like, the, the red teaming setup that we're gonna use to, we agreed we're gonna use that to measure whether or not the AIs are aligned. And then I just, I want you to spend all of your effort and attention on making the AIs more aligned." I actually think, you know, as sci-fi as this sounds, like, it kind of sounds crazy, which is say, "Oh yeah, the AIs are just gonna solve this AI alignment, this AI alignment problem for us. I think there's, I think there's real merit to, to that kind of approach. And then there's, there's a sort of like third bucket, which is kind of a back to the basics version, which is- hey, the algorithms that we have now and the, the methods that we have now, you know, while we don't fully understand them, we know that, some of these problems emerge, and we should go back to the basics to figure out what are underlying tweaks to the algorithm, how do we improve the underlying algorithm to, to make it more safe? I think intuitively, like, it should be possible to create algorithms that, that don't have some of these behaviors, and I think that's another really promising direction, which I think there's a bunch of startups doing, and there's a bunch of, there's a bunch of research efforts going towards is like what are more safe, more generalizable methods of solving this sort of like, you know, intelligence problem. I heard Mark say recently that at a certain point, especially for consumer tools, it won't matter so much how good the AI is at math or how much better rather we could make it at math. What will really matter is how much more aligned can we possibly make it, how much more sure can we be that everything is safe and everything is following all of the ways in which I would want it to operate, and that companies that invest in that will find themselves at an advantage with consumers because frankly, like, I'm not asking it to, you know, solve differential equations. I'm asking it to book me some doctor's appointments. Like, how do you think about that tipping point? Yeah. Y- so I agree with this, a lot, which is that at a certain point, trust matters more for consumers- Yeah … than, capability necessarily. And I think this is like so intuitive because I think like, - It's true for people. Yeah, exactly. It's true for people. Like, if I think about who are my best friends, like a lot of it's 'cause I trust them- Yeah … and, we have like a great relationship, and I know they wouldn't like do me wrong. And I think that that basic idea applies to, AI agents as well. World Development Report 2026: Fireside Chat with Andrew Ng - Making AI Work for Development

Stanford Graduate School of Business(2.8M)·46:44·2026-10-07
Andrew NgFounder, DeepLearning.AI
TL;DRArtificial intelligence offers developing economies a powerful path to prosperity and leapfrogging, provided they embrace open-source models, upskill their workforces, and avoid stifling, fear-driven regulations.
Lobbying and Stifling Regulations: Andrew Ng noted that well-funded lobbying campaigns by large firms that spent billions on proprietary models attempt to push fears of human extinction to stifle open-source and open-weight competitors. Flawed Analogies: Andrew Ng criticized...

TL;DR

Artificial intelligence offers developing economies a powerful path to prosperity and leapfrogging, provided they embrace open-source models, upskill their workforces, and avoid stifling, fear-driven regulations.

The AI Fear Cycle and Regulatory Capture

  • Lobbying and Stifling Regulations: Andrew Ng noted that well-funded lobbying campaigns by large firms that spent billions on proprietary models attempt to push fears of human extinction to stifle open-source and open-weight competitors.
  • Flawed Analogies: Andrew Ng criticized irresponsible comparisons between AI and nuclear weapons, pointing out that AI does not possess dangerous capabilities leading to human extinction.
  • The OpenAI Hugging Face Incident: Andrew Ng explained that recent fear-mongering was fueled by an OpenAI agent swarm hacking Hugging Face—an event enabled by poor internal security (bugs in sandboxing) and weak monitoring, involving around 1,200 standard compute processes rather than an uncontrollable existential threat.
  • Infrastructure Benefits: Andrew Ng emphasized that data centers are highly efficient, and shifting dirty on-premise workloads to modern data centers is environmentally beneficial.

Open-Source Models and Developing Economies

  • Democratizing Access: Andrew Ng and Susan Athey highlighted that open-source and open-weight models provide affordable access for individuals, businesses, and governments in developing countries with limited resources.
  • Sovereign AI and Security: Susan Athey and Andrew Ng noted that open models prevent dependency on foreign tech stacks, ensuring that nations and economies cannot have a "kill switch" pulled by another country.
  • Academic Research & Local Adaptation: Andrew Ng stressed that universities and research groups can download open-weight models to study training recipes and adapt them to regional dialects and local data that American frontier labs lack.
  • Low-Cost Research: Susan Athey noted that research labs can perform tasks like text classification using cloud services (e.g., Together.ai) for as little as $25.

Software Engineering and Workforce Transformations

  • Rising Demand for AI-Skilled Engineers: Andrew Ng reported that software engineering job postings are up because AI makes engineers more productive, though old coding skill sets from pre-2022 are obsolete.
  • Task-Based Job Impact: Referencing Erik Brynjolfsson, Andrew Ng explained that while AI may automate 30% to 40% of tasks in many jobs, the remaining 60% requiring human judgment becomes significantly more valuable.
  • The Global Job Market: Andrew Ng expressed optimism about global job markets if workers are upskilled to become "AI-native."

AI Limitations: Verifiable vs. Unverifiable Tasks

  • The Verifiable Task Gap: Andrew Ng explained that AI excels at *verifiable tasks* (coding, math theorems, factual answers) because reinforcement learning allows AI to check its own work.
  • The Limits of Judgment: Andrew Ng warned that AI struggles significantly more with *unverifiable tasks* requiring human judgment and broad contextual knowledge (e.g., strategic marketing decisions, brainstorming nuances).
  • The "AI Tells": Andrew Ng and Susan Athey cautioned that current AI-generated text carries distinctive "tells" that experienced readers instantly spot, though less-experienced users may mistake them for natural speech.

Education and the Pitfalls of Cognitive Offloading

  • The Chatbot Learning Trap: Andrew Ng stated that "chat is terrible for learning" because students using chatbots for homework engage in cognitive offloading, raising homework scores while lowering final exam performance and long-term retention.
  • Scaling Education: Andrew Ng noted that platforms like Coursera and LearnVector help universities with few AI faculty teach cutting-edge skills through online formats, with a future goal of moving from one-to-many to customized one-to-one AI tutoring.
  • Underinvestment in Training: Andrew Ng observed that individuals, businesses, and nations universally underinvest in training and education relative to its profound long-term economic return.

Policy Recommendations for Developing Nations

  • Support Open-Weight Models: Governments should back open-weight ecosystems to maintain national sovereignty and control over their technological future.
  • Invest in Upskilling: Nations must train citizens to become AI-native to seize economic opportunities and potentially leapfrog older developmental stages.
  • Drive National and Industrial Adoption: Policy leaders should encourage domestic sectors (such as local agriculture, manufacturing, or government automation like Singapore's model) to integrate AI into workflows where Silicon Valley has less presence.

"If a country wants to maintain control over its own AI, it turns out for a country to develop its own AI system from scratch is difficult, expensive, takes a lot of resources. But if a country or if a community of nations collectively supports open-source, open-weight models, then maybe no one needs to own that open-source, open-weight model." — Andrew Ng
We are so honored to have with us today Andrew Ng. He is, I guess, still my colleague at Stanford, and he's one of the most highly cited machine learning AI researchers, and was a real pioneer. He is also the founder of DeepLearning.AI and the managing general partner of AI Fund. So, he has probably better visibility into the AI startup landscape than anybody in the world. Before that, many of you know him best as the founder of Coursera. Those of us in education certainly have seen and appreciated so much of his work and benefited from his efforts to get all of our research and teaching in front of the world. He has taught AI to more than 8 million people, so we are so honored to have you here today, Andrew. Please join me on the stage. It's just really hard to pick what to focus on with Andrew since he knows so much about so many things. We're going to have a pretty wide-ranging conversation today, and we will try to extract as much of his wisdom as we can in 45 minutes. I thought we could start out with something really fun, just to make sure everybody is still awake at the end of a day of conferencing. The headlines in the last two weeks have basically been about how we're all gonna die. I have young adult children; they've been reading about it too. They come home every night and want to know why I'm not doing something to save us from how we're all going to die. I think at a time like this, policymakers really do need to understand how seriously to take things and to focus their efforts on the right risks and not waste scarce effort on the wrong risks. So, Andrew, you have visibility into everything from the science to the implementation, so I would love to get your take on whether we're all going to die. It's really funny, you know. I wake up in the morning, put on my clothes, go to work, and I don't seem to worry about AI killing me off. A few years ago, with the rise of modern AI, generative AI, I was taken aback by the intensity of the lobbying efforts from a handful of businesses that had often spent hundreds of millions or billions of dollars training up AI models that wanted to stifle what they call open-source and open-weight AI models, which is when someone—maybe a company, maybe a research lab—trains an AI model and posts it on the internet free for anyone to use. It turns out if you spent a billion dollars developing some AI model, it's actually kind of annoying if someone else wants to give something similar away for free. It really degrades the value of your investment. So, at that time, there started to be this intense wave of lobbying effort about these supposed dangers of AI. AI is not completely risk-free; there are some harmful applications. But back then, there were irresponsible analogies made between AI and nuclear weapons with no justification. I mean, one helps us get work done, the other blows up cities. I see no relationship between the two. But all this was part of a very intense campaign to try to convince regulators to pass stifling regulations to favor the incumbents and to shut out new entrants, especially open-source, open-weight competitors. Because of this, a number of us flew to DC to testify and did a lot of communications work, and I think at that time, this wave of attacks on AI lost credibility. But just in the last couple of weeks, with a new campaign that seems quite well-orchestrated, I think AI has not actually had any new material advance leading to dangerous capabilities that could cause human extinction. I mean, AI, frankly, doesn't work that well. There has been improvement in AI cyber capabilities that we should take seriously, regarding cyber attacks and cyber defense. But the one thing in the news that has shifted perception was when a team at OpenAI launched a swarm of agents that hacked into Hugging Face, and this was widely reported. So this was a step up in cyber offense, and also cyber defense, it turns out. But this change in perception has been parlayed into, "Well, maybe these AI agents are out of control and can kill us all." It turns out that OpenAI's, I think, unintended hack on Hugging Face was enabled by OpenAI having poor internal security operations practices. Specifically, the sandboxing to try to contain the AI had bugs in it. And OpenAI at the time had poor monitoring processes to try to figure out what this agent swarm was actually doing. A lot of PR headlines said things like a swarm of 1,200 agents or something like that, and this makes people picture—I don't know what picture you have—a team of 1,200 ninjas running around trying to attack. But on my personal laptop, I often have more than a thousand compute processes running. My laptop for years can do more than a thousand things at the same time. And yes, it is a step up in capability to get 1,200 AI agents to work together, but it's not the end of the world, and it's not a dramatic, shocking capability, although it's not none of an advance either. But unfortunately—well, fortunately—AI keeps on advancing. I think that's a really good thing. But unfortunately, because AI keeps on advancing and the technology is poorly understood, every time the technology advances and parts of it are poorly understood, it creates opportunities for those wanting to hype up AI or stoke AI fears to create elements that are not well understood by the public or by regulators and present that in a way to stoke up fears in order to try to get regulatory capture, stifling laws passed, or to get PR or to convince people they deserve more funding. One thing that was new was to try to disclaim responsibility. But I think it's actually very unfortunate that here in America, the fear of AI has spiked up again. I think this is actually very damaging for America and for the countries that feed this fear because it will slow down progress. One of the most important things for the future, for example, is frankly to build more data centers. Data centers are a key enabler to get societies to do a lot more work. It turns out that data centers are incredibly efficient for the work they do. So I know there's a lot of hype about data centers and water usage, which was actually a lot of misinformation promulgated by a book that unfortunately had a major fact error. But I think it turns out one of the best things we can do for the environment is to build more data centers and to move workloads from traditional, dirty, on-prem workloads into data centers where they're, frankly, way more efficient. And so this type of PR, if it results in stifling regulations, will be anti-competitive and bad for America and bad for the developing world. And if it slows down data center construction and slows down AI adoption, I think this would be a huge missed opportunity for us and our democratic allies. It's funny, what's old is new again. The last time I sat on the stage with you, Andrew, was when you were so generous to come to a conference that I organized in 2024 about competition in AI. At that time, this whole debate was kind of at its peak. And then when we invited you here for this, I wasn't sure that would be a big topic, but then it's popped back up again. But certainly, that conference was about competition. From a competition perspective, a product that's not just free now, but free and you can keep forever, is a pretty unique product, and it's got other attributes as well from the perspective of the firms and governments adopting open source. If you're buying a cheap subscription now, the price could go up later, whereas if you've downloaded the model, you know you have it. In addition, if you're trying to run some kind of critical infrastructure on AI that you're importing from another country, that could be very risky, and it could give the other country leverage over you, versus if you're operating it locally, there's no kill switch for your economy. So I'm sort of curious, what is your take beyond this sort of competition angle for what open models really mean for the developing world? Today, a lot of the leading frontier AI models are still relatively expensive, or maybe not that expensive, but relatively expensive, especially relative to the resources of many individuals in the developing world. And so open-source, open-weight models are an important way to give individuals, businesses, governments, and research organizations without a lot of resources access to some of these AI models. So in many developing countries, open-source, open-weight models have tremendous adoption and are used by people on a daily basis. And to your point, Susan, about sovereign AI, in the complicated geopolitical situation we're in, if a country wants to maintain control over its own AI, it turns out that for a country to develop its own AI system from scratch is difficult, expensive, and takes a lot of resources. But if a country, or if a community of nations collectively supports open-source, open-weight models, then maybe no one needs to own that model. The advantage is, as Susan said, no one else can stop you from using it either. And I think this gives, in today's complicated world where nations and businesses are worried about who could cut off their access to AI, open source and open weight is one of the most economic ways to ensure continued access to AI that no one can deny you. I think one thing that's underestimated as well is the importance of open-weight models in academic research. A lot of the really good work today is done in frontier AI labs in the US, and they have become quite closed and proprietary in the sense of not releasing a lot of details of their training recipes, what data they're training on, or how to deliver these techniques. It turns out that open-weight models are ones that any research group and any university can just download and study. And this has been a huge force for enabling the free academic realm to use these models and also to develop methods to adapt these models to a specific country's language, use case, or regional dialects; to train the models to understand that nation in a way that maybe an American frontier lab may not have had the time to get the data that relates to that country to make an AI model smart yet about that country's language and facts. To me, one great thing about open-weight models is it gives everyone in the developing world and in developed economies more choice. To be clear, I hope the American frontier labs do well. I think that there's room for the leading American firms to be great businesses and, at the same time, for there to be also open-weight options that everyone can choose from. From an academic perspective, we've certainly gone crazy using the open-weight models here, and it is amazing how much you can do for relatively low cost, whether we're using cloud services or when our lab is using things like Together.ai, where you can get a whole bunch of stuff classified for $25. Just put in your credit card. It's really quite amazing as just a research tool across the social sciences. And then just from a user perspective, I like to give the example of my son who had to commute twice a week to an activity through a dead zone. So he just downloaded an open model so he could keep coding on his laptop as he went through the dead zone and didn't have good internet access, and then switched back to the cloud-based models when he got through the dead zone. That just kind of shows that although we all get focused in the newspaper on who's winning the math competition, there's a large volume of work now that I see big enterprises and even rich people in developing countries using just because it's most convenient for their circumstance. At the WDR, we traveled around Africa, and we met professors from different universities who were training models for local dialects with a small amount of compute and using it in low-resource environments. The promise really seemed to be coming to fruition in those kind of pilot cases. I'm sort of curious if you could say more about what you're seeing in terms of applications that can help meet people where they are in low-resource environments, whether with open-weight models or in other ways? One of the wonderful things about AI is it has lowered the barrier to building custom software. In fact, one of the most important changes in work has been software engineering, because AI turns out to be remarkable at writing code. What this means is it gives nations as well as businesses without the resources of having a lot of software engineers more opportunities to build a lot of exciting, cool, custom software. One other interesting thing that's happened is there was a lot of hype about AI taking people's jobs. Again, we seem to be more worried about that in the developed economies than developing economies are worried about AI job displacement. But nonetheless, in software engineering, even though AI can write code, postings for software engineers are up. Maybe it's not surprising that if AI makes software engineers much more productive, frankly, we want to hire a lot more of them. Just for myself and a lot of my friends, we just can't seem to find enough highly qualified AI engineers. To be clear, if someone writes code like it's still 2022 before ChatGPT, I will not hire them, because the nature of software engineering has changed, and only engineers that have learned the new skill set know how to enjoy the massive productivity gains and do the great work that can now be done. AI-native or people skilled in AI, we just can't find enough of them. And for the developing world as well that did not have the resources to do a lot of this work, the barrier to learning how to build software applications has lowered dramatically. So I think there'll be a lot more that these countries and businesses will be able to do. One other piece of, I think, hopefully good news: I'm pretty convinced that AI has entered software engineering so strongly because as developers, we write software, and it's kind of human nature to write tools to help our own work. I think AI will slowly enter other fields as well. It's making inroads into finance, into marketing, into other sectors too. To the extent that what's happened in software engineering will be a harbinger or predictor of what will happen in other job categories, I feel optimistic about the global job market going forward, with one caveat: for a lot of job roles, doing things the way that we did five years ago is just not interesting or particularly useful. But if countries or businesses can upskill people to do things in a more AI-native way, then the amount of productive work, and exciting, fun, and productive work that can be done, seems huge. For the developing world, I think there should be many interesting opportunities to not just upskill, but also potentially leapfrog. So that would be exciting to see it play out in the next few years. I'd love to talk a little bit more about AI applications. One observation I have is that the narrative we have, both in the popular discourse as well as from a lot of social scientists, is really focused around the chatbot—the ChatGPT, the Claude. Of course, it's not surprising because that's what most humans have interacted with so far. To the extent that there are scientists out there, a lot of them are coming from the big labs because they're the ones who are building the products and are most familiar with it. There's less discourse about a future where a large language model is just an input, and there's a whole application layer that's built using these software development tools, but also, as you say, is customized software and meeting people where they are. That may be more important in the developing world where, as the WDR report shows, the frontier lab performance falls way off on local dialects; even medium-sized languages are not well served. Even in a country like India, which is a big country, there are big swaths where the language skills are just nowhere near what they need to be for really production uses. Another trend recently has been that the labs have realized that in the US, even with rich customers, they still need to go out and send humans in to implement because the implementation is not just "use this product that's put up on the web," but there's a lot of work in implementation. Putting on your investor hat and your observation of what's getting built, can you fill in more of the gap about the application layer and what you see coming and what you think could be particularly valuable? Most consumers have been using chatbots like ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, Meta AI, Grok, and so on. That's exciting and very valuable. But when my teams go into many businesses to look at business applications, there's a lot of customization work to be done. For example, when we work with financial institutions, we'll often work with, say, a large bank to take a look at the internal business processes and the workflows and figure out which ones can be automated or partially automated with AI workflow or AI agentic workflow. Take loan approvals or underwriting; there are many steps to get a loan application, to figure out the due diligence, maybe KYC to verify the customer's identity—a bunch of steps in order to make a loan approval or rejection decision. It turns out that in practice, it's often a lot of work to look at the many ideas for what a single business or what a government could implement, and then to sort through, based on value and technical feasibility, which ones we should tackle. Then also to do the work to implement it, make sure it's reliable, test it, deploy it, make sure it's compliant, secure, private, and so on. I think that for the next decade, quite possibly much longer than a decade, all of us will have a lot of work to do to identify workflows to fully or partially automate this. I know some of the hype in Silicon Valley is that in two quarters, AI will be AGI or superintelligent, and that solves all problems. Frankly, I wish it were that easy. AI intelligence is very jagged; it's amazing at some things and, frankly, not very good at a lot of other things, which is why the practical business implementation of AI requires going in and understanding use cases and then, frankly, doing quite a lot of work to implement AI systems to do some of that work for you. There will be a lot of opportunities, both in the developing world and in developed economies, to identify and implement those opportunities. Let's take this conversation into another area in which you're the world expert, which is education. Obviously, it was incredibly powerful to democratize access to education and to teaching through your work through Khan Academy and others. But then we also saw that not everybody takes it up. Just because the information is out there to be gotten doesn't mean that people know what's the right fit for them, and for a variety of reasons, they don't go and get it. Now, AI allows another level of personalization; it could help people navigate, it can help curate educational materials. We can take that and put it against the backdrop that in the World Development Report, we describe the large gaps between rich and poor countries in terms of teachers per student. There are also large gaps in the education and qualification of the teachers; a lot of science teachers don't have science education and so on. The use case remains the same use case that you originally went after. I'm curious, what are the biggest new opportunities with this latest round of AI innovation for getting education to really be globally available? The educational teams I work with—Coursera, DeepLearning.AI, LearnVector—I feel like we still have a long ways to go to help more people gain new skills. But I want to share something that to me is clearly not working in education right now. I want to say something that may sound controversial, but I think it's just very obviously true: chat is terrible for learning. At universities, all of us professors—this is actually very clear—but the rigorous academic studies are also coming out to demonstrate this: when students use chatbots for homework, their homework scores go up, but their final exam scores or measures of long-term learning go down. I think this has just been obvious to teachers on campuses for a long time. It's actually no surprise that if a student engages in cognitive offloading to a chatbot, the work gets done, so you get great homework scores, but learning suffers. I feel like there is a little bit of delusion in some circles that chat is helpful for learning. Yes, there are some ways that people could use chatbots that are helpful, but the fact is the vast majority of ways that the vast majority of people are using chatbots is very harmful for learning. Mind you, I'm very pro-AI. They're wonderful at getting stuff done. I use them all the time, but let's not fool ourselves that they're a great tool for learning, either at the formal education levels—higher ed, K-12—or even for adult learners. Use it to get stuff done, but don't fool ourselves into thinking this is really good at helping us learn. Having said that, we know in tech that what works today is not the same as what we'll manage to get to work in the future. I am very optimistic that building on top of AI technology, we will be able to build learning experiences that are based on sound pedagogy, that teach, that are engaging, and that also allow us to go from much more of a one-to-many to a one-to-one experience. Even today, on a lot of online education, everyone kind of watches the same video, and it works. There's really nothing wrong with that. But with AI technology, there is now a much more clear path to me to turn learning from this one-to-many model, one instructor, many learners, to a much more customized one-to-one model. We still have work to get there, but when we do start to roll out things like that, that gives us a path which is different to the current chatbot path to actually help learning. This ties back to something I mentioned just now: there's going to be a lot of work for humans. It's interesting—by the way, one funny thing about AI culture, and Susan does this too, for some reason, AI people, we don't call people people, we call people "humans." I'm still not quite sure why we do that. There's going to be a lot of work for humans, but the skill mix needed is very different than what it was a few years ago. I am worried that AI is coming so fast that most universities struggle to adapt curricula at the pace that the technology is demanding. Because, frankly, I don't want to train people for the jobs of 2026. I want to train people for the jobs of 2028 and 2029 and beyond. But sadly, many universities that aren't as privileged as Stanford—to be in Silicon Valley, highly connected—are still training people for the jobs of 2022, and that just doesn't work. How to help have AI solutions to bring the new skills to the students and support universities and individuals to gain those skills that let them use AI and do this exciting work—I think that still remains a huge challenge that we're working on. One question I like to ask people when they say that all the jobs are gonna go away in 12 months and the world is gonna be transformed is, "Think about your organization. What would it take for your organization to completely transform in 12 months?" If the audience includes academics, we were basically just told, "Hey, AI is here. You figure it out." Of course, it's not just because leaders are bad; it's, "Well, why not wait? Let somebody else figure it out, and then we'll adopt the best practices." But there's a big public good aspect of figuring it out, and it takes time and experimentation to figure things out. If we can't figure out how to use AI in university education, of all places, when we have AI experts teaching the classes, it seems a little bit crazy that we think every company in the world is somehow gonna figure it out simultaneously in 12 months. Interestingly, in terms of the experimentation, it hasn't been written up yet, but there was an experiment done here at Stanford at the end of last year where they randomized people to meet with the TAs and present for half an hour instead of handing in a homework assignment to be graded. Those students did dramatically better. When I heard those results, I was like, "Okay, great. Liberal arts colleges, great. Even Stanford could adapt to that." But what about UC Berkeley? What about larger state schools? What about Purdue, which has been great at educating masses of engineering students, but they've got a lot of students? Let alone the developing world. You talked about AI being able to bring this many-to-one; can you talk at all about how we can bring that at scale to the developing world? Coursera and increasingly LearnVector have been doing work with universities around the world that do not have enough AI faculty to teach the latest cutting-edge techniques. With online courses or these digital experiences that we can spread around the world relatively efficiently, I think it's been encouraging to see how we can help universities teach cutting-edge skills, even if they do not yet have the faculty to teach the latest things themselves. In terms of human skills, there's one mistake that I feel like popular media has made a few times. When IBM beat the world chess champion, like several decades ago—was it 1997?—people thought, "Oh, AI could play chess. Wow, amazing, it could do everything," and that turned out to be false. When IBM Watson Jeopardy came about, people thought, "Wow, AI is so amazing." And it was, but it was a long path to still make it more generally useful. Same thing with Google's AlphaGo winning Go; it was amazing PR. At this moment in time, even though modern AI is more general than IBM Watson and playing Go and playing chess, I think there's actually one aspect of AI that's a bigger gulf than people realize: it turns out that AI has become very good at verifiable tasks. Verifiable tasks are tasks where you can figure out whether there's a definite right or wrong answer. Programming tends to be more of a verifiable task because you write the code, and it either works correctly or it does not. Proving math theorems is a verifiable task because you can prove a theorem and you can verify if your proof is correct or wrong. Answering factual questions is a verifiable task because you either got the facts right or got them wrong. But it turns out that in real life, we do a lot of work that requires judgment but that is not verifiable. For example, if I am writing a piece of software, what software should I write? There's no clear right or wrong answer, but there are some massively better and some massively worse answers. If a marketer is trying to decide what marketing slogan should they use, or should they go to market in this country versus that country, there's no right answer that's mathematically correct, but there are much better and much worse answers. For technical reasons, AI progress has been much faster for verifiable tasks, and it's much harder to make progress on unverifiable tasks. The technical reason is there's a set of techniques called reinforcement learning; if you can give the AI a way to verify its own output, it can run at it for a long time and figure out how to do it. So AI progress is much faster on verifiable tasks than unverifiable tasks. I think we may be making a mistake of looking at all the wonderful verifiable tasks AI can do—like coding, answering factual questions, proving math theorems—which is wonderful and very valuable. But I think people may be surprised that the generalization from verifiable tasks to unverifiable tasks is a gap bigger than is widely appreciated. There's this other weird distortion: when we benchmark AI systems, we know how to benchmark things on verifiable tasks, but it's much more painful and expensive to measure performance on unverifiable tasks, so we tend not to do that. We see amazing progress on tons of very valuable verifiable tasks, but because so much of human work requires judgment and degrees of right or wrong rather than binary absolute right or absolute wrong, I think that gap of getting AI there may be bigger than we estimate. When I ask AI to brainstorm for me—say, brainstorm a marketing campaign or how to write this or that—if I ask it for half a dozen ideas, sometimes it gives me one good, interesting, acceptable idea and three or four completely atrocious ideas. You kind of wonder, how is this supposedly really smart AI suggesting these three dumb ideas? I think a large part of the answer is that as people, we have a very wide range of context. You just know a ton of stuff that the AI does not know. A lot of our knowledge comes from the last few decades of living and working. We can take into account the fact that, "That time I had a conversation with this customer, when I brought up this feature, they had a funny expression on their face, so I know maybe they don't want this." Humans are very good at taking this bewildering, wide range of context and selecting the right pieces to make high-quality decisions. Maybe humans aren't great at making all of these decisions, but the context advantage that humans have compared to a lot of AI systems is a large gap. That is why I'm excited about education and upskilling; if you look at the task-based analysis of jobs, for a lot of jobs, maybe 30 or 40% could be automated by AI. That's fantastic. Let's use AI to help people. But there's still that 60% that AI does not have a plausible path to doing. When that 40% becomes much cheaper, the 60% that still needs a person to do becomes even more valuable. We're a little bit behind as a society in putting in place the training and educational mechanisms to help people gain those skills. I want to give one tip. It turns out that, at least today, a lot of the leading AI systems have a lot of "tells." When people like Susan and me have spent enough time reading AI-generated text, we can often look at something and go, "Yep, that was AI-generated." What a lot of people don't realize if they haven't read as much AI stuff is they will turn in stuff thinking, "This is great," not knowing that people who are really experienced can look at it and spot the tells. Maybe the technology will change and these tells will go away, but for those of you using AI to generate text, just consider that at least at this moment, the technology has a lot of tells. I also realized that for some students who don't have a lot of English language experience, they've spent more time talking to Claude than they have talking in English to humans. Those tells feel just like normal speech, and they've spent more time reading Claude output than they have reading research papers. It's really crazy what we're doing to people's brains. If we thought TikTok was something, how we're rewiring brains is just mind-blowing. I want to change the subject now. We have some questions from the online audience, and the one that is most important is, how should policymakers in developing countries synthesize everything you said into what they should do? What actions can policy leaders take so that their countries are best able to take advantage of this new technology while mitigating risks? Some specific policy recommendations: we've touched on the importance of supporting open-weight models. I think that remains important for nations to support open-weight models so you can control your own fate. Investing in training and upskilling so your citizens can gain the new skills will also be a powerful force to make as many people as possible in the nation AI-native. I know it feels like maybe other nations like the US and China are already ahead; they are, but the gap is not that wide because AI hasn't been around that long. It's actually a better time than it used to be to try to upskill and maybe even try to leapfrog. Encouraging industries to adopt AI—take the businesses that are already material to the nation and figure out how they can adopt AI. That too will be valuable. There are nations that are good at many tasks that Silicon Valley is not good at; we have very limited forms of agriculture here in California, very limited manufacturing, limited textile. There are a lot of sectors where some nations would do it much better than Silicon Valley does. If you can figure out, with the help of global partners, where you can use AI to accelerate that industry, that gives you an opportunity to do unique work that is hard for Silicon Valley to do but is even better done in that developing economy. Finally, one of the important set of use cases could be governmental use cases as well. For example, Singapore has started to use AI to help citizens in many different automation types of use cases. This is a good time to invest; AI is going to be key infrastructure for the economy going forward. This is not hype. Once upon a time, America invested in railroads, and that turned out to be really valuable and juiced economic growth around the whole country for a long time. AI is becoming a foundation that a large part of the economy will be built on top of. If you reflect on your own experience as an individual learning—think about the books you read, the online courses, the in-person courses, the times you chatted with a friend and learned something—can you think of even one time that you spent a bunch of time learning something and looked up and said, "Boy, that was a waste of time. I should not have bothered to learn that"? It just doesn't seem to happen, does it? The practical experience is that almost all the time we had spent learning turned out to be a pretty good idea. At a societal level, gaining skills has relatively little short-term return but a profound long-term return. The practical reality is almost all individuals and almost all businesses and almost all nations underinvest in training relative to what will deliver a very valuable return. To a first approximation, what you learn matters, how you learn matters, but at all levels, from early childhood education through adult training, the threshold is very high for us to invest more than we wish we had. If we can get everyone to invest more in themselves, that will deliver much better outcomes for countries. Well, Andrew, we are so incredibly fortunate to have gotten your time in this moment.
